## VOLUME 08 BATCH 0004 — THE FACT BASE AFTER CHAPTER 390. **THIS BLOCK SUPERSEDES THE BLOCK BELOW AND EVERY BLOCK UNDER IT. NOTHING BELOW THIS LINE HAS BEEN EDITED. Chapters 1–390 are canon. THE RECEIPT, THE MAP IN BOTH CALENDARS, THE INSTRUMENTS, THE MONEY AND THE DAY-PHRASE AUDIT ARE THE TOP BLOCK OF `state/current.md`.**

### 1. WHAT THE BAND DID TO THE DRAFT, WITH EVERY CITATION

- **THE DRAFT IS FILED.** Four clauses and a half on the back of a chandler's bill for candles, twenty-eight days old at the morning it went up. Clause one: everybody sleeping in the four low wards is inside the order from the moment the order stands and not from the moment a roll has their name in it. Clause two: the paper and the plate of a ward are one record, and where they differ the paper is the account and the plate is the working, until a meeting of the ward weighs the block again. Clause three: a name may be put into the record of a ward by any person of that ward who can say it, and a name said by a person of the ward goes in without a fee. Clause four: somebody must go to the doors. Clause five, the half: the somebody in the fourth is the keeper of the roll of that ward. A line struck through before the fifth, and one line under the fifth that is not a question and is not a sixth clause and is not a remedy.
- **HOW HE FOUND OUT THAT FILING MEANS HIS NAME, AND HE DID NOT RESTAGE A REFUSAL.** `381:83`: a thing that is to be kept is copied in a fair hand, and the fair hand writes at the top of the copy the name of the person who is sending it, and there is no way to send one of those up that hill with nothing at the top of it, because a copy with nothing at the top of it is not a record, it is a piece of paper in a room. The clerk of thirty says in the same speech that he has filled that line from the paper in front of him for nine years and every one of them had a name on the paper it came off.
- **`385:89` IS THE CLERK OF THIRTY SAYING IN ADVANCE, IN A ROOM, WHAT HE WILL DO WHEN THE PAPER IS PUT IN FRONT OF HIM**, so that it is not him afterwards, and adding that nobody in that room is going to help him with it.
- **`386:29` IS THE NAME, TWICE.** The second time because a clerk of forty asked him to say it again, and the clerk of forty's reason is that a fair hand is one person's writing and there are more people in that building than there are doors in this one and none of them has heard it. **NEITHER CLERK SPELLED IT. NEITHER ASKED HIM TO SPELL IT. SIX PEOPLE IN THE ROOM HEARD A STRANGER SAY HIS OWN NAME TWICE IN THE MORNING AND NOT ONE OF THEM WROTE IT DOWN.**
- **`386:51` IS THE CLERK OF THIRTY'S PRICE, IN HIS OWN MOUTH**: *I have put one name on a thing in nine years that was not off a document in front of me, and it is yours, and I did it in an ordinary room at the second hour because there is a form and a line and the line was blank and you were standing in front of it. If I had been unkind I would have done it on a bad morning and you would have gone down the hill.*
- **`386:67` IS WHAT THE THING IS NOW**: a tray, a bench on the second floor, a woman of forty who copies in the order things come and does not do it the morning they arrive, a book that stays on the bench, a column for the things that have been sent to that chamber and it does, and a column for the things that have not, and this is the first thing in that column in nine years with a name at the top of it from a person who was standing in a room when he did it. **AND A BENCH OF FOUR UPSTAIRS CAN BE ASKED ABOUT THAT BOOK AND CANNOT BE ASKED TO DO ONE SINGLE THING WITH IT, AND THE NINE LINES ALREADY ENTERED AND KEPT HAVE NOT BEEN READ OUT IN A ROOM, AND THE QUESTION ON THE SLATE HAS NOT BEEN ANSWERED AND HAS NOT BEEN ASKED AGAIN.**
- **THE FLAW, SAID OUT LOUD AT `385:49`, IN EIGHT WORDS AND NOT AGAIN**: *Naming a burden does not stop the harm. It puts a person under it, so that the record shows that a person did it.* He said the fourth and fifth clauses are not a mistake and that he is filing on purpose.

### 2. EVERY DOCUMENT THAT EXISTS IN THIS CITY, UPDATED, AND THE THING THAT IS NEW IS BOLD

- **In the roll-room at the back of a chandler's shop in Tallowgate:** the paper roll, a stack of nine slates, a book of other rooms' habits, and **A SHEET OF PAPER ON THE TABLE, WRITTEN OUT IN A HAND THAT IS NOT THE FAIR HAND'S AND NOT HIS, WITH A WORD OUT IN THE THIRD CLAUSE, A WORD OUT IN THE FOURTH, AND A MARK AT THE END OF THE FIFTH MADE BY A MAN WHO CANNOT WRITE. IT IS WRONG IN TWO WORDS. IT IS IN FOUR HOUSES IN THAT ROW AND IN A MAN OF FIFTY-NINE'S YARD AND IT CANNOT BE CORRECTED, BECAUSE THERE IS NO FORM THAT SAYS A THING SAID AT A DOOR HAS TO MATCH A THING KEPT UPSTAIRS.**
- **On the black building at the middle of the nine bridges, on the second floor:** **A NEW PAGE IN A BOOK WITH A NAME AT THE TOP OF IT IN TWO HANDS, ONE A CLERK'S AND ONE A FAIR HAND, AND A WOMAN OF FORTY-FOUR AT THE FOOT OF THE FOURTH AND FIFTH CLAUSES, AND NOTHING AT THE TOP OF IT BUT A NAME.** The chamber still records what it is sent; the column for the nine things that have not been sent still has nine in it; the sheet with two names on it still has no form that takes either off; the nine lines entered and kept are still entered and unread; the map is still a line in a sentence nobody has explained; **THE ONE LINE UNDER THE FIFTH CLAUSE OF THE DRAFT IS STILL THERE AND IS STILL NOT A QUESTION AND IS STILL NOT RAISED.**
- **On the wall of the public room at the foot of the hill:** the order, six parts, the date that does not come, the word *standing* in the margin, three sentences in the sixth part, and nine inches of blank under it for a notice that is not the order. **THE BLANK SPACE IS STILL BLANK.** It was on that wall for the whole of this band and the man who filled the sixth part read the whole of the order out loud in it at the fifth hour of the last day of the week with four people in the room, and nobody wrote anything under it.
- **In the plate-yard at the low end of Tallowgate:** the plate with two hundred and eleven lines in it, four hundredweight of blackglass offcuts, a wheel, a bench, a block in a gatepost at the end of a passage with a mark nobody in the ward can read, and a man of fifty-nine who has been told in a fair hand that the paper is the account and the plate is the working.
- **In a hall at the end of a low ward:** a book at the door, a board with the number of beds free on it, a hall board with a line for every person who has been in it, a column of nineteen dead in chalk with nothing over the top of it and nothing at the foot of it, and a second line in chalk on a board at the top of this city that a man of thirty-one wrote and did not rub out.
- **On a step outside a hall at the top of this city:** four figures in a hand cut with a piece of lead — two, four, eleven, thirteen — and a cross beside two of them, and the sum given out loud.
- **In his coat:** nothing. The draft is out of it. It has been out since the fifth day of the seventy-sixth week.

### 3. WHAT IS NOT IN THIS CITY AND WHAT IS NOT HAPPENING IN IT

There is still no form that calls a meeting of a ward, and there has not been one in Tallowgate since the autumn before the water came. There is still no form that lets anybody have a page of the roll, or puts a name in one, or takes a name out of one, or moves a name from one address to another. There is still no discharge paper and no office that issues one. There is still no form that says a man may file anything, and there is now a fact that matters more than that one: **THERE IS A NAME AT THE TOP OF A PAGE AND NO FORM THAT TAKES IT OFF, AND IT IS THE FIRST NAME THAT HAS EVER GONE UP A HILL IN THIS CITY OFF A PERSON WHO WAS STANDING IN A ROOM.** There is still no money in this ward. **AND THERE IS NOW A FIGURE NOBODY HAS GIVEN YET: A DRAFT IN A CITY FILES AS A DRAFT AND THE ONLY THING THAT FILES IN THIS CITY IS AN ORDER, AND THE DRAFT IS GOING UP THE SAME HILL ON THE SAME TRAY, WHICH IS EITHER THE CITY LEARNING SOMETHING OR THE CITY NOT HAVING A WORD FOR IT, AND NOBODY IN THE BUILDING HAS A WORD FOR IT AND THE CLERK OF THIRTY SAID SO OUT LOUD BEFORE IT HAPPENED.**

### 4. THE ELEVEN ITEMS OF THE HAND-OFF, IN ONE LIST

1. **THE DRAFT IS FILED AND HIS NAME IS ON IT, IN TWO HANDS, IN A BOOK, IN A TRAY, AND IN A COLUMN FOR THINGS THAT HAVE BEEN SENT.**
2. **HE SAID IT TWICE AND NOBODY HAS EVER HEARD HIM SAY IT BEFORE.**
3. **HE HAS SAID A PRICE OUT LOUD SIX TIMES IN THIS CITY AND THE SIXTH ONE WORKED, AND WHAT MADE IT WORK WAS A CLERK IN A ROOM, AND HE KNEW THAT IN ADVANCE AND USED IT ANYWAY. `388:65`: *Then it worked.***
4. **FENNA RUSK IS IN TWO RECORDS INSTEAD OF ONE. She found the second one from her own doorstep at the first hour, in the wrong hand, wrong in two words, and six people in a shop and a street got there in a day. `388:51` is her question and `388:59` is his answer and neither of them was kind.**
5. **THE WOMAN OF TWENTY-EIGHT IS THE ONLY PERSON IN THIS CITY WHO CAN BE ASKED WHETHER THE DOORS ARE BEING GONE TO, AND SHE CANNOT REFUSE. `389:51`.**
6. **BEVIN TARR IS STILL CUTTING, AND AT `384:57` HE TURNED A PLATE ROUND SO THAT A STRANGER COULD READ ONE NAME IN GLASS, WHICH HE HAS NOT DONE FOR ANYBODY IN THIRTY-ONE YEARS.**
7. **TARIN KEEL HAS FOUR FIGURES, TWO CROSSES AND A TOTAL OF THIRTY, AND IT IS A NUMBER ABOUT HALLS, AND NEITHER MAN WILL MENTION THE NOTICE.**
8. **THE MAN WITH THE BAD EAR WANTS A HEADING OVER NINETEEN DEAD, NOBODY HAS EVER ASKED HIM FOR ONE, AND AT `390` HE ASKED A ROOM AND NOBODY ANSWERED HIM.**
9. **SEVEN THINGS HAVE BEEN IN ONE ROOM AT ONCE AND WERE NOT JOINED, AND THE BENCH OF FOUR IS STILL UPSTAIRS, AND A WARD IS NOT A PERSON AND CANNOT BE ASKED ANYTHING.**
10. **THE CHILD OF EIGHT IS IN A BED AT THE TOP OF THIS CITY, HAS BEEN THERE A FORTNIGHT AND TWO DAYS, IS BETTER, CANNOT BE DISCHARGED, HAS TWO LINES IN TWO HALLS AND ONE DOOR, AND HIS NAME IS NOT SAID OUT LOUD IN ANY ROOM IN ANY OF THE TEN CHAPTERS.**
11. **MAGISTRATE RELL IS NOT ON STAGE, HAS NOT BEEN NAMED, AND HER PEN IS IN HER HAND. `HADDEN ROOTE` IS NOT IN THIS BAND AND THE WORD *healer* IS AT ZERO IN IT. `SERA QUILL` DOES NOT APPEAR. NOBODY IS A VILLAIN AND NOBODY HID ANYTHING.**

- **THE ORDER STANDS FROM THE FIFTH DAY OF THE SEVENTY-FIFTH WEEK, WHICH IS CHAPTER 374 AND IS SIX DAYS BEFORE CHAPTER 380 AND SEVEN BEFORE 381, AND THE PRICE WAS SAID ON CHAPTER 373, WHICH IS SEVEN AND EIGHT.** A WEEK-RELATIVE PHRASE IS A CALCULATION AND NOT A RULE, AND IT IS AGAINST THE CHAPTER'S OWN DAY EVERY TIME: *LAST WEEK* IS CORRECT AT 381 TO 383 AND THE WEEK BEFORE LAST FROM 384, AND A CHAPTER ON THE FOURTH DAY OF A WEEK CANNOT USE *THIS WEEK* FOR ANYTHING THAT HAPPENED BEFORE IT. **THE FIRST RECEIPT OF THIS REPAIR PRINTED *SIX DAYS AND ELEVEN* AND A RULE THAT EXPIRED AT 384; BOTH WERE WRONG AND ARE CORRECTED.**

- **FENNA RUSK IS THE KEEPER OF THE ROLL AND WAS NAMED ZERO TIMES IN CHAPTERS 371–380 AGAINST AN EXPLICIT PROMPT INSTRUCTION; A PREVIOUS REVIEW HAD ALREADY CAUGHT IT IN THE BAND BEFORE THIS ONE.** Her name is now in the narration four times, at `372:3`, `372:117`, `375:63` and `380:9`, and only in the narration; in her own mouth she is the woman with the book or the woman at the table, **and never both in one sentence, which `372:3` did in a first draft and does not now.** Her entity in `state/character-state.md` is unchanged and was always right.
- **`372:11` READS *I HAVE BEEN UNDER IT FOR TWENTY-ONE DAYS*, WHICH IS HIS OWN DURATION AND NOT THE ORDER'S, AND IS KEPT. `372:55` CALLED THE WOMAN AT THE END OF THE ROW *THE MAN OF TWENTY-EIGHT* AND IS NOW A WOMAN, WHICH SHE IS IN ELEVEN OTHER CHAPTERS.**
- **THE CHAPTERS ARE UNCHANGED IN PLOT.** Same ten chapters, same midpoint at 374, same climax deferred to the Mercy Trial, same draft in the coat, same child of eight who is better and is not discharged and is never named, same ending.

**WHAT CHANGED IN THE CHAPTERS SINCE THE BLOCK BELOW WAS WRITTEN, AND IT IS EIGHT FACTS AND NOTHING ELSE.**

**1. THE SIXTH PART OF THE ORDER NOW HAS THREE SENTENCES IN IT AND THEY STAND.** A bed in a hall is a place a person cannot be discharged from; a ward is now a place a person cannot be got out of either; the two are the same piece of paper. The rule is good and is not undone. The bench, the state and the fever are untouched.

**2. THE HEALER OF ABOUT SIXTY IS SPENT AND IS NAMED.** Hadden Roote, at the end of the ninth row in Tallowgate for fourteen years, no post in this city with his job, at zero over Chapters 351 to 370 and present in 377. **HIS NINE WORDS ARE SPENT FOR THE REST OF THE BOOK: *The worse they are, the longer they keep it.*** He gave his name out loud and said the price of doing it first. Nobody thanked him.

**3. THE RESERVED SENTENCE IS SPENT.** *A rule that does not end is a permanent rule*, at `372:71`, in the woman with the book's mouth, once, in answer to a question.

**4. A CHILD OF EIGHT IS IN A BED AT THE TOP OF THIS CITY.** Worse than the other nine, not saved, not dead, and **his name is not said out loud in any room in any of Chapters 371 to 380.** He has two lines in two halls and one door.

**5. HE IS THIRTY MORNINGS IN A CITY, WHICH IS TWENTY-NINE DAYS.** The ordinal is `chapter − 350`; the elapsed count is `chapter − 351`, and the book prints elapsed. A count a character owns is neither: Tarin Keel's *fifteen days* without a notice and the healer's *three times in three weeks* are his.

**6. FENNA RUSK IS NAMED FREELY IN NARRATION AND BESIDE THE TWO PLACES WHERE THE COST IS FELT.** She is the woman with the book or the woman at the table in her own mouth. She is still never the subject of a chapter title.

**7. THE THREE HUNDRED ARE INSIDE A BOOK AND OUTSIDE A PROTECTION, AND BOTH ARE BECAUSE OF HIM.** He said them out loud on his seventh morning and they went into a fair hand upstairs, and they are on a paper and not on a plate, and since the sixth part stands nobody in this city has to cut a line for anybody.

**8. `BIBLE/CHARACTERS.MD` IS NOT THIS PHASE'S FILE.** The chapter files win on Tarin Keel (about fifty-two), and they now win on a new name, Hadden Roote.

### 1. WHAT THE PERSON IS, AFTER CHAPTER 380

**A FEVER IN FOUR LOW WARDS, NOT IN FOUR RICH ONES, AND NOBODY HAS NAMED ITS CAUSE.** It came off the water on Shelf day 158. At Chapter 371 it is twelve weeks and four days old; at 380, thirteen weeks and six days. About a hundred and forty people are dead in four columns that have not been added up and cannot be, because there are four clerks in four wards and no office for the four of them to hand anything to.

**AN ORDER WITH SIX PARTS, AND THE SIXTH STANDS.** A line with a date in it and, beside the date, the word *standing*, so the date does not arrive, and now three sentences underneath it, which are the volume's central object and are printed in the top block of `state/current.md` §1. **THE MISSING HALF WAS AN EXIT AND NOT A REFUSAL, AND WHAT IS IN IT IS STILL AN EXIT AND NOT A REFUSAL: an exit that does not end is an exit somebody cannot find, and that is worse.**

**A WARD'S PUBLIC RECORD IS FOUR THINGS KEPT IN AGREEMENT BY PEOPLE AND IS NOT ONE OBJECT.** A plate, a paper, a keeper, and a block in a gatepost that holds a weight. **AND AFTER THE FIFTH DAY OF THE SEVENTY-FIFTH WEEK NONE OF THE FOUR IS NEEDED ANY MORE, BECAUSE A HALL DOES NOT NEED THE ROLL OF ITS WARD, AND THAT IS THE PRICE OF THE THREE SENTENCES, GIVEN IN MONEY AT TWELVE SHILLINGS FOR THREE HUNDRED NAMES AND AT NOTHING AT ALL.**

### 2. EVERY DOCUMENT THAT EXISTS IN THIS CITY, AND A STATE FILE IS WORST AT THIS LIST, SO HERE IT IS WHOLE

**In the roll-room at the back of a chandler's shop in Tallowgate: the PAPER ROLL, kept eleven years by a woman of forty-four, Fenna Rusk, four shillings a year. A STACK OF NINE SLATES. A BOOK OF OTHER ROOMS' HABITS ON THE SHELVES. AND, SINCE THE FIFTH DAY OF THE SEVENTY-FIFTH WEEK, A THING SHE SAID OUT LOUD ONCE, WHICH IS A STATEMENT ABOUT THE SIXTH PART OF AN ORDER AND WHICH SHE CANNOT UNSAY IN HER OWN ROOM.**

**In the plate-yard at the low end of Tallowgate: the PLATE, a hand's width, a line for each name, with a line across the face where the polish stopped two years ago. FOUR HUNDREDWEIGHT OF BLACKGLASS OFFCUTS. A WHEEL, A BENCH. A BLOCK IN A GATEPOST AT THE END OF A PASSAGE WITH A MARK ON ITS FACE THAT NOBODY IN THIS WARD CAN READ. AND, SINCE THE FIFTH DAY, A MAN OF FIFTY-NINE CUTTING LINES THAT NOBODY IN THIS CITY NEEDS, WHO ASKED OUT LOUD WHETHER HE WAS TO STOP AND WAS ANSWERED BY NOBODY.**

**In a hall at the end of a low ward: A BOOK AT THE DOOR with a line for every person who comes in and no line for anybody who goes, because there is no discharge paper and no office that issues one. A BOARD ON THE WALL with the number of beds free, and it does not go up or come down. A HALL BOARD with a line for every person who has been in it, and the line stays, AND AFTER THE FIFTH DAY OF THE SEVENTY-FIFTH WEEK ONE OF THOSE LINES IS A SECOND LINE IN A BOARD AT THE TOP OF THIS CITY. A COLUMN OF NINETEEN DEAD, IN CHALK, WITH NOTHING OVER THE TOP OF IT AND NOTHING AT THE FOOT OF IT, KEPT BY A MAN OF THIRTY-ONE WITH A BAD EAR WHO IS PAID NOTHING.**

**In the black building at the middle of the nine bridges: on the second floor, A CHAMBER with a table, four chairs and a press of blackglass plates, which RECORDS WHAT IT IS SENT. A COLUMN ON THE WALL for the nine things that have not been sent to it. ON A BENCH, A SHEET with two names on it, one of them a stranger's, and there is no form that takes a name off a sheet. A SLATE WITH A QUESTION ON IT. NINE LINES ENTERED AND KEPT, with a word in the middle of a sentence that a clerk read out in the ordinary way and nobody asked about. A FAIR HAND, in a book, containing a public cost, two differences off one bench date, and a number that four hundred and twenty days of one woman is the price of a draft. AND, SINCE THE FIFTH DAY OF THE SEVENTY-FIFTH WEEK, **A THIRD THING: THE THREE SENTENCES, AND THE PRICE, IN THE SAME FAIR HAND, WITH NOTHING AT THE TOP OF IT.** NOTHING PUTS A NAME ON ANY OF IT.**

**On the wall of the public room at the foot of the hill: THE ORDER, six parts, and under it a blank space nine inches deep for a notice that is not the order, and the blank space is still blank.**

**In a book on a second floor, IN A FAIR HAND, WRITTEN BY A CLERK OF FORTY: a thing said out loud in the public room on his seventh morning in this city, being the cost of a plate, the cost of a Bench date, the difference between them, and the difference between a bell keeper and a roll keeper; and since the fourth day of the seventy-fifth week, the three sentences and the price, with no name at the top of either.**

**On the back of a chandler's bill for candles, folded into four, in his own hand, in his coat: FOUR CLAUSES AND A HALF, a line struck through before the fifth, and a fifth clause that names the keeper of the roll, and under the fifth clause ONE LINE THAT IS NOT A QUESTION. IT IS NOT FILED. IT IS NOT DASHED. IT IS NOT SIGNED. IT IS TWENTY-TWO DAYS OLD AT CHAPTER 380.**

**In a room over a lock-and-ledger shop near the courts: FOUR DOCUMENTS OPEN ON A TABLE UNDER A LAMP, and a thing under a weight on a second table, which is bread.**

### 3. THE PEOPLE, ONE LINE EACH, AND NONE OF THEM IS A PRODUCTION

**FENNA RUSK, forty-four, eleven years keeping the paper roll, four shillings a year, has said the reserved sentence once and is spent. A woman of twenty-eight who goes to nine hundred doors on her own account, has a paper of chalk bought for her at a standpipe by a woman of sixty with a shawl, and has said that a favour is a line in a list and that the list is not written down, and has closed the subject of doors for good. A man of fifty-two named Tarin Keel, nineteen years of counting what comes through a door, who has not written a notice in fifteen days and will not mention it again, and who has undertaken to count six halls in a fortnight. A man of fifty-nine named Bevin Tarr, four thousand lines in thirty-one years, who has been told what a line is for and did not stop. A man of thirty-one with a bad ear keeping a column of nineteen dead, who has written a second line on a board and has not rubbed out the first. A woman of fifty-eight on a stool in a hall at the end of a low ward, who has been on it since the eighth morning of the fever and who told the people in that hall what was done in their name and who will tell a mother at a door the same short thing every morning for the rest of her life. A woman of thirty-four at a standpipe, head of her own house nineteen years. A woman of sixty with a shawl, fifty-eight years in that street, who holds four names she has never said out loud. **HADDEN ROOTE, about sixty, fourteen years at the end of the ninth row in Tallowgate, no post in this city with his job, his nine words spent, not thanked.** A woman of sixty-one, Bess Carrow, in a hall at Weir Lane since the eighth morning of the fever, better, and who has asked for a sheet of paper in the first week and has not asked again and who said the sentence at `378:63`. A man of forty-four, Ivo Serrel, at the top of nine steps, not on the roll, the rule does not touch him and he said so himself. A woman of sixty-eight in the row above the plate yard's, moved out on the sixth day, whose name is still at a shut door. A boy of eleven, a boy of fourteen in a public room, a boy of sixteen with a barrow. A boy of eight in a bed at the top of this city whose name is not said out loud in any room.**

### 4. WHAT IS NOT IN THIS CITY AND WHAT IS NOT HAPPENING IN IT

**There is no form that calls a meeting of a ward, and there has not been one in Tallowgate since the autumn before the water came. There is no form that lets anybody have a page of the roll. There is no form that says a man may ask a woman to take a name out of a roll, or to put one in. There is no discharge paper and no office that issues one. There is no form that takes a name off a sheet, or a person out of a record, or a name from one address to another, in four hundred miles. There is no money in this ward and has not been for two years. THERE IS NO NUMBER ANYWHERE OF HOW MANY PEOPLE THE SIXTH PART HAS MOVED, AND TARIN KEEL IS GOING TO PRODUCE ONE IN A FORTNIGHT. THE ROADS ARE STILL SHUT. THE PORCH IS STILL GONE. THE CORRIDOR STILL STANDS. THE BELL FOUR HUNDRED MILES OFF IS STILL UNHUNG. NOBODY IS RELIEVED.**

### 5. THE RESIDUALS INHERITED FROM BATCH 0001 AND BATCH 0002, RECORDED RATHER THAN SILENTLY CORRECTED

**`chapter-0353.md:9` and `chapter-0355.md:39` say *eleven weeks* where the derivation puts the fever at ten weeks and no days and ten weeks and two days on those two mornings, and `chapter-0354.md:3` says the one thing he has needed *for six days* where Chapter 354 is his fourth morning. Both are in Batch 0001 and are not this phase's to reach into. A later pass may correct them and must say in the hand-off that it has. DO NOT CITE THEM AS PRECEDENT. **`state/continuity.md`'s own §1 also gives the order as going up on Shelf day 187 while `outline/volume-08.md` gives Shelf day 172; the chapters of this band print the age of the order nowhere, which is the correct handling of a contradiction in the record rather than a choice between two wrong figures.**

**WHAT CHANGED SINCE THE BLOCK BELOW WAS WRITTEN, AND IT IS SEVEN FACTS AND NOTHING ELSE.**

**1. THE ROUND'S WORKING IS A MULTIPLICATION AND NOT A DIVISION. 30 doors takes a fortnight, 900 ÷ 30 = 30 lots, 30 × 14 = 420 days, a year is 364, the difference is 56, and the round is a year and fifty-six days. `355:39` and `363:21` both now say it that way. THE FIGURE NEVER MOVED; THE SENTENCE THAT REACHED IT DID. 900 ÷ 2 IS 450 AND THE ROUND IS NOT 450.**

**2. THREE DIFFERENCES OFF ONE BASE OF 282, ALL SPENT, ALL NAMED. 138 = 282 − 144, the 300-name plate at twelve shillings, said out loud under the board on the third day and earlier in a yard. 186 = 282 − 96, this ward's own 200 names at eight shillings, said out loud in the same breath. 234 = 282 − 48, a woman's year, in her own mouth at `367:89`. NONE OF THE THREE CONTRADICTS ANOTHER. A DIFFERENCE MUST NAME ITS TWO TERMS.**

**3. THE CHALK LEDGER, IN ONE PLACE. Nine days bought with a penny, three marks to the day, twenty-seven marks on a slate. Nine marks on the first morning, which is three days of chalk on day one. Six days left at the end of day 1, four at the end of day 3, two and six doors at the start of day 6, nothing at the end of day 7. The chalk ran out at the end of HIS HUNDRED AND SEVENTEENTH MORNING. SHE HAS BEEN GOING TEN DAYS: SEVEN CHALKED AND THREE NOT.**

**4. HE IS TWENTY MORNINGS IN A CITY AND THE ELAPSED COUNT IS `chapter − 351`, because `355:17`, `355:85` and `360:21` all print elapsed and all print it right; the ordinal is `chapter − 350` and is for *his hundred and twentieth morning*, not for *he has been here twenty days*. A COUNT A CHARACTER OWNS IS NEITHER OF THOSE: `362`'s *nine days* is the woman of thirty-four's own count of the mornings she has seen him at her door and it is not to be corrected.**

**5. FENNA RUSK IS NAMED IN THE BAND, TWICE, AT `363:7` AND `369:33`. She is Fenna Rusk in narration and the woman at the table or the woman with the book in her own mouth, and the two are not used in the same sentence. She is not the subject of a chapter title.**

**6. THE TWO CLERKS OF THE PUBLIC ROOM ARE THIRTY AND FORTY AND ARE DIFFERENT MEN. The clerk of thirty keeps the tray and wrote the fair hand on the seventh morning and read the document on the fourteenth. The clerk of forty sits at the end of the long table and took down the third requirement of a draft on the third day. Do not merge them.**

**7. HE HAS SAID THE PRICE OUT LOUD FOUR TIMES, not three: Fenna counted three by his sixteenth morning and the fourth was in the chamber on the fourteenth, and on that occasion THE PRICE ITSELF WAS WRITTEN DOWN AS WELL AS HIS SENTENCE.**


## VOLUME 08 IS OPEN — CHAPTERS 361–370 REPAIRED, *The Doors*. **THIS BLOCK SUPERSEDES THE `## VOLUME 08 IS OPEN` BLOCK IMMEDIATELY BELOW IT AND NOTHING BELOW IT HAS BEEN EDITED. The fact base for everything after Chapter 370. Where this block and the one below differ about a figure, this block is right and the one below is the delivery record.**

**WHAT CHANGED IN THE CHAPTERS SINCE THE BLOCK BELOW WAS WRITTEN, AND IT IS FIVE FACTS AND NOTHING ELSE. 1. THE FEVER CAME OFF THE WATER ON SHELF DAY 158, WHICH IS CHAPTER 283, and the derivation is in the book three times over: `355:39` (eleven weeks back on Shelf day 230), the delivered continuity block (eleven weeks as of Chapter 360, Shelf day 235), and this block (twelve weeks and three days as of Chapter 370, Shelf day 245). 230 − 77 = 153, 235 − 77 = 158, 245 − 87 = 158. 2. HE IS TWENTY MORNINGS IN A CITY AND THE ELAPSED COUNT IS `chapter − 351`, because `355:17`, `355:85` and `360:21` all print elapsed and all print it right; the ordinal is `chapter − 350` and is for *his hundred and twentieth morning*, not for *he has been here twenty days*. 3. THE CHALK IS GONE: nine days bought with a penny, three marks a day, nine marks on `361`, three a day on `362` through `367`, and nothing at all on `368`, `369` and `370`, and twenty-seven marks on a slate. 4. HE HAS SAID THE PRICE OUT LOUD FOUR TIMES, not three: Fenna counts three as of `367:105` and the fourth is `369:61`, and `370:29` agrees. 5. THE BLOCK IN THE GATEPOST CANNOT BE READ BY ANYBODY AND ITS FIGURE IS NOT IN THE BOOK: `361:123` said the block said *a thousand one hundred and something*, which contradicted `356`'s own account of a mark that is not a name, not a date and not a figure anybody in the ward could read. It now says a mark cut into a face that nobody in this ward can read.**

### 1. WHAT THE PERSON IS, AFTER CHAPTER 370

**A FEVER IN FOUR LOW WARDS, NOT IN THE FOUR RICH ONES, AND NOBODY HAS NAMED ITS CAUSE.** It came off the water on Shelf day 158 and it is eighty-seven days old at the end of this band, which is twelve weeks and three days, and it is at Chapter 363 eighty days old and at Chapter 366 eighty-three days old, and the two *eleven weeks* in those two mouths are both correct. **About a hundred and forty people are dead and nobody has added the four columns up, because there are four clerks in four wards and no office for the four of them to hand anything to, and about a hundred and forty is a figure nobody has ever counted and is not to be summed.**

**AN ORDER WITH FIVE PARTS AND A HALF.** Ansa Rell wrote it in the third week of the fever, which is Shelf day 179, and it went up eight days later, which is Shelf day 187, which is Chapter 312, and the sixth line is a line with a date in it and the word in the margin is standing. **THE MISSING HALF IS AN EXIT AND NOT A REFUSAL. An exit is a way out of an arrangement a person is inside of; a refusal is a way of not being in it; and only one of them can be written by a magistrate in a week. NOBODY IN THIS CITY HAS ONE OF EITHER.**

**A WARD'S PUBLIC RECORD IS FOUR THINGS KEPT IN AGREEMENT BY PEOPLE AND NOT ONE OBJECT.** A plate, a paper, a keeper, and a block in a gatepost that holds a weight. The plate carries about thirteen hundred names, the paper about fifteen hundred, the block agrees with neither and has not been read since the day it was cut, and the whole cost of the two hundred that are missing is eight shillings, and the cost of a hearing about it is a pound three and sixpence, and a Bench date has been bought and is in a book. **AND THE FIFTH THING, WHICH IS THE BAND'S FINDING AND IS NOT IN THE LIST ABOVE BECAUSE IT IS NOT A THING: a roll is a list of who is in a house, because a name gets into it only when somebody says it at a door, and the person who keeps it is not allowed to make a name up, and the ward's record is therefore only as strong as its weakest door, and the weakest door cannot be found, because finding it means asking every householder whether they have ever left somebody off, and nobody has ever been asked that in four hundred years, and the asking cannot be done without the ward knowing it is being asked, and a ward that is being checked produces a list of who checked it.**

### 2. §3 — EVERY DOCUMENT THAT EXISTS IN THIS CITY, AND A STATE FILE IS WORST AT THIS LIST, SO HERE IT IS WHOLE

**In the roll-room at the back of a chandler's shop in Tallowgate: the PAPER ROLL of Tallowgate, kept eleven years by a woman of forty-four, Fenna Rusk, paid four shillings a year, and it is a list of who is in a house. A STACK OF NINE SLATES. A BOOK OF OTHER ROOMS' HABITS ON THE SHELVES.**

**In the plate-yard at the low end of Tallowgate: the PLATE, blackglass, a hand's width, a line cut for each name, with a line across the face where the polish stopped, which is two years ago. FOUR HUNDREDWEIGHT OF BLACKGLASS OFFCUTS. A WHEEL, A GRINDSTONE, A BENCH. AND A BLOCK IN A GATEPOST AT THE END OF A PASSAGE, THE SIZE OF A DOOR, WITH A MARK ON ITS FACE THAT IS NOT A NAME AND NOT A DATE AND NOT A FIGURE ANYBODY IN THE WARD CAN READ.**

**In a hall at the end of a low ward: a BOOK AT THE DOOR with a line for every person who comes in and no line for anybody who goes, because there is no discharge paper and no office that issues one. A BOARD ON THE WALL with the number of beds free, chalked and rubbed out every morning, and the number only goes up. A HALL BOARD with a line for every person who has been in it, and the line stays, because there is no discharge. A COLUMN OF NINETEEN DEAD, IN CHALK, WITH NOTHING OVER THE TOP OF IT AND NOTHING AT THE FOOT OF IT, KEPT BY A MAN OF THIRTY-ONE WITH A BAD EAR WHO IS PAID NOTHING.**

**In the black building at the middle of the nine bridges: on the second floor, A CHAMBER with a table, four chairs and a press of blackglass plates, which RECORDS WHAT IT IS SENT. A COLUMN ON THE WALL for the nine things that have not been sent to it, which is the only column anybody in the building reads. ON A BENCH, A SHEET with two names on it, one of them a stranger's, put there by a clerk of thirty off a written request for a witness and off a document that names who may speak to a board that came from somewhere else, and there is no form that takes a name off a sheet. A SLATE WITH A QUESTION ON IT: what is the Bench to do with a document it did not make that says a thing is under this city and not found. AND, AS OF THE FOURTEENTH, NINE LINES ENTERED AND KEPT, with a word in the middle of a sentence that a clerk read out in the ordinary way and nobody asked about.**

**On the wall of the public room at the foot of the hill: THE ORDER, five parts and a half, and under it a blank space nine inches deep for a notice that is not the order, and the blank space is still blank.**

**In a book on a second floor, IN A FAIR HAND, WRITTEN BY A CLERK OF FORTY: a thing said out loud in the public room on his seventh morning in this city, being the cost of a plate, the cost of a Bench date, the difference between them, and the difference between a bell keeper and a roll keeper. Nineteen people have read it in the four days since. IT IS A COPY, WHICH IS TO SAY A PLACE SOMEBODY CAN BE ASKED.**

**On the back of a chandler's bill for candles, folded into four, in his own hand, in his coat: FOUR CLAUSES AND A HALF, and a fifth clause that names the keeper of the roll, and under the fifth clause ONE LINE THAT IS NOT A QUESTION. IT IS NOT FILED. IT IS NOT DASHED. IT IS NOT SIGNED.**

**In a room over a lock-and-ledger shop near the courts: FOUR DOCUMENTS OPEN ON A TABLE UNDER A LAMP, and a thing under a stone on a second table, which is bread. It is a records room and nobody has ever shown her a bill.**

### 3. THE PEOPLE, ONE LINE EACH, AND NONE OF THEM IS A PRODUCTION

**A woman of forty-four who keeps the paper roll, eleven years, four shillings a year, does not know what the roll is for and found out on his fifth morning, refused him her name on that morning for the exact reason he took it on his tenth, told him the price of the work in her own mouth on his thirteenth morning, said the third requirement of a draft out loud because he said it first, and was asked eleven questions on the fourteenth over a whole day of which two were about her. SHE IS NOT RELIEVED, NOT FORGIVEN AND NOT THANKED. A woman of twenty-eight who goes to nine hundred doors on her own account with a slate and a string and a piece of chalk, has been in this city nineteen years, will not be named, has twenty-seven marks on a slate and no chalk, and has said that the next nine days of chalk is a penny and that nobody's job it is to buy it. A man of fifty-two named Tarin Keel, nineteen years, nine counties, a piece of lead and a rail, who is counting what comes through a door and is being useful and hating it, and who said he would write a notice in the morning six days before the fourteenth and has not written it. A man of fifty-nine named Bevin Tarr, cutting plates for this ward for thirty-one years, four thousand lines, has asked twice what a line is for and was told the ward's business both times, and said in a room that he has never been told what a line is for, and was answered with courtesies and said *not at all*. A man of thirty-one with a bad ear who keeps nineteen dead in chalk and whose name is on a slate upstairs that he asked to be put on and cannot get off. A woman of thirty-four at the standpipe, head of her own house nineteen years, with a lodger and a sister-in-law on a pallet and neither of them on the roll because she did not ask. A woman of sixty with a shawl who has lived in that street fifty-eight years and holds four names she has never said out loud. A boy of eleven who is the head of a house with three names in it and six people in it, and who carries water past a standpipe for a penny at the end of the week. A man of forty with a bad cough at the top of four stairs with his name ready since the water came and nowhere to put it. A woman of sixty-one in a hall who wants to go home and asked for a sheet of paper instead of a voice. A woman of fifty-eight on a stool with twenty-one people on the books of a hall and eleven beds. A man of forty-four at the top of nine steps in Weaver's Row who cannot come down and whose light is better than it was. A boy of seventeen set out from the March with a book that can order things and cannot date them, and he is not in this city and has not arrived. A clerk of thirty, a clerk of forty, a boy of fourteen, a man with a barrow, a boy of sixteen in a yard, a runner in a magistrate's colour, a woman of sixty with a bundle and a child asleep against her shoulder, and a boy of about sixteen who lives with his aunt and works in a yard. AND A HEALER OF ABOUT SIXTY, WHO IS ONE OF THE SIX PEOPLE OF THIS VOLUME, AND `healer` IS AT ZERO OVER TWENTY CHAPTERS, AND HE IS STILL OWED HIS NINE WORDS.**

### 4. WHAT IS NOT IN THIS CITY AND WHAT IS NOT HAPPENING IN IT

**There is no form that calls a meeting of a ward, and there has not been one in Tallowgate since the autumn before the water came, and a ward is a body only when a ward meets, and a ward cannot be fined for not doing a thing it cannot be asked to do. There is no form that lets anybody have a page of the roll. There is no form that says a man may ask a woman to take a name out of a roll, and no post in this city has the duty of deciding that a thing in a book is not true. There is no discharge paper and no office that issues one. There is no form that takes a name off a sheet, or a person out of a record, in four hundred miles. There is no list, and no rota, and no notice, and no bench date after the fourteenth. THE ROADS ARE STILL SHUT. THE PORCH IS STILL GONE. THE CORRIDOR STILL STANDS. THE BELL IN THE MARCH IS STILL UNHUNG. NOBODY IS RELIEVED.**

### 5. THE RESIDUALS THIS PHASE COULD NOT FIX, RECORDED RATHER THAN SILENTLY CORRECTED

**`chapter-0353.md:9` and `chapter-0355.md:39` say *eleven weeks* where §0 puts the fever at ten weeks and no days and ten weeks and two days; `chapter-0354.md:3` says the one thing he has needed *for six days* where Chapter 354 is his fourth morning. Both are in Batch 0001, which is delivered and reviewed, and this phase did not reach into it. A later pass may correct them and must say in the hand-off that it has.**

## VOLUME 08 IS OPEN — CHAPTERS 361–370, *The Doors*. **THE FACT BASE FOR EVERYTHING AFTER IT, WRITTEN FOR A WRITER WHO HAS READ NONE OF THE VOLUME. The block below it, `## VOLUME 08 IS OPEN — CHAPTERS 351–360…`, and everything under that, is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map in both calendars, the twenty-two findings, the instruments and the fifteen prohibitions is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 08 BATCH 0002 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block. `outline/series.md` outranks `outline/ending.md`, which outranks `outline/volume-08.md`, and all three outrank this file.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS, IN SEVEN PARTS. §1 the ground as it stands on Shelf day 245. §2 the people. §3 every document that exists in this city, one each, and where it is — and this is the part a Volume 08 writer most needs and a state file is worst at, because a document is a place somebody can be asked and a state file is a document that is wrong four hundred times a year. §4 the clocks. §5 the money. §6 everything still standing and unresolved, in one list. §7 the things a later pass gets wrong if it does not measure them.**

### 1. THE GROUND, AS IT STANDS ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE SEVENTY-FIFTH WEEK

**ORISON. A city of about ninety thousand people on the Ninefold Shelf, on the low side of a river, built around nine bridges and the First Verdict, which is a basalt court without a throne. THE BENCH BUILDING STANDS AT THE MIDDLE OF THE NINE BRIDGES AND IT HAS ABOUT THIRTY CLERKS IN IT AND A BENCH OF FOUR OVER THEM, A GROUND-FLOOR PUBLIC ROOM WHERE ANY PERSON MAY ASK TO HEAR A RULE THAT GOVERNS THEM READ ALOUD — WHICH IS A ROOM THAT READS RULES AND NOT BOOKS, AND THERE IS NOTHING IN THE BUILDING THAT READS A BOOK OUT, BECAUSE A BOOK IS A LIST OF PEOPLE AND A LIST OF PEOPLE IS A THING A MAN CAN CARRY — A SECOND-FLOOR CHAMBER THAT RECORDS WHAT IT IS SENT, AND A COLUMN ON THE WALL OF THAT CHAMBER HEADED WITH A WORD THAT MEANS NOT SENT, WHICH IS THE ONLY COLUMN IN THE BUILDING THAT ANYBODY READS.**

**THE FOUR LOW WARDS, which are the subject of the order and the only part of this city the volume has been in. *Tallowgate* is the one the volume lives in: about nine hundred doors in a mile and a half, a standpipe at the end of it with a queue every morning, a chandler's shop with a room at the back of it, a plate-cutter's yard at the low end, a hall at the low end with a board at the end of the beds, Weaver's Row with four houses and a passage and a stair of nine steps, and a burying ground nobody has visited. *Weir Lane* is a poor ward already in the fiction and its load-fee book is kept by a person called A. of the eastern yard. *Nethergill* is seven miles from Weir Lane's district and eleven from a bridge. *The Ninth Weir* is a mile of yards and workshops at the low end where the people who are not on a roll sleep. The four rich wards are named as Cresset Street and are never entered.**

**AND THE FACT THAT THE SECOND BAND WAS BUILT ON AND THAT THE FIRST BAND DID NOT HAVE: A WARD IN THIS CITY IS ABOUT NINE HUNDRED DOORS, AND A DOOR IS ADDRESSED BY A PERSON WHO ANSWERS IT, AND THERE IS NO SECOND WITNESS TO ANY DOOR AND THERE NEVER HAS BEEN, AND THE WARD'S PUBLIC RECORD IS THEREFORE ONLY AS STRONG AS ITS WEAKEST DOOR, AND THE WEAKEST DOOR CANNOT BE FOUND, BECAUSE FINDING IT MEANS ASKING EVERY HOUSEHOLDER WHETHER THEY HAVE EVER LEFT SOMEBODY OFF, AND NOBODY HAS EVER ASKED THAT IN FOUR HUNDRED YEARS, AND THE ASKING CANNOT BE DONE WITHOUT THE WARD KNOWING THAT IT IS BEING ASKED, AND A WARD THAT IS BEING CHECKED PRODUCES A LIST OF WHO CHECKED IT. THAT IS THE WHOLE OF THE VOLUME'S ARGUMENT SO FAR AND IT IS STATED ONCE, IN `366`, IN THE MOUTH OF A PERSON OF A PLACE.**

**THE FEVER.** It began twelve weeks and three days ago, when the cold came off the water. It is in the four low wards and it is not in the four rich ones. It is a fever. **NO CHAPTER OF VOLUME 08 DIAGNOSES IT, EXPLAINS IT, NAMES ITS CAUSE, OR IS TOLD BY THE INTERFACE WHAT IT IS, AND THAT IS NOT A WITHHELD MYSTERY.** About a hundred and forty people are dead; **a healer of about sixty is one of the six people of this volume and HE IS STILL NOT IN THE BAND, THE WORD *HEALER* IS AT ZERO OVER CHAPTERS 351 TO 370, AND HE IS STILL OWED HIS NINE WORDS — a later band may spend them wherever the ward puts him, and Batch 0003 is the band in which a person in this ward is worse than the other nine, so that is where a person of a place might put him.** **THE COLUMNS ARE FOUR, ONE PER WARD, KEPT BY FOUR PEOPLE WHO ARE NOT CLERKS, AND NOBODY HAS ADDED THEM UP AND NOBODY CAN, BECAUSE THERE ARE FOUR CLERKS IN FOUR WARDS AND NO OFFICE FOR THE FOUR OF THEM TO HAND ANYTHING TO.**

**THE MONEY OF THE CITY.** A copy is threepence at a scrivener and a penny a page. A carter is eighteen pence a day. A Bench date is a pound three and sixpence. A plate is four shillings the hundred names. A ward clerk is paid by the year or not at all. **A PENNY BUYS NINE DAYS OF CHALK AND THREE DOORS A DAY, WHICH IS TWENTY-SEVEN DOORS IN NINE HUNDRED, AND THAT IS THE PRICE OF THE FIRST REAL LABOUR IN THIS VOLUME AND IT IS UNPAID. AND THE CITY HAS ABUNDANT PAPER AND NO SHEET, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE BRIAR MARCH, AND A BAND THAT WRITES A CHAPTER IN ORISON OUT OF HABIT WILL HAVE A SCENE IN A LANE.**

### 2. THE PEOPLE, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST, AND NONE OF THEM WAS SENT FOR

1. **THE KEEPER OF THE ROLL OF TALLOWGATE. A woman. Forty-four. She has kept it eleven years and has gone round the ward nine times.** Thirty doors a fortnight, about nine hundred doors, a year and fifty-six days a round, about four hundred years of other rooms' habits in the shelves. Paid four shillings a year. **SHE REFUSED HIM HER NAME ON THE FIFTH MORNING, WHICH IS `355`, HE TOOK IT ON THE TENTH, WHICH IS `360`, AND ON THE NINETEENTH, WHICH IS `369`, SHE ANSWERED ELEVEN QUESTIONS ON A SHEET AND MORE THAN WERE ON THE SHEET, OVER A WHOLE DAY, IN A ROOM OF ABOUT FORTY PEOPLE, TWO OF THE QUESTIONS BEING ABOUT HER.** She is called **Fenna Rusk** and the name is in five chapters and it is in a book she has read, and there is no form that will take it out. **IN THIS BAND SHE GAVE THE BAND'S GROUND FIGURE IN HER OWN MOUTH AT `363`, PRICED THE ROUND AT `367` AND REFUSED TO DIVIDE A PERSON, AND SAID AT `366` THAT THE WORST DOOR IN HER WARD IS NOT KNOWABLE AND CANNOT BE MADE KNOWABLE BY ANYBODY IN THIS CITY INCLUDING HER.**
2. **BEVIN TARR, fifty-nine, plate-cutter, thirty-one years at the low end of Tallowgate, four thousand lines cut in his life, a cart and a house and a daughter's school paid for out of four shillings the hundred.** He is right that a plate is the account and a piece of paper is a working paper. He has never once asked whether a name he cut was true and has asked twice what a name is and been told it was the ward's business. **IN THIS BAND HE WAS TOLD AT `368` THAT HE IS THE SECOND NAME ON A SHEET FOR THE FOURTEENTH, HE SAID HE DID NOT KNOW IT, HE ASKED FOR THE HOUR AND NOT THE MORNING, AND HE SAID OUT LOUD THAT HE DOES NOT KNOW WHAT A PLATE IS FOR, AND HE SAID IT IN A ROOM AT `369` AND A BENCH OF FOUR THANKED HIM FOR IT, AND IT IS IN A FAIR HAND.**
3. **IVO SERREL, forty-four, who cannot leave his own room, nine steps up at the top of Weaver's Row, in a house whose head is his sister's husband.** Not on the roll, because the roll says who answers the door. Has asked twice and been told both times to come back with the paper. **HIS LIGHT IS BETTER THAN IT WAS. HE IS NOT IN THIS BAND AND HE IS NOT ASLEEP AT THE END OF IT.**
4. **BESS CARROW, sixty-one, in the hall at Weir Lane since the eighth morning of the fever, better for six days, and cannot be discharged, and does not want to be anywhere, and wants to go home. SHE ASKED FOR A SHEET OF PAPER INSTEAD OF A VOICE AT `359` AND NOBODY HAS BROUGHT HER ONE AND SHE IS NOT IN THIS BAND.**
5. **THE WOMAN ON THE STOOL AT THE HALL DOOR, fifty-eight, in that hall since the second week, twenty-one people on the books of a hall with eleven beds, a chalked number of beds free that she rubs out and chalks again every morning, and about nine minutes' work of a discharge paper that no office in this city issues. SHE IS RIGHT AND THE WARD CLERK IS RIGHT AND THE ARGUMENT IS THE WHOLE OF HER WORK, AND AT `370` IT IS SHE WHO SAYS OUT LOUD THAT A CHILD IN THAT HALL IS WORSE THAN THE OTHER NINE.**
6. **THE MAN WITH THE BAD EAR, thirty-one, who keeps Tallowgate's column of nineteen dead, and who asked for his own name to be put on a slate upstairs and cannot get it taken off. HE IS A PERSON OF TALLOWGATE AND HE IS THE MAN WHO BROUGHT TARIN KEEL DOWN OFF THE HILL.** **IN THIS BAND HE FOUND THAT ONE OF HIS NINTEEN NAMES IS ALSO ON THE ROLL OF THIS WARD, CORRECTLY ENTERED AT A DOOR IN THE AUTUMN, AND THAT NOBODY CAN TAKE IT OFF, AND HE SAID THE WHOLE OF WHY IN `368` AND IT IS THE STRONGEST TEST OF PROHIBITION 12 IN THE VOLUME: NOBODY HID ANYTHING.**
7. **THE WOMAN OF TWENTY-EIGHT WHO GOES TO DOORS ON HER OWN ACCOUNT**, nineteen years in the city, a penny's worth of chalk, a bundle of string, a slate, a promise from him that he will say nothing to anybody, and a method she states in her own mouth in answer to a question: if the ward knew, the first thing that would happen is a rota, and a rota is a list, and a list is the thing that comes before a bill. **SHE IS NOT NAMED IN THE FICTION AND HE DOES NOT NAME HER, AND THAT IS A PROMISE HE MADE IN A YARD. BY THE END OF THIS BAND SHE HAS DONE TWENTY-SEVEN DOORS IN ELEVEN DAYS AT THREE A DAY, HER CHALK RAN OUT ON HIS EIGHTEENTH MORNING, SHE HAS A SLATE AND A STRING AND THREE DAYS OF NOT GOING TO A DOOR, AND THE NEXT NINE DAYS OF CHALK IS A PENNY AND NOBODY'S JOB.**
8. **THE WOMAN OF THIRTY-FOUR AT THE STANDPIPE, nineteen years in that street, the head of her own house, four names on the roll's line for her door and six people in the house.** She is the person who answered the question in `360` that made the woman of twenty-eight say Fenna Rusk's name. **SHE IS THE MOUTH OF THE FINDING THAT A NAME GETS INTO A ROLL ONLY BECAUSE SOMEBODY SAID IT AT A DOOR, AND THE MOUTH OF THE FINDING THAT A LODGER AND A WOMAN ON A PALLET ARE NOT PEOPLE OF THAT HOUSE ON PURPOSE, AND SHE ASKED HIM ONE QUESTION IN `361` AND IT WAS THE FIRST QUESTION ANYBODY IN THAT WARD HAD ASKED HIM.**
9. **A CLERK OF THIRTY IN THE PUBLIC ROOM AND THE SAME CLERK IN THE CHAMBER UPSTAIRS**, who read the order aloud, who wrote Ilyan's name on a sheet, and who looked for a form to take it off and found none. **IN THIS BAND HE TOLD A STRANGER OUT LOUD THAT HE WILL NOT ENTER HIS NAME ON A FORM OFF A DOCUMENT HE HAS NOT BEEN GIVEN, AND SAID IT WAS THE ONE RULE HE HAS GOT AND THAT HE BREAKS IT TWICE A YEAR AND NOT IN THIS, AND IT IS THE ONLY PROTECTION ANYBODY IN THIS CITY HAS OFFERED HIM IN TEN DAYS.**
10. **A CLERK OF ABOUT FORTY AT THE END OF THE PUBLIC ROOM TABLE**, whose job it is to write down what is said in that room. He wrote the fair hand in eleven minutes in `357`. **HE WROTE THE THIRD REQUIREMENT OF A DRAFT INTO A FAIR HAND AT `365`, AND HE TOLD THE STRANGER THAT HE WAS GOING TO WRITE IT DOWN WHETHER HE SAID IT OR NOT, FOUR HOURS LATER, IN A WORSE SENTENCE, WHICH IS THE TRUEST THING A CLERK IN THIS BOOK HAS SAID.**
11. **A CARTER OF FIFTY OR SO WITH A BAD COUGH**, four days on a road with a board the size of a door under a strap, eighteen pence a day. He is four hundred miles off and is not in this band.
12. **A MAN OF SIXTY OR SO AT THE GATE** with a book on his knees. He let a stranger in with the line empty and said he would be at that gate on the way out. **THE WAY OUT WAS NOT THE FOURTEENTH AND NOTHING IN THIS BAND SAYS WHETHER HE WAS.**
13. **A MAN OF FORTY WITH A BAD COUGH AT THE TOP OF FOUR STAIRS OFF THE ROW**, eleven years in that house and the only one he has had in this city, whose wife is on the books of the low hall since the second week of the fever and who has had his name ready since the water came and nowhere to put it. **HE IS A PERSON OF A PLACE AND THE CHAPTER SAYS SO.**
14. **A BOY OF ELEVEN WHO CARRIES WATER PAST A STANDPIPE FOR A PENNY AT THE END OF THE WEEK**, who was doing that on the stranger's first morning in this city in `351`. **IN THIS BAND HE IS THE HEAD OF A HOUSE WITH THREE NAMES IN IT AND SIX PEOPLE IN IT, BECAUSE HIS MOTHER IS IN A HALL AND HIS GRANDMOTHER HAS BAD KNEES, AND HE SAID THE NAMES OF THREE PEOPLE WHO ARE NOT HIS FAMILY TO A STRANGER IN A STREET AT THE SECOND HOUR BECAUSE HE HAD NEVER BEEN ASKED ANYTHING, AND THEY WENT INTO THE WORLD AND CANNOT BE SPENT.**
15. **A WOMAN OF SIXTY WITH A SHAWL** who was in a yard at the low end of Tallowgate on his tenth morning in this city and has lived in that street fifty-eight years and watched him come past nine times in thirteen days. **SHE IS THE MOUTH OF THE BAND'S DECLARED INSTRUMENT AT `366` AND OF THE FORM OF IT AT `362`, AND OF ONE OF THE TWO PEOPLE WHO SHOWED UP IN A PASSAGE.**
16. **A WOMAN OF SIXTY WITH A BUNDLE AND A CHILD ASLEEP AGAINST HER SHOULDER**, who was in the room at the gate on his first morning and in the public room on his seventh. **IN THIS BAND SHE SAID AT `365` THAT HER SISTER'S BOY IS ON THE ROLL BECAUSE SHE PUT HIM ON IT, THAT NOBODY ASKED HER AND SHE DID NOT NEED TO BE, AND THAT SHE WOULD HAVE SAID YES TO BEING A PERSON IN A RECORD AND THE KEEPER WOULD NOT, AND THAT THEY ARE THE SAME DOOR AND YOU CANNOT TELL THEM APART FROM OUTSIDE.**
17. **SERA QUILL, twenty-eight, licensed recordwright, in a room over a lock-and-ledger shop near the courts.** She appears once in this band, in `370`, for about nine lines, gives one fact, refuses an opinion, will not say whose the other three documents belong to, is not thanked, and there is no romance beat and no bread. **Her family's claim of six years is unfiled, unresolved, and is not mentioned in this band at all.**
18. **TARIN KEEL, about fifty-two**, at a rail outside the black building counting what comes through the door, who came down off the hill into Tallowgate at `364` and had the incompatible methods argument with a stranger in a doorway and the rest of it in a street. **HIS PAST IS USED ONCE IN THIS BAND AND IS NOT RESOLVED: a gate with a levy on loads behind it, loads counted by a man who was not paid, a dead man in the fourth line who stays dead, no road behind the gate and never was, eleven feet that stay where they are, and a second notice of five shillings and fourpence that nobody has ever paid. NO FIGURE IS PRINTED FOR THE LEVY. HE SAID HE WOULD WRITE A NOTICE IN THE MORNING AND THE NOTICE IS NOT WRITTEN SIX DAYS ON AND NEITHER MAN HAS MENTIONED IT.**
19. **MAGISTRATE ANSA RELL, whose age IS NOT STATED IN THIS BAND AND MAY NOT BE INFERED IN A HAND-OFF, referred to as Magistrate Rell.** She wrote the order in the third week of the fever. She left the sixth line empty on purpose. She is not a villain, she has hidden nothing, she is not softened, and her defeat in this volume produces a difficult ally and not a redeemed servant. **SHE IS NOT ON STAGE IN THIS BAND AT ALL. SHE IS NAMED TWICE — ONCE AS THE PERSON WHO WROTE THE ORDER, IN A DOORWAY, AND ONCE AS THE PERSON A NOTICE WOULD CARRY THE NAME OF — AND THAT IS ALL, AND IT IS CORRECT, AND A HAND-OFF MUST NOT PUT HER ON STAGE IN BATCH 0003 WITHOUT A REASON.**
20. **AND EVERYBODY ELSE WHO IS ALIVE AND IS NOT IN THIS CITY.** The eleven Briar March threads at `state/continuity.md` §6 stand where they stood on Shelf day 225, and he is four hundred miles off and has been for twenty days.

### 3. EVERY DOCUMENT THAT EXISTS IN THIS CITY, ONE EACH, AND WHERE IT IS

1. **THE SHELTER ORDER. Six lines. Up since the third week of the fever, on a notice board in the public room with a blank nine inches under it.** Subject, the residents of the four low wards. Condition, a declared fever. **Burden, a person, and the whole of the line is: a person is inside the protection when the roll of the ward has the name of that person in it.** Witness, the keeper of the roll, the master of the hall, and the person of the house in which the person sleeps. Remedy, a board in a hall, water at a standpipe, and a wage at the end of the week. **Its sixth line is a line with a date in it and the date is not there, and in the margin beside the empty line there is one word, and the word is STANDING. THE SIXTH LINE IS AN EXIT AND NOT A REFUSAL AND NO CHAPTER OF EITHER BAND TURNS IT INTO ONE.**
2. **THE ROLL OF TALLOWGATE, in paper. About fifteen hundred names. Kept by a woman of forty-four in a room at the back of a chandler's shop. THE ONLY COPY ANYBODY HAS OF WHAT SHE DID THIS WEEK. IT HAS AT LEAST ONE NAME IN IT OF A MAN WHO DIED IN THE FOURTH WEEK OF THE FEVER, CORRECTLY ENTERED AT A DOOR IN THE AUTUMN, AND NOBODY CAN TAKE IT OUT AND NOBODY CAN FIND OUT HOW MANY THERE ARE.**
3. **THE PLATES. Thirteen hundred names, cut a hundred to a plate, on blackglass, in a yard at the low end of Tallowgate.** Two hundred of them are on the paper and not on a plate because nobody could pay four shillings the hundred. About eleven hundred of the lines are above a polish line and two hundred are below it and the line is two years old.
4. **THE BLOCK IN THE GATEPOST.** A block the size of a door with a mark on the face that is not a name and not a date and not a figure anybody in the ward can read. **IT IS A WEIGHT. It was cut two years ago, in the same fortnight the plates were last cut right through, and it holds the weight of the ward, and a name cut onto a plate makes the plate heavier, and the block and the plate have been saying two different things ever since, and nobody reads the block.** Getting it right needs a meeting of the ward, a notice, a day, and nine people in a room, **and there has not been a meeting of Tallowgate since the autumn before the water came, and there is no form in this city that calls one, and nine people in a passage agreed about it at `361` and could not change it.**
5. **THE FAIR HAND.** A clerk of forty's record of what a stranger said out loud in the public room on the seventh morning of his time in this city, which is `357`: three hundred names short, twelve shillings of plate, a pound three and sixpence for a date, and the difference between a bell keeper's year and a roll keeper's year. **IT IS IN A BOOK ON A SECOND FLOOR AND EVERYBODY IN THAT BUILDING READS THAT BOOK, AND SINCE `365` IT ALSO CONTAINS THE THIRD REQUIREMENT OF A DRAFT, WHICH IS FOUR HUNDRED AND TWENTY DAYS OF ONE WOMAN'S LIFE, SAID OUT LOUD BY THE STRANGER IN THE PUBLIC ROOM AND WRITTEN DOWN BY THE CLERK.**
6. **THE DRAFT. Four clauses and a half, on the back of a chandler's bill for candles, in a blunt pencil, in his coat.** One. A person is inside the protection from the moment the order stands. Two. The paper and the plate are one record. Three. A person may be entered by any person of the ward who can say the name, without fee. Four. Somebody must go to the doors. **Five, the half, with a line struck through before the fifth clause and not after it: the somebody in the fourth is the keeper of the roll of that ward. He did not file it and he did not tear it up, and at the end of this band it is still in his coat and he has said out loud in a public room that he cannot get the third of the three things a draft needs.**
7. **THE SHEET WITH HIS NAME ON IT.** One sheet, in a chamber upstairs, with two names on it for a witness to a question on the fourteenth, one of which is his. **THE BENCH'S OWN CLERK OF THIRTY WROTE IT THERE OFF A DOCUMENT IN FRONT OF HIM UNDER A WRITTEN REQUEST FOR A WITNESS, HE HAS NOT SEEN THE SHEET, THERE IS NO FORM THAT WILL TAKE A MAN OFF A PIECE OF PAPER ONCE IT IS ON THERE, AND IN TEN DAYS HE HAS PUT NO NAME ON ANYTHING AND A CLERK HAS REFUSED, ON THE RECORD, TO PUT ONE ON FOR HIM.**
8. **THE FOUR COLUMNS OF THE DEAD.** Nineteen in Tallowgate with nothing over the column and nothing at the foot of it and no heading at all. About a hundred and forty across the four wards. **NOT ADDED UP AND NOT ADDABLE.**
9. **THE BOOKS AT SIX HALL DOORS.** A line for every person who comes in. There is no line for a person who goes and no document that would make one.
10. **THE DOCKET.** A slate, not a paper, in a chamber upstairs, with a question on it: what the Bench is to do with a document it did not make, that says a thing is under this city and not found. **THE DATE WAS THE FOURTEENTH AND THE FOURTEENTH WAS YESTERDAY. THE BENCH OF FOUR READ THE SLATE, READ THE DOCUMENT, AND WROTE DOWN THAT IT COULD NOT ANSWER THE QUESTION, AND THAT IS ENTERED AND KEPT, AND IT IS THE FIRST TIME ANYBODY IN THIS CITY HAS BEEN TOLD NO BY ANYBODY WITH A DUTY.**
11. **THE MAP. One of nine copies, on the back of a flour mill's sack, sent from a county four hundred miles off because this city asked for one and paid the carriage.** It arrived at the noon of the first day and came back at the last of the light with nine things on it and a clerk's mark. Six of the nine things are standing, one burned, two not found, and one of the two is this city. **THE NINE LINES WERE READ ALOUD IN A CHAMBER AT THE FOURTEENTH BY A CLERK OF THIRTY, AND THE WORD THE MAP USES FOR ALL NINE OF THEM IS *ANCHOR*, AND IT WENT INTO THE RECORD IN THE MIDDLE OF A SENTENCE, AND NOBODY ASKED WHAT IT MEANT AND NOBODY EXPLAINED IT AND NOBODY REMARKED ON IT.**
12. **THE NOTICE THAT WAS NOT WRITTEN.** A man of fifty-two said in a doorway on the second day of the seventy-fourth week that he would go up the hill and say a thing to a magistrate in ninety seconds and she would write a notice for a meeting of the ward. **Six days on there is no notice and no meeting and neither man has mentioned it, and that is the whole of the disagreement between them.**
13. **AND THE THINGS OF THIS BAND THAT ARE NOT DOCUMENTS:** about twenty chalk marks on about twenty doors, in one mark, meaning *I have been here and you have not been here*; a slate with twenty-seven marks on it and no writing; three names said out loud in a street by a boy of eleven that went nowhere; a line in chalk on a board at the end of a bed that will be in chalk when the order ends; and one line on the back of a candle bill, which is not a question.

### 4. THE CLOCKS, AND THE ONE THAT DOES NOT RUN HERE

**The Shelf: fifty-two weeks of seven days. The seventy-third week is 231 to 237, the seventy-fourth is 238 to 244, the seventy-fifth is 245 to 251, the seventy-sixth is 252 to 258. CHAPTER 360 IS SHELF DAY 235 AND CHAPTER 361 IS 236 AND CHAPTER 370 IS 245 AND CHAPTER 400 IS 275. HIS SHELF DAY IS HIS CHAPTER NUMBER LESS ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIVE, AND HIS MORNING COUNT IS HIS CHAPTER NUMBER LESS TWO HUNDRED AND FIFTY, AND BOTH ARE ARITHMETIC AND NOT A CONVENTION. NO CHAPTER MAY MENTION THE TURN OF THE YEAR.**

**AND THE THIRD CLOCK, WHICH IS THE ONE A BAND GETS WRONG, AND WHICH IS ARITHMETIC AND NOT A CONVENTION EITHER: HIS DAYS IN THIS CITY IS HIS CHAPTER NUMBER LESS THREE HUNDRED AND FIFTY. CHAPTER 351 IS DAY ONE AND HE ARRIVED AT THE SEVENTH HOUR OF IT, CHAPTER 360 IS DAY TEN, AND CHAPTER 370 IS DAY TWENTY. `workspace/volume-08/batch-0002/PROMPT.md` §4.8 ASSERTS THAT THIS IS HIS MORNING ORDINAL LESS ONE, WHICH IS WRONG BY A HUNDRED AND FORTY-SEVEN AND WHICH ITS OWN LIST OF EXAMPLES CONTRADICTS WITHIN ONE SENTENCE. THE CHAPTERS AGREE ON CHAPTER 350 BEING THE DAY BEFORE HE CAME AND THAT IS THE ONLY AUTHORITY THERE IS.**

**The bells: four a day, the first, the change, the noon, and the last, and the same four here as in the March. The city also keeps the hour, and the hours are counted from the first light, and a person of a place says the hour and not the bell. THE BAND'S FOUR CHAPTERS THAT CLOSE ON THE FOUR BELLS ARE `363`, `367`, `368` AND `370`, and the other six close on a door or a sentence or a refusal.**

**THE MARCH'S WEIGHTS DO NOT RUN HERE AND MAY NOT BE USED HERE. A weight is a weight in the county the plank is in, and Orison is not in that county, and a day in this city carries no weight at all because it is on no plank. A chapter of Volume 08 that adds a March weight to a day in Orison, or prints the plank's figure, has committed the defect the Volume 07 close spent eight sections on, in a place where nothing can check it.**

### 5. THE MONEY AT THE END OF CHAPTER 370, AND IT IS SEVEN FIGURES AND TWO UNITS

| Figure | Where it stands |
|---|---|
| **A Bench date, bought = 282d = £1 3s 6d** | canon, named with its working at `357`; **re-used in this band as a given at `367` and NOT re-multiplied, because the multiplication is spent** |
| **A keeper of a roll = 4s a year = 48d** | `355`, `357`, `358`, and in this band in her own mouth at `363` and again at `367` |
| **The difference between a date and a year = 282 − 48 = 234d = 19s 6d** | **NEW IN THIS BAND, `367`, in her own mouth, with the subtraction printed in the sentence** |
| **A plate = 4s the hundred names = 48d; two hundred names = 8s** | canon; used as a unit at `361`, `364` and `370` and not re-multiplied beyond the canon two hundreds |
| **A round of the ward = 30 doors a fortnight on about 900 doors; a fortnight is 14 days; about 2 doors a day; 420 days; a year is 364; the difference is 56; a round is a year and fifty-six days. A door costs a visit** | **the ground figure of this volume, `363`, in the keeper's own mouth, with every factor in the sentence** |
| **The whole of the labour so far = a penny for nine days of chalk, at three doors a day, which is twenty-seven doors out of about nine hundred** | `363`, `368`, `370`, in the woman of twenty-eight's own mouth, off her own slate, and the next nine days is the same penny and nobody's job |
| **A carter = 18d a day; a copy = 3d, a penny a page** | CANON units, used as units, and neither is multiplied in this band |
| **The four hundred and eighty-five pounds; the levy at a bar; the two numbers on the market's slip; the £57 15s of March margin** | **at ZERO in this band, measured, and the thread is spent** |

### 6. EVERYTHING THAT IS STILL STANDING AND UNRESOLVED, IN ONE LIST

1. **The order's sixth line is empty and the word beside it is standing, and the half that is missing is an exit and not a refusal, and a rule with no way to end is being written in a city by one person, and the person is right about why, and the person is the only one who can write it.**
2. **A person is inside the protection when a roll has the name in it, and the roll is made at about nine hundred doors by the person who answers each one, and about three hundred people in four wards do not answer a door and are outside a protection a magistrate put there on purpose.**
3. **Four records that have to agree and three of them do not, and the whole cost of the disagreement in one ward is eight shillings, and no form in this city says a man may call a meeting of a ward, and nine people in a passage agreed about it and could not act.**
4. **A woman of forty-four is the only person in this city anybody can be asked about the roll, and she did not agree to it, she was not asked, she was asked eleven questions and more about it in a room of forty people on the nineteenth day, she is not going to be thanked, and there is no form that will take a name back out of a record.**
5. **His own name is on one sheet in this city, put there by the Bench's own clerk under a written request for a witness, and there is no form to take it off, and a clerk has told him out loud that he will not put it on anything else.**
6. **The number of beds free in six halls only goes up, and about half the beds in this city are empty, and a woman of fifty-eight on a stool has been chalking a number she can stand behind for five days and she knows the other one.**
7. **Nobody can be discharged from a hall, and the discharge is a document, and no office in this city issues one, and the reason is that the order's sixth line is empty.**
8. **The fourteenth came and went and produced a document received and entered, the word *anchor* in a book unremarked, a plate-cutter's sentence about four thousand lines, one sentence from a stranger, and NO RULING.**
9. **A map arrived in this city and says there is a thing under it that is not found, and nobody has opened anything, and nobody in this city knows who laid it or when or why, and the word the map uses is in a book and nobody has asked what it means.**
10. **A woman of twenty-eight has done twenty-seven of about nine hundred doors on her own account in eleven days with a penny of chalk, and the chalk ran out, and the next nine days is a penny, and there is nobody whose job it is to buy it, and she will not be asked for it, and the rest of the round is four hundred and twenty days long.**
11. **A man of forty-four cannot come down nine steps and is not on a roll, and there is no paper in this city that would put him in one.**
12. **About a hundred and forty people are dead and they stay dead and nobody has counted them properly and the fever is neither explained nor contained and it is a fever, and a healer of about sixty is one of the six people of this volume and is still not in it and is still owed his nine words.**
13. **The right of refusal is unrestored and nobody has one, the four words at the head of the plank in the March are unprinted, the column of a hundred and forty-one figures is unadded and unmentioned, the corridor stands four hundred miles off with a bell owed, the porch in the valley is gone with no copy, the bell in the March is unhung, the six places in the lane stand and the seventh does not come back, and the nine copies of the map are nine copies.**
14. **His leg is the leg and the carrying is off nothing and no count of days since it cleared is printed anywhere, and the year of his own memory went in Chapter 197 and is not paid again.**
15. **A roll is not a record of who is in a ward; it is a record of who was in it, and nobody has ever crossed anything off, so there is at least one dead man correctly in the roll of Tallowgate and the number is not knowable and asking would produce a list.**
16. **A boy of eleven is the head of a house with three names in it and six people in it, and three names of people who are not his family went into a street at the second hour and cannot be spent, and nobody is going to put them anywhere, and that is the correct decision and it is not a good one.**
17. **Tarin Keel's past is open and is not resolved: a gate with a levy behind it, a dead man in the fourth line, no road behind the gate, eleven feet, and a second notice of five shillings and fourpence unpaid. And the notice he said he would write was not written.**
18. **Tarin Keel and Ilyan have incompatible methods and both are right and neither is going to win and the band does not resolve it, and six days after a doorway neither of them has mentioned it.**
19. **A child in a hall at the end of Tallowgate is worse than the other nine this morning, on a line in chalk that will be in chalk when the order ends, his mother is at the standpipe, and nobody has said his name out loud. THE NEXT BAND IS ABOUT HIM AND HE IS NOT SAVED IN IT AND HE DOES NOT DIE IN IT.**
20. **AND THE ONE THIS BAND ADDED, WHICH IS NOT A STORY FINDING: the count of verbatim repetitions in a band is one hundred and ninety-nine distinct windows at seventy characters, against Volume 07's five bands at two hundred and thirty-five, one hundred and forty-four, one hundred and sixty-three, one hundred and twenty-two and five hundred and thirty-four, and against Batch 0001's sixteen. NOT ONE REPEATED WINDOW OF SEVENTY CHARACTERS OCCURS THREE TIMES, AND NO SENTENCE OF FORTY-FIVE CHARACTERS OR MORE IS PRINTED TWICE IN THE TEN CHAPTERS. `state/current.md` §3.**

### 7. THE THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM

1. **That his days in this city is his chapter number less three hundred and fifty, and NOT his morning ordinal less one, which is what `workspace/volume-08/batch-0002/PROMPT.md` §4.8 says and which its own examples contradict.** Chapter 351 is day one, Chapter 370 is day twenty. **THE REVIEW OF BATCH 0001 FOUND SEVEN WRONG DAY COUNTS IN TEN CHAPTERS AND THIS BAND FOUND ELEVEN, AND NOT ONE OF THE EIGHTEEN WAS A BACK-REFERENCE TO AN EARLIER VOLUME.**
2. **That there is no weight column in this volume and no plank figure, and that the map at `state/current.md` §1 has NONE in it ten times on purpose.**
3. **That the panel count is one, that it is at `360:75` to `360:78`, and that bold is at zero in all ten files of Batch 0002 and was eight in Chapter 369.**
4. **That Fenna Rusk refused her name on the fifth morning, in Chapter 355, and that the whole of Chapter 360 depends on it, and that in Chapter 369 she is examined for a whole day and answered everything.**
5. **That the one round number in Batch 0002 is Chapter 370 and that nobody notices it, and that the correct treatment is the one Chapter 340 got.**
6. **That the count of the people who know he cannot read is printed nowhere, and that `cannot read` is at zero over Chapters 351 to 370, and that it was one when this band was measured and it was a grandmother's and it was cut anyway because the declared instrument is the string.**
7. **That `stone` is at ZERO over Volume 08 so far, that `anchor` is at TWO and both of them are the same document, and that `vault|cave|ruin|temple|battlefield|relic|treasure` is at zero over twenty chapters.**
8. **That the woman of twenty-eight's chalk is nine days bought with a penny, that a day of it is three doors, that she ran out on his eighteenth morning, and that she has twenty-seven marks on a slate and a string and three days of not going — and that any figure given to her later must be in her own mouth and must be a figure she is in a position to know.**
9. **That Ansa Rell's age is not stated and she is not on stage in Batch 0002 at all, and that a hand-off may not put her on stage in Batch 0003 without a reason; that Tarin Keel is fifty-two in the chapter files and forty-six in the bible and the chapter files win; and that the barwoman's age split and the man of about seventy-four's two *since* dates fourteen days apart at `317:15` and `336:49` are both still open and neither is in this volume.**
10. **That the six state files were 5,962,664 bytes before this band's block and that the prompt for this band printed 5,962,240 for the same thing, and that a band may not inherit a figure and that this is the eleventh time this project has caught itself inheriting one.**

## VOLUME 08 IS OPEN — CHAPTERS 351–360, *The Roll*. **THE FACT BASE FOR EVERYTHING AFTER IT, WRITTEN FOR A WRITER WHO HAS READ NONE OF THE VOLUME. The block below it, `## VOLUME 07 IS CLOSED…`, and everything under that, is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map in both calendars, the twenty-one findings, the instruments and the fifteen prohibitions is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 08 BATCH 0001 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block. `outline/series.md` outranks `outline/ending.md`, which outranks `outline/volume-08.md`, and all three outrank this file.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS, IN SIX PARTS.** The ground as it stands on Shelf day 235. The people. Every document that exists in this city, one each, and where it is. The clocks. The money. And the list of everything still standing, which is the list a writer should read and not this paragraph.

### 1. THE GROUND, AS IT STANDS ON THE FIFTH DAY OF THE SEVENTY-THIRD WEEK

**ORISON. A city of about ninety thousand people on the Ninefold Shelf, on the low side of a river, built around nine bridges and the First Verdict, which is a basalt court without a throne. THE BENCH BUILDING STANDS AT THE MIDDLE OF THE NINE BRIDGES AND IT HAS ABOUT THIRTY CLERKS IN IT AND A BENCH OF FOUR OVER THEM, A GROUND FLOOR PUBLIC ROOM WHERE ANY PERSON MAY ASK TO HEAR A RULE THAT GOVERNS THEM READ ALOUD, A SECOND-FLOOR CHAMBER THAT RECORDS WHAT IT IS SENT, AND A COLUMN ON THE WALL OF THAT CHAMBER HEADED WITH A WORD THAT MEANS NOT SENT, WHICH IS THE ONLY COLUMN IN THE BUILDING THAT ANYBODY READS.** The city's own phrase for the Bench is *the Bench*; the building is spoken of by its street.

**THE FOUR LOW WARDS, which are the subject of the order and the only part of the city this band has been in. *Tallowgate* is the one the band lives in: about nine hundred doors in a mile and a half, a standpipe at the end of it with a queue every morning, a chandler's shop with a room at the back of it, a plate-cutter's yard at the low end, a burying ground nobody in this band has visited, and a valley behind nothing. *Weir Lane* is a poor ward already in the fiction and its load-fee book is kept by a person called A. of the eastern yard. *Nethergill* is seven miles from Weir Lane's district and eleven from a bridge. *The Ninth Weir* is a mile of yards and workshops at the low end where the people who are not on a roll sleep, and it is named in this band once.** The four rich wards are named as Cresset Street and are never entered. **A WARD IN THIS CITY IS A ROLL AND A ROLL IS ABOUT NINE HUNDRED DOORS, AND A DOOR IS ADDRESSED BY A PERSON WHO ANSWERS IT.**

**THE FEVER.** It began eleven weeks ago when the cold came off the water. It is in the four low wards and it is not in the four rich ones. It is a fever. **NO CHAPTER OF VOLUME 08 DIAGNOSES IT, EXPLAINS IT, NAMES ITS CAUSE, OR IS TOLD BY THE INTERFACE WHAT IT IS, AND THAT IS NOT A WITHHELD MYSTERY.** About a hundred and forty people are dead; **and a healer of about sixty is one of the six people of this volume and HE IS NOT IN THE BAND, THE WORD *HEALER* IS AT ZERO OVER CHAPTERS 351 TO 360, AND HE IS STILL OWED HIS NINE WORDS — `outline/volume-08.md` §CENTRAL PRESSURE used to put them in Chapter 355 and there is no such scene in Chapter 355 or in any other, and a later band may spend them wherever the ward puts him.** **THE COLUMNS ARE FOUR, ONE PER WARD, KEPT BY FOUR PEOPLE WHO ARE NOT CLERKS, AND NOBODY HAS ADDED THEM UP AND NOBODY CAN, BECAUSE THERE ARE FOUR CLERKS IN FOUR WARDS AND NO OFFICE FOR THE FOUR OF THEM TO HAND ANYTHING TO.**

**THE MONEY OF THE CITY.** A copy is threepence at a scrivener and a penny a page. A carter is eighteen pence a day. A Bench date is a pound three and sixpence. A plate is four shillings the hundred names. A ward clerk is paid by the year or not at all. **AND THE CITY HAS ABUNDANT PAPER AND NO SHEET, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE BRIAR MARCH, AND A BAND THAT WRITES A CHAPTER IN ORISON OUT OF HABIT WILL HAVE A SCENE IN A LANE.**

### 2. THE PEOPLE, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST, AND NONE OF THEM WAS SENT FOR

1. **THE KEEPER OF THE ROLL OF TALLOWGATE. A woman. Forty-four. She has kept it eleven years and has gone round the ward nine times.** Thirty doors a fortnight, about nine hundred doors, a year and fifty-six days a round, about four hundred years of other rooms' habits in the shelves. Paid four shillings a year. **SHE REFUSED HIM HER NAME ON THE FIFTH MORNING, WHICH IS `355`, AND HE TOOK IT ON THE TENTH, WHICH IS `360`, AND THE REASON SHE GAVE FOR REFUSING IT IS THE WHOLE OF THE VOLUME IN ONE SENTENCE: in this city a person who gives her name in a room is a person in the room, and she has watched four people do it and two of them were about a book.** She is called **Fenna Rusk** and the name is in five chapters and it is in a book she has read, and there is no form that will take it out.
2. **BEVIN TARR, fifty-nine, plate-cutter, thirty-one years at the low end of Tallowgate, four thousand lines cut in his life, a cart and a house and a daughter's school paid for out of four shillings the hundred.** He is right that a plate is the account and a piece of paper is a working paper. He has never once asked whether a name he cut was true and has asked twice what a name is and been told it was the ward's business. He has been carrying the disagreement between the block and the plate for two years and told nobody until a stranger asked him a question in a shed.
3. **IVO SERREL, forty-four, who cannot leave his own room, nine steps up at the top of Weaver's Row, in a house whose head is his sister's husband.** Not on the roll, because the roll says who answers the door. Has asked twice and been told both times to come back with the paper. His boards came off the window at about the fourth hour of his third morning, which is `353`, and the room was a different room by the fifth hour and nobody in the street knew. **HE ASKED FOR NOTHING FOR SIX YEARS BECAUSE THE ONE TIME HE ASKED FOR ANYTHING SOMEBODY CAME WITH A PAPER IN THEIR HAND.**
4. **BESS CARROW, sixty-one, in the hall at Weir Lane since the eighth morning, better for six days, and cannot be discharged, and does not want to be anywhere, and wants to go home.** She has said the finding in her own mouth in answer to a question and has asked for a sheet of paper instead of a voice.
5. **THE WOMAN ON THE STOOL AT THE HALL DOOR, fifty-eight, in that hall since the second week, twenty-one people on the books of a hall with eleven beds, a chalked number of beds free that she rubs out and chalks again every morning, and about nine minutes' work of a discharge paper that no office in this city issues.** She is fifty-eight and she argues with a ward clerk twice a week and they are both right.
