### EVERYTHING THAT IS STILL STANDING AND UNRESOLVED, IN ONE LIST, BECAUSE THIS VOLUME INHERITS SIX OPEN THINGS AND CLOSES NONE OF THEM

1. **A corridor is standing in a valley of about a hundred and forty people four hundred miles off and is not going to be torn up. A man of forty called Corvin Hale signed it out loud with the working printed, is alive in his own field, and is owed a bell nobody bought.** Not mentioned in this band. Not undone.
2. **A status is on the inside of a door in a village of about ninety people and it has no gate, no toll, no date, no bench, no seat, no chair, no room with a door on it except a press, no vote, no meeting, no officer and no money, and about four people in a room understood that and about nine did not.** Not mentioned in this band.
3. **Four people in two counties can be asked about something, twice as many as there were fifty days ago, and one of them is about sixty and there is nobody coming, and one of them cannot write and shut her gate bar for four seconds because a hen was out.** The four were not counted in this band and are not to be counted in any file.
4. **A word is not a person, and the instrument for turning a word into a person is a name at the foot of a thing, and he offered it once in a room in Volume 06 and was refused.** **AND IN THIS BAND HE IS A NAME AT THE FOOT OF A THING, ON THE BACK OF A FLOUR BILL, AND HE DID NOT ASK.**
5. **A column of a hundred and forty-one figures on the inside of a door in a tower has not been added up and was refused out loud a third time, and what the figures count is not known to anybody.** Not mentioned in this band. May not be summed.
6. **A right of refusal that is a permission with no second half is unrestored and nobody has one, and the four words at the head of the plank in the March are not reprinted in any of the ten chapters of this band.** And **the new finding of Chapter 307 is that there is no form for stopping either, and that is the same hole in the same piece of paper, four hundred years on, in a different county.**
7. **A bell in the March is unhung and a bell in Draymoor is owed and is still not rung, and the difference between a bell and a keeper's year is one pound ten, and the difference was said out loud in a kitchen in Ashgill in this band and nobody bought anything with it.**
8. **Nesh Arrad's clock keeps running. The second notice of five shillings and fourpence stays unpaid. The dead man in the fourth line stays dead. The eleven feet stay off the settlement. The charter on the wall in Sallowby is revocable and is not amended.**
9. **The carrying is off nothing. The leg is the leg. No chapter in this band prints a count of days since the leg cleared and a later pass may not print one.**
10. **The Ryefold matter, off nothing, in a book with two halves in it, of which he did not open the second half in this band and which is not named in any of the ten chapters, and the new question is not among the things named and is not adjacent to any of them.**
11. **The question what route connects the null districts to the First Compact's original anchor network is asked in its stated form NOWHERE in this band, and all three shapes it took in Volume 06 are spent, and `outline/volume-07.md:104` forbids a fourth.**
12. **Why the missing positions answer in a voice that sounds like the System is asked once, near the end of the volume, and is not answered. IT IS NOT ASKED IN THIS BAND AND NOBODY SPECULATES IT.**
13. **The midpoint has not landed. It is fixed at Chapter 321, 322, 323 or 324, and no character in Chapters 301 to 320 may speculate it and be right.**
14. **The volume's real work — six arrangements put beside each other without joining them, and a man who discovers he cannot be in two places at once — has begun and has not been paid for. HE IS A NAME ON A BILL AND NINE PEOPLE IN A MARKET KNOW HE CAN SEE A JOIN AND A MAN OF THIRTY EIGHT HAS A MONTH OF NOTICE AND A WOMAN OF THIRTY IS THE NINTH IN A LINE AND A WOMAN OF SEVENTY-FOUR HAS NINE DAYS AND A DATE.**
15. **Draymoor is not one of the six and he did not go there. The finding that belongs to it is said in one paragraph in Chapter 306 and rests there.**

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM

1. **The board chain, and the ten own rings on a line of their own, which is at the top block of `state/current.md` §1 and NOWHERE ELSE, and a table with a ring on the wrong row is a defect this project has been bitten four times.**
2. **The Batch 0001 prompt's own table disagrees with its own cards on seven of the ten days and three of its ten totals are not constructible from the March's weights. The morning figures and the own-rings line are the authority and the delivery took them.**
3. **A clean legality sweep, which is not the same thing as a correct chapter. The two-alternative day pattern returns three over this band and the prose says its day in a third form; when the unseen two thirds were read by hand both of the band's own calendar errors were in them.**
4. **A forty-character carry-over is connective tissue and a seventy-character one is a defect in most cases, and the within-band residual in this band is entirely the two printed money workings, which must not be reworded.**
5. **The hundred and forty days is still wrong and has still not been corrected, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110. And the same failure with a bell in it is a bell that is not rung.**
6. **A well-formed number. This band's own first writing had four, and every one of them had the unit price in the sentence, which is the only reason they were caught. Print the unit price in the sentence, find it, and multiply, and do not run a list of numbers and believe it. AND DO NOT LET AN INSTRUMENT OPEN A CHAPTER FILE FOR WRITING.**

## VOLUME 06 — Chapters 291–300 and the CLOSE OF THE VOLUME, *The Erasure, And The Status*. **THE FACT BASE FOR EVERYTHING AFTER IT. The block below it, `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0004`, and everything under that, is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the thirty-one findings is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 06 IS CLOSED, AND BATCH 0005 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the state of the Briar March and of one valley four hundred miles off at the end of Chapter 300, written to be read by somebody who has read none of the fifty chapters. Every figure was measured against the ten chapter files after the prose was finished, and the live record's §1 and §4 are the authority for both the chain and the money.**

### THE GROUND IN IT AS IT STANDS ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE SIXTY-FIFTH WEEK

**Coldharrow: about two hundred and ten people in thirty-eight houses, a tower with a bell in it, about nine hundred elms of which about two hundred are dead, one gate which is a post with a bar across it that is not shut and is not shut for the whole of its life, and a board on the north wall of the tower at chest height where a person in the road can read it.** The lane runs from village to village and not from north to south, through eleven villages: Coldharrow, **Ellerby**, **Trope**, **Nettlebed**, Wrenny, Ashgill, Sallow, Cadgerow, Henthill, Birrow, and one not named. **Ellerby is nine miles up the lane from Coldharrow. Trope is about the fourth village on it and Nettlebed about the sixth, and the two of them are about four miles apart. Coldharrow-edge is eleven miles off the lane, is not in the March, and keeps Shelf weeks.** At the far end of the lane, past the last village, there is about two miles of hedge and a field and a boundary nobody has walked in about forty years, and then a road, four miles off, going somewhere else entirely. **AND, FROM CHAPTER 292, THE WAY PAST ALL OF THAT: a bad road called Stennick goes out of the sixth house, past nine farms, and comes in at a place where there is a well and a roof that fell in, and a person who comes in at that end of the county does not pass a bar, because there is no bar on that road and there never has been. That road goes on for the better part of a day and the ground drops and after that it is somebody else's ground, and about four hundred miles off the end of it is Draymoor.**

**THE BOARD. A plank about the size of a door, painted and unpainted so many times that the grain has come up in long pale streaks. Thirty-one years in two hands: the older hand is the first six years and it is in the grain, the newer is twenty-five years and it is not in the grain and it is the more careful. No year at the head, no column, no mark against any stroke. THE OPENING FIGURE IS 63,510 AND THE WORKING IS 6 × 1,460 = 8,760 PLUS 25 × 2,190 = 54,750.** It read **sixty-three thousand eight hundred and forty-one at the second light of the first day of the sixty-fifth week, which is the last morning of Volume 06, and the difference from the figure on the first morning of Chapter 251 is three hundred and thirty-one, and the first morning after the volume is sixty-three thousand eight hundred and forty-nine, which a woman of forty-one said out loud on a ladder for the first time in twenty-five years.**

**AND THE THIRD HAND, and this is the whole of what is known about it: it has not changed. A figure in white chalk at the foot, written on the first morning of a man's being in this country, reading sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten, and it has stood there for forty-nine days and nobody has told him to wipe it and he has not offered, and NOBODY WROTE ON THAT BOARD IN CHAPTERS 291 TO 300 AT ALL.** The figure in it is also the figure at the foot of a sheet in a press eleven miles off and also the figure in a yard two hundred and ten people know at a glance, and nobody has said whose hand it is in.

**AND TWO NEW THINGS THE BOARD DID IN THE LAST TEN DAYS, AND THEY ARE THE LAST THINGS THE INSTRUMENT HAS.** From Chapter 297: **a day of eight and a day of eight, and they are not the same day, and nothing on that plank will ever say which is which.** Eight is four bells and a market. Eight is four bells and a stranger and a burial, because a stranger is three and a burial is one. From Chapter 299: eight is also four bells and a debt called in and a fire, because a debt called in is two and a fire is two. **THE COMPLETE LIST OF WEIGHTS IS A BURIAL ONE, A BOUNDARY WALKED SEVEN, A DEBT CALLED IN TWO, A MARKET FOUR, A STRANGER THREE, A FIRE TWO — and from Chapter 299 a stroke can be a count of a thing that did not happen, because the second of the two rings for a fire is rung whether or not it is out.** A day of four is the ordinary number; a day of eight has now three wholly different causes; a day of thirteen is nine strangers; and a day's figure remains a WEIGHTED number that cannot be turned back into what was done.

**NONE OF IT CONVERTS INTO THE SHELF AND THERE IS NO FORMULA AND THERE IS NOT GOING TO BE ONE.** A ring is not a week, a March boundary is dated in rings, a Bench order must be dated in the grain year, and the price of the conversion is £1 3s 6d and fifteen days of the road. **AND FROM CHAPTER 292, THE SAME FAILURE IN A FOURTH SHAPE: a bell that is not rung is a day that does not happen. Draymoor has a tower with a bell in it that has not been rung since before a woman of forty-one's mother died, and nobody in that valley can say in which year the money for ringing it stopped, and she has asked twice and has been told both times that it was before she was there.**

### THE DOCUMENTS THAT EXIST AFTER CHAPTER 300, ONE EACH, AND WHERE EACH ONE IS

1. **THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW.** Above. **63,841, two hands and a third in chalk, and the third has not been added to in forty-nine days.**
2. **THE CORRIDOR, AND IT IS THE ONLY DOCUMENT IN THIS PROJECT WITH A SIGNATURE ON IT THAT MATTERS.** One sheet, nine lines, at the head of it the one word CORRIDOR, the fourth line saying it takes effect on the day it is signed, and a space at the foot about two inches long which is the widest thing on the sheet. A copy of it at threepence was carried up the lane in a corded bundle and was read out twice in a yard at Coldharrow on the sixth day of the sixty-third week. **THE ORIGINAL IS IN A VALLEY FOUR HUNDRED MILES OFF AND IS NOT IN THIS COUNTY, AND THE CORRIDOR IS STANDING AND IS NOT GOING TO BE TORN UP, AND THERE IS NO CLAUSE IN IT AND NOTHING WRITTEN ON THE BACK OF IT AND NOTHING TO APPEAL TO.** It cost a valley of about a hundred and forty people its place on every map a city keeps.
3. **THE SEALED COPY OF THE FOUR PAGES.** Four lines about a water, a height, a day and a party to pay, copied and sealed at Sallowby at **threepence**, carried four hundred miles by a sawyer of about fifty-one who was given five shillings and four days and has not read it. **It is under the seat of a timber cart in this county and will be under a seat on the day it goes, and it was NOT opened, read aloud, produced or discussed in ANY of the fifty chapters of this volume**, and at the end of Chapter 300 it was still in the county and nobody had asked for it and about four people know it is there.
4. **THE SHEET.** Two hundred words in SABLE VENN'S OWN HAND — she could not read it back, she put no day on it on purpose — carried out of this county on a barrow at a gate that is not shut by a woman of about forty who has carried other people's paper between eleven villages for nineteen years and has never once opened any of it. At the foot of it, on the left, under the last line, in pencil, in a hand that is not any hand in eleven villages, is **sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten**, and there is no day anywhere on the sheet, and nobody in this county has said whose it is. **THIRTY-ONE DAYS OLD AT THE END OF THE BAND, and a second route out of this county went out on the seventh day of the sixty-fourth week in the same barrow by the same woman, who did not open that one either, and nobody in that yard knew what was on it and about four of them had a fair idea.**
5. **THE FORM 6 OF THE WATER-SEAT OF SALLOWBY.** One printed sheet, dated in a year this county does not keep, brought up on a barrow under a cloth with a list of eggs on it. **Its fourth line is a door: where no day can be shown, the acknowledgment may be entered without one, and the entry will be marked as made without a day. IT IS NOT OPENED, IT IS NOT REFUSED, AND NOBODY HAS BEEN ASKED, and a clerk of forty-four has confirmed from four hundred miles off that NINE SUCH FORMS HAVE BEEN ENTERED IN NINE YEARS, every one of them by a person who was in a room and had been asked what day it was and had said there was not one, and that nobody in a building with about thirty clerks in it has ever been asked to look at what a form does after it is entered.** **AND THE BAND'S OWN FINDING, WHICH IS THE SAME FACT FROM THE OTHER END AND IS NEW: the corridor was produced to a room and a line will be written about it in a book in a building with a yard, and nobody will ever be asked what that line does afterwards, and a man of fifty-two said so in a yard in about nine words while describing a sheet he had cut out of the back of a bill with his own knife.**
6. **BRAM ORREN'S LINE BOOK.** A book of order and not of dates, run since he was twelve, four bells at the top of every page, two pages lost in the ninth year, not copied and not shown. **It has a question in it with a mark on the end of it that nobody has answered. IT IS FORTY-THREE DAYS OLD AT THE END OF THIS BAND AND NOT ONE DAY OF THAT IS THE BOY'S FAULT: the Chapter 296 card for Chapter 297 would have had him at forty-one days, and `290:29` in the previous chapter says twenty days, and both are wrong, and the truth is forty-three.** He can still be asked about the third place in about six weeks and was not asked and does not want two things asked at once. His read-back is not a witness and he says so before anybody does.
7. **THE BOARD AT ELLERBY.** **TWO HUNDRED AND NINE NAMES WITH NO FIGURES, NO COLUMN, NO TOTAL AND NO DATE**, on the outside wall of the last house, in the order things happened, nobody struck out. **THERE IS NO BELL-KEEPER AND NO WAGE IN ELLERBY. The bell is in a frame on two uprights in a field behind the last house and it is rung by whoever is nearest, and it is a custom of about four hundred years that everybody can describe and nobody in the village can date, and the man of about thirty-five with a barrow has said that he says four hundred because that is what you say. NO MOUTH THAT HAS BEEN IN ELLERBY SAYS THE WORDS *FOUR HUNDRED YEARS* IN ANY CHAPTER OF THIS BAND.** And a man of about seventy-four who died at the second house in Coldharrow before the noon bell in this band is on this wall and is not, because he was not in Ellerby and nobody struck him out because he was never on it.
8. **THE BOARD IN THE TOWER AT NETTLEBED, AND IT IS THE FIRST OF THE THREE TO BE NAMED IN A RECORD AT ALL.** A plank on the **inside** of the door of a tower about four times the height of a house, with a hasp that is not in its staple, and a key on a nail inside a house at the tower foot that a man of about thirty-one has never once used. **THE HAS HAS BEEN ON THE HAS SINCE THE SECOND DAY OF THE SIXTY-FIRST WEEK AND HE SAID HE WOULD LEAVE IT, and it was still on the hasp at the end of this volume, and nobody has taken it off the hasp and nobody has asked him to.** A HUNDRED AND FORTY-ONE NAMES, in no column and no row and in an order that is not the order things happened, about seventy of them in the other ten villages, and one in a blue coat, and nobody struck out. At the head of it, in a hand **dated at about four hundred years by a clerk of forty-four whose trade is dating hands and who is NOT NAMED IN ANY CHAPTER OF VOLUME 06 AND MAY NOT BE**, four words. **THE FOUR WORDS ARE NOT REPRINTED IN ANY OF CHAPTERS 291 TO 300.** Under them, in the same hand, a line about a finger long which the clerk could not get all of in a tower and read out the part he could. **It is not a charter. There is no seal, no signature, no witness line and nothing at the foot of it.** **BESIDE THE NAMES THERE IS A COLUMN WITH NO HEADING OVER IT, with a figure in it against every one of the hundred and forty-one, and the figures are all different, and NOBODY ADDED THEM UP, and a man of fifty-two refused to, out loud, in front of about twenty people, twice, on purpose, because a column with nothing over it is not a total and adding it produces a number that is wrong about the world. The last nine figures in it are in the hand of a woman of fifty-one's grandmother and the column stops there.** **IT WAS NOT REMOVED BY ANYBODY. A bell is the only thing in eleven villages anybody has ever gone and looked for, and there has not been one in that village for about two hundred years. WHAT THE COLUMN COUNTS IS NOT KNOWN TO ANYBODY, WAS REFUSED A THIRD TIME AT `300:53`, AND IS NOT TO BE ANSWERED IN VOLUME 07 EITHER.**
9. **THE REGISTER, AND IT IS NOW FIFTY-TWO PLACES AND NOT FIFTY-ONE, AND THE FIFTY-SECOND IS A VALLEY FOUR HUNDRED MILES OFF.** Four sheets of paper cut out of the back of a bill with a knife, in the hand of a man of fifty-two, with a second hand on the fourth. **THE FIFTY-SECOND PLACE, DRAYMOOR, WAS ENTERED BY A PERSON OF THAT PLACE SAYING A SENTENCE IN THAT PLACE, over four days, and about eleven of a hundred and forty people there said one and about a hundred and twenty did not, AND NOBODY ASKED THE HUNDRED AND TWENTY, and the leader of the place was asked and did not.** **IT STILL HAS ONE PLACE IN IT FROM THE LANE ITSELF, WHICH IS NETTLEBED, ON FOUR WORDS ABOUT A LANE, and a second line nobody can classify, which is a dispute about ninety acres and eleven families, and it has lost two crosses on a market morning without anybody asking, and there is a cross against the eleventh name on the sheet which is a place on this lane that nobody in two hundred and ten people can name and that therefore cannot come off.** It is kept in a book on a shelf in the one room in eleven villages, which is a press at Coldharrow-edge belonging to a woman of twenty-nine who sells days, and she is not letting it and is not charging anybody and has not been asked what it costs her. **The recordwright of twenty-eight keeps it and is not in it. The clerk of forty-four is not in it. The man of thirty-one is not in it and put no name to it. The woman with a bag is not in it in any capacity. The man who signed the corridor is not in it. NOBODY ELSE IS IN IT EXCEPT PLACES.** **THE RULES ARE ELEVEN LINES AND THERE IS NO TWELFTH LINE, and a recordwright of twenty-eight has said so out loud twice and has not improved on them.**
10. **THE STATUS, AND IT IS NEW IN THIS BAND, AND IT IS THE ONLY THING IN EITHER COUNTY THAT IS A DOCUMENT ANYBODY CAN PRODUCE.** One sheet, on the fifth of nine sheets cut out of the back of a bill, in the hand of a man of fifty-two, **nothing at the top of it and nothing at the foot of it and no name on it**, standing on the inside of a door in a tower in a village of about ninety people four miles up the lane. It says five things and they are at `state/current.md` §5 item 29 and in `298:15`–`21`. **It contains no gate, no toll, no bar, no date, no bench, no seat, no chair, no room with a door on it except a press, no vote, no meeting, no officer and no money, and it names a place that cannot be found and leaves it named.** About four people in a room understood what it meant and about nine did not.
11. **THE LETTER, which came into this county on a barrow on the third day of the sixty-third week and was read out in a yard by two people neither of whom was in the room where it was written.** About the size of a hand, sealed, and not a form. It says four things and the four are at `state/current.md` §5 of the Batch 0004 block. It contains a two-syllable word at the head of a column in a building four hundred miles off, which about five of nine people in that yard had never had cause to learn. **ELEVEN DAYS OLD AT THE END OF THIS BAND. The clerk of forty-four is four hundred miles off and is not coming back and told the yard not to write back, and the nine-word question at the end of it — is there a road inside your lane — was read out in a yard and nobody answered it and nobody said they could not answer it.**
12. **AND THE THING ON THE BACK OF A MAN'S HAND, which is not a paper: a man of thirty-four from a county four hundred miles off, who stands in front of about forty people a week and has never once been able to see the thing he is saying out loud, wrote down a thing a woman of about fifty at a bar said to him in about forty seconds in a hand that is not good, and it is the only thing that man has written down in about eleven years. HE WENT HOME FOUR HUNDRED MILES AND DID NOT ACT ON HIS SENTENCE IN THIS COUNTY, AND THERE WAS NOTHING HERE FOR HIM TO ACT ON, AND THAT IS A FACT AND NOT A MERCY.**

**AND WHAT IS NOT IN EITHER COUNTY AND WAS NOT IN EITHER COUNTY AND CANNOT BE PRODUCED IN EITHER: a day.** A Bench order must be dated in the grain year. There is nothing in any form in the building at Coldharrow-edge that will take sixty-three thousand eight hundred and forty-one, and there is nothing in any form in a building four hundred miles off that will take it either, and the fourth line of the form says so politely and in writing, and a clerk of forty-four has confirmed from the other end that nine such entries have been made in nine years by people who were in a room and were asked and answered truthfully. **AND THE BAND'S ADDITION, WHICH IS THE SAME FACT POINTING THE OTHER WAY: a STATUS WITH NO DAY IN IT IS NOT A LESSER THING THAN A CORRIDOR WITH A DAY IN IT, IT IS THE SAME PAPER WITH THE DAY LEFT OUT ON PURPOSE, and that is the whole of what four hundred years of a list on the inside of a door was for, and about four people in a yard heard it and nobody thanked anybody.**

### THE PEOPLE, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST AND NONE OF THEM IS RELIEVED AND NONE OF THEM IS REDEEMED

**CORVIN HALE, FORTY. THE LEADER OF DRAYMOOR, AND THE PERSON WHO SIGNED THE CORRIDOR, AND HE IS ALIVE.** He has been the one who is asked for in that valley for about nine years and has never once been asked what he is for. He had a sentence and it was one question: *does a corridor have anything to say about the water, and if it has, who does the water belong to.* He threw a longer version away because a longer form sounded like a claim and a claim is a thing somebody can be entered against. He got the whole of what forty-four days of a stranger's method got out of a room, which was that the corridor does not touch the water. **He accepted a bargain out loud, in his own mouth, before anybody asked him, with the working printed, and nobody could stop him and the chapter did not stop him. He is not a villain, is not redeemed, is not forgiven, is not relieved and is not thanked. He is on no part of the register and no part of the status. He is owed a bell. He said out loud that he was not going to be told he had done a wicked thing and was not going to be told he had done a good one, and he gave four reasons and kept the fourth, and the fourth is that nobody in nine years had ever asked him what he was for.** `294`, `295`, `298`

**A WOMAN OF FORTY-ONE IN DRAYMOOR. THE FIRST PERSON OF THAT VALLEY THE PROJECT HAS NAMED, AND SHE IS NOT A PRODUCTION AND NOT A VILLAIN.** She walked in at the Stennick end of the county on the evening before Chapter 291 and asked the woman of about fifty at a bar whether a piece of paper can unmake a village. She has a tower with a bell in it that has not been rung since before her mother died, and she has been the one who is asked for in that valley for nine years, and she cannot say in which year the money for ringing it stopped and has asked twice and been told both times that it was before she was there. **She refused the sentence a man of thirty-one offered in her own yard, in about nine words, and gave the reason that a word is not a person and that the instrument for turning a word into a person is a name at the foot of a thing. She said the volume's argument better than he could, in about nine sentences, about a hundred and forty-one people and a bill that somebody kept. She thanked him and was told he was not thanked, and refused was a different sentence and not a worse one.** `291`, `292`, `296`, `297`

**A WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-EIGHT WITH A STICK, in Draymoor, who asked him what he is for, in a yard, unasked by anybody, and a man of about NINETEEN with a hurdle frame who came up the lane and asked whether the man who signed the corridor is on any of the status, and a woman of about THIRTY-ONE with a child on her hip who asked who is going to be responsible for it and got three words back. None of them is a villain and none of them is sent for.** `297`, `298`, `299`

**A WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-SIX WITH A FORM AND A BENCH OF FOUR NINE HUNDRED MILES OFF.** She has done the thing she does eleven times. She is not authorised to describe the report, has not read it, has changed her mind about wanting to know, and would still rather nobody in that valley had it. She allowed the one thing the room got, about a water, in about nine seconds, and said at the gate that the office that deals with water is four hundred miles away and is not her. **SHE IS NOT A VILLAIN AND SHE IS NOT AN AGENT OF ANYTHING AND SHE IS THE MOST ORDINARY PERSON IN THE BAND.** `294`, `299`, `300`

**Ottily Roke, forty-one.** Keeps the bell and the board, twenty-five years, ninepence a day for a hundred and forty days, five pounds five shillings a year. **In this band she read a corridor out loud, standing up, in her own yard, unasked, because that is what she does, and a woman of forty-one from four hundred miles off stood in that yard and heard her and neither of them had been asked and about four people understood the whole of it. She gave the only answer only she could give about the price of a bell and she gave it to a stranger. She said out loud that she had been the only person anybody could ask about for thirty-one years and had not known that was a thing that could be spent, and said it to one person and not to a yard. She worked out, standing on a ladder before anybody else in the village did, that a day of eight and a day of eight are not the same day, and wrote both of them on the end of her cloth in pencil because the plank cannot hold them. She gave out the name of a man of about seventy-four who died in her village and who is on none of the boards in this county. And at the last morning of the volume she said the number out loud on a ladder for the first time in twenty-five years and came down and went to do her day.** `291`, `292`, `297`, `300`

**Isel Cardew, fifty-one. A person of Trope, not produced, not sent for.** Cannot write. Has a handbell she has rung at a lane end for about forty years and has refused three times to say who taught her. **In this band she said, in her own yard, that a hundred and forty-one people said no about four hundred years ago and the thing is not theirs to spend, and that nobody may carry it four hundred miles and lay it on a table in a room so that it can be looked at, and that she was not coming with them, and the man of thirty-one did not argue with her and told her so.** She is one of the four people who understood the status and cannot read it. She was in a corridor four hundred miles off and said out loud, in a yard, that the right of refusal is not restored and nobody has one, and that about nine people have already been told they had got something back and every one of the nine is wrong. Her gate bar still hangs open. `292`, `298`, `299`

**Sera Quill, twenty-eight.** Recordwright, expressly not an advocate, fifty days in the country. **In this band she read a corridor out loud twice in a yard and got two right readings and said the difference between them is nine lines long and every one of the nine is a way of saying the first one; she read the status out loud in a yard in about four minutes, which is the first time anybody in eleven villages has ever done that; she read the whole of the archive of a hundred and forty-one people who said no four hundred years ago out loud in a kitchen, and it is five objects and four of them have never been in a room; she found the direction of the paperwork, which is that an abusive bargain and an honest one are the same document with a different person at the bottom of it and the only instrument is a name; she kept the register, which is now fifty-two places, and is not in it; and she said the cost of a name out loud, which is a pound three shillings and sixpence.** **SHE SAID THE NINE WORDS THREE TIMES IN CHAPTERS 261 TO 270 AND NOT ONCE IN CHAPTERS 271 TO 280 AND NOT ONCE IN CHAPTERS 281 TO 290 AND NOT ONCE IN CHAPTERS 291 TO 300.** Her family claim of six years is unfiled and is not going to be filed and was not mentioned. `291`, `293`, `295`, `296`, `297`, `298`, `299`

**Tarin Keel, fifty-two in the chapters.** **In this band he printed the bell working in a room in front of about nine people and said out loud, before he did it, that he was not saying it to anybody, and it was aimed at the man who was going to sign and it was not said to him. He said a claim about a place and a place nobody can prove are the same thing and that he had been wrong for three weeks. He said that the whole of the defence against a corridor is that nobody in that valley can produce a day, and that it is not a defence, it is the reason. He wrote the status out in about four minutes and read it twice and did not improve it. He said out loud, in a doorway, that the man who signed the corridor is on no part of anything and that he wanted that said by him and not found out in a fortnight by somebody who is annoyed. And at the last hour of the forty-seventh day he took a lead out of his pocket and put it down on a table and did not use it, and said he had noticed.** He has still not corrected the hundred and forty days and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110. `291`, `293`, `294`, `296`, `297`, `298`, `299`

**Sable Venn. A woman. Not a production, not on a paper in the March, not a party, not thanked, not relieved, not rescued, not redeemed, not defeated, and not the villain of anything.** In this band a person of a village, in a village, in about ten words, said that the movement is not hers and that it is a thing places are in, and **she agreed before it had finished**. What changed is not the carrying. She keeps about thirty people of her own and is going to go on going up lanes on her own and putting a paper in front of somebody who has never had one, and she is not going to ask them what they want, and nobody has asked her to stop. **NO CHAPTER OF THIS BAND IS ABOUT HER AND NO CHAPTER HAD HIM TAKE ANYTHING OFF HER, and he was in the doorway and said nothing at all and was not asked and did not offer.** `298`

**Ferrow Hask, thirty-eight.** **In this band he called the same load account in at the bar for the ninth time in nineteen years and has never once been paid, and said out loud that he has never once worked out why he goes on calling it in, and that he had had four days to work it out.** He is the honest opponent of the volume and not a villain. He is not relieved and he is not on the register. `295`, `299`, `300`

**Wenna Skell, about sixty.** The only living person who was standing on the strip when it was decided, and the only person who can say where a boundary started, and not the only one who thinks she can. **Unadvanced and untalked in this band, and that is a fact and not an oversight: she is in the March, she is about sixty, and nobody went to her and nobody asked her, and the finding of the volume is that nobody is coming and the two things she can say are both going when she is.** `297`, `300`

**A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO OWNS THE TOWER AT NETTLEBED AND MAKES HURDLES AND GATES. A MAN, and he is not the man of thirty-one from four hundred miles off.** **In this band he said the sentence that takes sole authority off a woman with a board, in his own doorway, in about ten words, unasked, and she agreed before it had finished.** `298`

**Petrow Sill, thirty-five.** Lives at the end of a bad road called Stennick, nine farms and a well and a roof that fell in and about eleven people in his house. **In this band he told three people the way past the end of the county, before anybody asked him, and he would not give it up for a paper, and he printed his own two days at one shilling and sixpence and said he was not going to be paid and was not going to be thanked and was going to lose two days of his year over it.** He is not on the register in any capacity and nobody asked him. `292`

**A WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY AT A BAR.** Twenty-two years at one gate, and AT THE BAR at the head of it the only heading in eleven villages. **In this band she said out loud, in a yard, that a word at the top of a thing means every line underneath it is for that and nothing else, and that a word at the top of a sheet about a place is going to be the reason nobody in that place is asked a second question for as long as the sheet stands, and that she has been at that bar for twenty-two years and nobody has ever asked her a second question. She told nine strangers, in about four minutes, that they were not counting and that she could tell the difference between a man counting and a man looking, and she would like it noticed that she has told two people that in about four minutes and has not been asked it once. NOBODY ASKED HER A SECOND QUESTION IN THIS BAND, and that is the last state of that and nobody in eleven villages has a form that would change it.** `291`, `293`, `298`, `299`, `300`

**A CLERK OF FORTY-FOUR. A man, of the Seat, four hundred miles off, canon in eleven chapters of Volume 05 and NOT NAMED IN ANY CHAPTER OF VOLUME 06 AND MAY NOT BE NAMED BY ONE.** He is four hundred miles off and is not coming back. He sent the letter, and he is the reason the Band knows that nine forms have been entered without a day in nine years, and he is quoted three times in the last chapter of the volume as the man who has handled about four thousand documents and could not get the last line of the head of a plank in a stair. `299`, `300`

**THREE MEN FROM FOUR HUNDRED MILES OFF**, of whom one is a man of thirty-four who stands in front of about forty people a week and has never once been able to see the thing he is saying out loud. **He said his sentence in this county three times and it was said a fourth time in a yard in Draymoor by a man of fifty-two, to nine people of that district, and he did not send it and was not in the county when it was said. HE WENT HOME FOUR HUNDRED MILES WITH A SCRAP OF HIS OWN HANDWRITING ON THE BACK OF HIS OWN HAND AND HE DID NOT ACT ON IT IN THIS COUNTY AND THERE WAS NOTHING HERE FOR HIM TO ACT ON.** Neither of the three is a villain and neither is an agent of anything and nobody sent for them. `293`, `299`, `300`

**Unnamed and standing:** about two hundred and ten people; about nine hundred elms; a man of about seventy-four at the second house in Coldharrow who died before the noon bell on the fifth day of the sixty-fourth week and is on none of the boards in this county; about forty people in a valley of about a hundred and forty, of whom about nine are under about nine years old; a miller at Trope whose wheel you can be seen from four hundred yards off; a woman of about thirty-five with a barrow in Ellerby; about nine people in a yard who know the third hand at a glance.

### THE THREE CLOCKS, AND WHAT THE BAND DID TO THEM

**The Shelf: fifty-two weeks of seven days, day one the second day of the fortieth week. The sixty-third week is 161 to 167, the sixty-fourth 168 to 174, the sixty-fifth opens at 175. Batch 0001 ran 126 to 135, Batch 0002 ran 136 to 145, Batch 0003 ran 146 to 155, Batch 0004 ran 156 to 165, this batch ran 166 to 175, and VOLUME 07 OPENS ON 176. Nothing turns the grain year here and no file may say that it turns.**

**The bell: four a day, the first, the change, the noon, the last, and a day begins at the first and ends at the last and nobody in eleven villages has ever said what either is a height above. From Chapter 299 a stroke can count a thing that did not happen.**

**The board: a count, thirty-one years, and a day's figure is a WEIGHTED number, and from Chapter 282 the list of weights is not a set of signatures, from Chapter 290 the commonest quantity in the county has no weight at all, and from Chapter 297 the weights COLLIDE: a day of eight has three wholly different causes and the plank cannot tell them apart.**

**The year turn: the first bell after the ewes come down off the fell, and it moves about three weeks and it is the only date a stranger can be taught in a morning.**

### THE MONEY AT THE END OF THE VOLUME, EVERY FIGURE WITH ITS WORKING

| Figure | The working, and it is the whole of it |
|---|---|
| **A keeper's year = 1,260d = £5 5s** | 9 × 140 = **1,260d** ✓ |
| **A carter's year = 2,520d = £10 10s, and two days there and two days back = 72d = 6s** | 18 × 140 = **2,520d** ✓; 4 × 18 = **72d**; 72 ÷ 12 = **6s** ✓. **AND 120d = 10s IS THE REMAINDER OF A CARTER'S YEAR, 2,520 − 2,400 = 120, and the first writing of this band printed the four days' walking as 120d and as eight shillings, which are the wrong figure and a wrong conversion of the right one, and it was caught by the unit-price instrument because the unit price was in the sentence and 4 × 18 is not 120** |
| **THE ONE FIGURE VOLUME 06 DERIVED IN ITS LAST BAND, and it is a difference between two rows and not a new row, and it is the price of the thing a man of forty agreed to accept: a bell, hung, with rope = £6 15s, and a keeper's year = £5 5s, and the difference = £1 10s, EVERY YEAR, FOR AS LONG AS IT IS RUNG** | 31 × 48d = 1,488; + 132 = **1,620d**; 1,620 − 1,440 = 180d = 15s = **£6 15s** ✓; 1,620 − 1,260 = **360d**; 360 − 240 = 120d = 10s = **£1 10s** ✓ |
| **A Bench date, bought = £1 3s 6d = fifteen days of the man who walks the road and one shilling** | 36 + 6 + 240 = **282d**; 282 ÷ 18 = 15 rem 12 ✓ |
| **A copy = threepence, at Sallowby and at any scrivener over a chandler** | CANON ✓ |
| **The levy at a bar = 3d a load at about nine loads a day, named with the unit price in the sentence and NOT derived in this band** | 140 × 9 × 3 = **3,780d** = **£15 15s** ✓ |
| **THE VOLUME'S LAST ARITHMETIC, which is not a new figure and is the volume's argument in a subtraction: 63,841 − 63,510 = 331, and 331 is not a round number, and 200 of it arrived before Batch 0004 began and nobody chose that either, and the other 131 is fifty days of a village doing things** | 63,841 − 63,510 = **331** ✓; and the ten own-days 4+4+13+8+6+4+8+8+8+8 = **71**, and 63,778 + 71 = **63,849** ✓ |
| The eleven families, the four minutes, the ninety acres, the hundred and forty-one figures, the man of about seventy-four at the second house, and the arrears of a valley four hundred miles off | there is no figure for any of these and there is not going to be one, and **the arrears of Draymoor are UNPRICED ON PURPOSE because the only figure this volume's last band was allowed to derive was the pound and ten** |

### THE TWO THINGS A VOLUME 07 WRITER NEEDS AND CANNOT GET FROM ANY FIGURE ABOVE

1. **THAT THE METHOD'S PRICE IS PAID AND THE METHOD CANNOT BE UNPAID, and that a word offered instead of a person was refused in a room four hundred miles off because a name at the foot of a thing is the only part of it anybody can be asked about, and that the whole of what four hundred years of a list on the inside of a door was for is a day left out on purpose.** The volume ends on a man of thirty-one who has offered his only instrument and had it refused and defended a district for four days and got one sentence and is on no part of anything.
2. **THAT A BARGAIN SOMEBODY ACCEPTED OUT LOUD IS STILL STANDING AT THE END OF THE VOLUME AND IS NOT TORN UP, AND THE BELL IT BOUGHT IS OWED AND UNPAID, AND THE PERSON WHO SIGNED IT IS ALIVE AND IS IN HIS OWN FIELD.** Nobody is redeemed. Nobody is forgiven. Nobody is relieved. Nobody is thanked. **Volume 07 inherits a standing cost and not a settlement, and the difference is the whole of what it has to work with.**

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM

1. **Reading a day's rings out of the same row of `outline/volume-06.md`'s Batch 0005 table as the morning figure.** The two columns are not on the same row and the reason is at `state/current.md` §1, and a writer who takes them together will be a day out on every figure in the band and on the volume's last figure.
2. **The chalk count is FIVE for the whole volume and all five are in Batch 0001.** This batch added none. The figure in the chalk is 63,510 and it is the figure on the sheet and it stood for forty-nine days at the last morning.
3. **A clean legality sweep, which is not the same thing as a correct chapter.** Twenty-three day-references, zero illegal, and the third question found two defects: an inherited wrong back-reference at `290:29` in the previous chapter, and Chapter 294's own day at `294:5`, which named the fourth day of the sixty-fourth week when Chapter 294 is the second and the fourth is Chapter 296's morning. **The sweep certified the second one because its pattern required the literal `day of the` and the chapter wrote the bare form *the second of the sixty-fourth week*, so the instrument was blind to the only reference in the band that was wrong. The pattern now runs both forms. A legality sweep that matches one way of saying a thing certifies nothing about the other ways.**
4. **A forty-character carry-over is connective tissue and a seventy-character one is a defect in most cases.** This band returned four hundred and forty-four cross-band and two hundred and fifty-seven within-batch at seventy, and the residual is declared in `state/current.md` §2.4 and §2.5 and is mostly the two printed money workings.
5. **The hundred and forty days.** A man of fifty-two said out loud that it is wrong by about seven in the hundred in a year about every seven years and that he has not corrected it, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110. **And the bell that has not been rung in Draymoor is the same failure with a bell in it: a bell that is not rung is a day that does not happen.**
6. **A well-formed number.** The largest single defect in this volume's history was a levy printed as *three hundred and seventy-eight pounds* where the figure is fifteen pounds fifteen shillings, and the number was perfectly well formed and every scan returned it as clean. **Print the unit price in the sentence, find it, and multiply, and do not run a list of numbers and believe it. And DO NOT LET AN INSTRUMENT OPEN A CHAPTER FILE FOR WRITING: a repair script over seven of the ten files in this batch normalised their whitespace and destroyed their paragraph structure, and the seven were rewritten.**

### THE TWO THINGS THIS BLOCK CARRIES FORWARD FROM THE BLOCKS BELOW IT, WHICH ARE ARCHIVE AND WERE NOT EDITED, AND WHICH ARE RECORDED HERE BECAUSE THEY WERE WRONG

1. **The Batch 0002 live block two blocks below this one said THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW reads *sixty-three thousand five hundred and forty-two* in two places. The figure is sixty-three thousand six hundred and forty-two. That correction was carried forward by Batch 0003, and again by Batch 0004, and is carried forward a third time here: the live figure at the end of Volume 06 is **sixty-three thousand eight hundred and forty-one** and the blocks below are archive and were not edited by this phase.**
2. **The Batch 0002 live block also said that *about thirty-one of the about hundred and forty people in the village are not on* the Ellerby board. It does not add up against a village of a hundred and forty, and the Ellerby board is **two hundred and nine names with no figures, no column, no total and no date**, and about seventy of the two hundred and nine belong to people in four other villages, and there is one in a blue coat that nobody here is for. The blocks below are archive and were not edited by this phase; the correction is here.**


## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0004 — Chapters 281–290, *The Network*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0003` block below it, which is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the twenty-nine findings is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0004 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the state of the Briar March at the end of Chapter 290, written to be read by somebody who has read none of the batch.** Every figure was measured against the ten chapter files after the prose was finished.

### THE GROUND IN IT AS IT STANDS ON THE FIFTH DAY OF THE SIXTY-THIRD WEEK

**Coldharrow: about two hundred and ten people in thirty-eight houses, a tower with a bell in it, about nine hundred elms of which about two hundred are dead, one gate which is a post with a bar across it that is not shut and is not shut for the whole of its life, and a board on the north wall of the tower at chest height where a person in the road can read it.** The lane runs from village to village and not from north to south, through eleven villages: Coldharrow, **Ellerby**, **Trope**, **Nettlebed**, Wrenny, Ashgill, Sallow, Cadgerow, Henthill, Birrow, and one not named. **Ellerby is nine miles up the lane from Coldharrow. Trope is about the fourth village on it and Nettlebed about the sixth, and the two of them are about four miles apart. Coldharrow-edge is eleven miles off the lane, is not in the March, and keeps Shelf weeks.** The ground comes up in eleven places in the first mile and the hedges are the problem and everybody who has walked it knows and nobody says so. **AND, FROM CHAPTER 289, THE OTHER HALF OF IT: at the far end of the lane, past the last village, there is about two miles of hedge and a field and a boundary nobody has walked in about forty years, and then a road, four miles off, going somewhere else entirely. A man of fifty-six with a barrow and a man of fifty-two walked the whole length of it in a day and a half and this is the only time in this volume that anybody has done so.**

**THE BOARD. A plank about the size of a door, painted and unpainted so many times that the grain has come up in long pale streaks. Thirty-one years in two hands: the older hand is the first six years and it is in the grain, the newer is twenty-five years and it is not in the grain and it is the more careful. No year at the head, no column, no mark against any stroke. THE OPENING FIGURE IS 63,510 AND THE WORKING IS 6 × 1,460 = 8,760 PLUS 25 × 2,190 = 54,750.** It read **sixty-three thousand seven hundred and seventy-four** at the second light of the fifth day of the sixty-third week, **and 63,778 is Chapter 291's morning, and that figure is not in this batch and is the one thing a Batch 0005 writer must take from `state/current.md` §1 and not from this file.**

**AND THE THIRD HAND, and this is the whole of what is known about it: it has not changed. A figure in white chalk at the foot, written on the first morning of a man's being in this country, reading sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten, and it has stood there for thirty-nine days and nobody has told him to wipe it and he has not offered, and NOBODY WROTE ON THAT BOARD IN THIS BATCH AT ALL.** The figure in it is also the figure at the foot of a sheet in a press eleven miles off and also the figure in a yard two hundred and ten people know at a glance, and nobody has said whose hand it is in.

**THE BELL. Four a day are the clock — the first, the change, the noon, the last — and about two a day on top of it is everything else, and the complete list is: a burial, one. A boundary walked, seven. A debt called in, two. A market, four. A stranger, three. A fire, two, and the second is rung whether or not it is out. They do not ring for a christening or a marriage.** **AND FROM CHAPTER 282, AND IT IS THE BATCH'S ONE POSITIVE FINDING ABOUT THE LIST ITSELF: the list is not a set of signatures. A stranger is three and a market is four and three and four is seven, and a debt called in is two and a fire is two and a stranger is three and two and two and three is also seven, so there are at least four different days a plank in this county can say seven about and there is nothing whatever on the plank that tells them apart. A woman of forty-one has two sevens in ten days and believed until this week that the pair meant one walk.**

**AND FROM CHAPTER 290, THE OTHER POSITIVE FINDING, and it is about a quantity and not about a day: there is no stroke in this county for a load.** Three for a stranger, four for a market, two for a debt, seven for a boundary, one for a burial, and a bar that collects loads all day at threepence each has nothing to count a load in, because a load is not a thing that happens, it is a thing a man has decided is a load, and he decides it about four hundred miles off in a building, and the woman at the bar finds out when it stops at her gate. She has known this for twenty-two years and nobody had ever asked her.

**AND THE TWENTY-FIVE-YEAR ARITHMETIC, unchanged: 25 × 2,190 = 54,750, and 25 × 1,460 = 36,500, and 54,750 − 36,500 = 18,250, and that remainder is every burial, boundary, debt, market, stranger and fire in twenty-five years, which is about two a day, and about two a day does not touch most days, and the days it does not touch are the days the board says four on.**

**NONE OF IT CONVERTS INTO THE SHELF AND THERE IS NO FORMULA AND THERE IS NOT GOING TO BE ONE.** A ring is not a week, a March boundary is dated in rings, a Bench order must be dated in the grain year, and the price of the conversion is £1 3s 6d and fifteen days of the road.

### THE DOCUMENTS THAT EXIST IN THE BRIAR MARCH AS OF CHAPTER 290, ONE EACH, AND WHERE EACH ONE IS

1. **THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW.** Above. **63,774, two hands and a third in chalk, and the third has not been added to in thirty-nine days.**
2. **THE SEALED COPY OF THE FOUR PAGES.** Four lines about a dam, a height, a day and a party to pay, copied and sealed at Sallowby at **threepence**, carried four hundred miles by a sawyer of about fifty-one who was given five shillings and four days and has not read it. **It went out of the county on the eleventh day and came back on the twentieth and has been under a cart seat ever since and will be under a seat on the day it goes. IT IS STILL THERE AND IT WAS NOT OPENED, READ ALOUD, PRODUCED OR DISCUSSED IN ANY OF CHAPTERS 281 TO 290**, and the only mention it got in this batch is a clerk of forty-four four hundred miles off writing about the fourth line of the form it was copied from. A copy of it is in the hands of a woman of about sixty at Ellerby who asked a woman with a barrow for it because she wanted to hold a thing that is not a field. A second copy is in the hand of a woman of twenty-nine at a press at Coldharrow-edge, and at the foot of it, on the left, under the last line, in pencil, in a hand that is not any hand in eleven villages, is **sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten**, and there is no day anywhere on the sheet, and nobody in this county has said whose it is.
3. **THE SHEET. Two hundred words in SABLE VENN'S OWN HAND — she could not read it back, she put no day on it on purpose, and she wrote it in a kitchen in Ellerby on the eighteenth day and had it put on a barrow at a gate that is not shut and carried down the lane by a woman of about forty who has never opened another person's paper in nineteen years and has said so out loud, unprompted, that she has not read it. It is not entered anywhere, it is not owed to anybody, and it is the first route out of the county that anybody chose.** Its bottom two inches were empty when the barrow went out of the gate and were not empty by the next morning, and the figure in them is the number above. **Unchanged in this batch. It is now thirty days old and about four people know whose hand the figure is in.**
4. **THE FORM 6 OF THE WATER-SEAT OF SALLOWBY.** One printed sheet, dated in a year this county does not keep, brought up on a barrow in a drawer under a cloth with a list of eggs on it, four days late on a day that was not a market day. It is correct about everything and wrong about the place in three ways, and **it asks for a day, and a name, and it says where the name goes, and its fourth line is a door: where no day can be shown, the acknowledgment may be entered without one, and the entry will be marked as made without a day. NOBODY HAS OPENED IT, NOBODY HAS REFUSED IT, AND NOBODY WAS ASKED, and it was not opened or refused or mentioned by anybody in eleven villages in this batch.** A clerk of forty-four describes it in a letter as a printed clause and not a secret and not a scandal and not in anybody's pocket, and says that a woman of about fifty at a gate read that line out loud in a yard on the seventh day of the sixtieth week, unasked, and said that whoever built that door in was being fair, and that being fair and being useful are two different things, and that she was not opening it.
5. **BRAM ORREN'S LINE BOOK.** A book of order and not of dates, run since he was twelve, four bells at the top of every page, two pages lost in the ninth year, not copied and not shown. **It contains one question, written on the sixth day of the sixtieth week in a doorway with a mark at the end of it, and nobody has answered it and it has not been asked a second time, and the man of thirty-one's answer to it in that doorway is on the record as not knowing, and the boy said that made two of them.** **IT HAS A THIRD PLACE IN IT AND HE CAN SEE IT AND DOES NOT KNOW WHAT IT IS FOR, and he wrote nothing, and he said he would not be hurried, and he can be asked in about six weeks and was not asked in this batch.** **AND FROM CHAPTER 284, HIS USE AS AN INSTRUMENT, which is not a witness and he says so: a recordwright of twenty-eight had a thing written on the inside of a door and had it read back by him and said out loud that the check is on her hand and not on her ear, and he said *that is not a witness* before she did, and she said *I know exactly what it is not*, and there is no word in this country for the difference and there ought to be one.**
6. **THE BOARD AT ELLERBY.** A plank of the same kind, and **two hundred and nine names on it and no figures anywhere and no total and no date and no column**, in the order they happened. **THERE IS NO BELL-KEEPER AND NO WAGE IN ELLERBY. The bell is in a frame on two uprights in a field behind the last house and it is rung by whoever is nearest, and it is a custom of about four hundred years that everybody can describe and nobody in the village can date, and the man of about thirty-five with a barrow has said that he says four hundred because that is what you say.** **NO MOUTH THAT HAS BEEN IN ELLERBY SAYS THE WORDS *FOUR HUNDRED YEARS* IN ANY OF THE TEN CHAPTERS OF THIS BATCH AND A BATCH 0005 WRITER MAY NOT EITHER.** The order the names are in holds the same round of eleven villages that the debts go round in at the Coldharrow bar, and it holds for people who were not in Ellerby when it was written, and it is not the order of the ground. **In Chapter 282 the wall was read as the one record either village has that names anybody and can be asked about, and a man of about thirty-five with a barrow came and went twice in that yard and offered them nothing and asked them nothing.**
7. **THE BOARD IN THE TOWER AT NETTLEBED, AND IT IS THE FIRST OF THE THREE TO BE NAMED IN A RECORD AT ALL.** A plank on the **inside** of the door of a tower about four times the height of a house, with a hasp that is not in its staple, and a key on a nail inside a house at the tower foot that a man of about thirty-one has never once used. **THE HAS HAS BEEN ON THE HAS SINCE THE SECOND DAY OF THE SIXTY-FIRST WEEK AND HE SAID HE WOULD LEAVE IT, and it was still on the hasp in Chapter 284, and nobody has taken it off the hasp, and nobody has asked him to.** **A HUNDRED AND FORTY-ONE NAMES, in no column and no row and in an order that is not the order things happened, about seventy of them in the other ten villages, and one in a blue coat, and nobody struck out.** At the head of it, in a hand **dated at about four hundred years by a clerk of forty-four whose trade is dating hands and who refuses to give a year**, four words: **ANY ONE MAY SAY NO. THE FOUR WORDS ARE NOT REPRINTED IN ANY OF CHAPTERS 281 TO 290 AND A BATCH 0005 WRITER MAY NOT REPRINT THEM EITHER.** Under them, in the same hand, a line about a finger long which the clerk could not get all of in a tower and read out the part he could. **It is not a charter. There is no seal, no signature, no witness line and nothing at the foot of it.** **BESIDE THE NAMES THERE IS A COLUMN WITH NO HEADING OVER IT, with a figure in it against every one of the hundred and forty-one, and the figures are all different, and NOBODY ADDED THEM UP, and a man of fifty-two refused to, out loud, in front of about twenty people, twice, on purpose, because a column with nothing over it is not a total. The last nine figures in it are in the hand of a woman of fifty-one's grandmother and the column stops there.** **IT WAS NOT REMOVED BY ANYBODY. It is on the inside of a door in a tower in a village that has no bell, and a bell is the only thing in eleven villages anybody has ever gone and looked for.** **WHAT THE COLUMN COUNTS IS NOT KNOWN TO ANYBODY, INCLUDING THE FOUR PEOPLE WHO READ IT, AND IT WAS NOT ANSWERED IN THIS BATCH.**
8. **THE REGISTER, AND IT IS THE ONLY THING IN THE BRIAR MARCH THAT A PERSON OF A VILLAGE WENT INTO.** Four sheets of paper cut out of the back of a bill with a knife, in the hand of a man of fifty-two, with a second hand on the fourth. **FIFTY-ONE PLACES as before. IT NOW HAS ONE PLACE IN IT, WHICH IS NETTLEBED, ENTERED ON THE SECOND DAY OF THE SIXTY-THIRD WEEK ON FOUR WORDS ABOUT A LANE SAID BY A PERSON OF THAT VILLAGE IN THAT VILLAGE. IT HAS A SECOND LINE UNDER IT, WHICH IS A DISPUTE ABOUT NINETY ACRES AND ELEVEN FAMILIES, WHICH A RECORDWRIGHT OF TWENTY-EIGHT WROTE DOWN AND CANNOT CLASSIFY AND WHICH NOBODY ELSE HAS CLASSIFIED. AND IT HAS LOST TWO CROSSES, ON A MARKET MORNING, WITHOUT ANYBODY ASKING.** It is kept in a book on a shelf in the one room in eleven villages, which is a press at Coldharrow-edge belonging to a woman of twenty-nine who sells days, and she is not letting it and is not charging anybody and has not been asked what it costs her, and she said in Chapter 283 that it is the only thing she has that is worth more than her press and that she is telling a stranger because he walked eleven miles. **The recordwright of twenty-eight keeps it and is not in it. The clerk of forty-four is not in it and is four hundred miles off. The man of thirty-one is not in it and put no name to it.** **THE RULES ARE ELEVEN LINES AND THERE IS NO TWELFTH LINE, and a recordwright of twenty-eight said so out loud in a yard on the last day of the sixty-second week and said that she wrote the lines and will not improve on them.**
9. **THE TALY IN A CARTER'S HEAD.** Ferrow Hask has kept one for nineteen years: what a gate is for. **IT WAS ANSWERED IN CHAPTER 289, IN A YARD, BY A MAN OF FIFTY-SIX WITH A BARROW WHO HAD WALKED THE LANE: a bar across the end of a lane is not a gate, there is no road behind it and there never was, and a lane does not have a gate, and a piece of wood across a gap in a hedge is what it is. AND THE CARTER OF THIRTY-EIGHT DID NOT SAY THE ANSWER IN A YARD, and nobody asked him, and nobody thanked anybody, and he went back to the ninth cart.**
10. **THE LETTER, WHICH IS NEW IN THIS BATCH AND IS THE FIRST PAPER IN THIS VOLUME THAT CAME FROM FOUR HUNDRED MILES WITH A PERSON'S HAND ON IT.** About the size of a hand, sealed, **and not a form.** Written in a taught hand in a town that keeps weeks the writer has never learned, by the clerk of forty-four, on the day before he got on a cart going further, and carried back up the lane on a barrow by a carrier who would not say where it had come from. **IT WAS READ ALOUD IN A YARD IN COLDHARROW ON THE THIRD DAY OF THE SIXTY-THIRD WEEK BY TWO PEOPLE, a woman of fifty-one who cannot read and a recordwright of twenty-eight who can, and neither of them was in the room where it was written, and the man of thirty-one was in the yard and was not the addressee and is not on it.** It says four things and the four are at `state/current.md` §5 item 27. **It contains a two-syllable word at the head of a column in a building four hundred miles off, which a recordwright of twenty-eight read out loud in about four seconds and which is now the only word anybody in this lane has ever had to learn because of a document.**
11. **AND THE THING ON THE BACK OF A MAN'S HAND, which is the second new paper and is not a paper: a man of thirty-four from a county four hundred miles off, who reads things out to about forty people a week and cannot read the thing he reads out, was asked a second question by a woman of about fifty at a bar in Chapter 290, and wrote down her answer on the back of his own hand in about forty seconds in a hand that is not good, and it is the only thing that man has written down in about eleven years. It is a thing that came up the lane and a thing that is a fact nobody can check, and about twenty people watched him do it, and a recordwright of twenty-eight said out loud that he had not known that was true until somebody said so.**

**AND WHAT IS NOT IN THE MARCH AND WAS NOT IN THE MARCH AND CANNOT BE PRODUCED HERE: a Shelf date.** A Bench order must be dated in the grain year. **There is nothing in any form in the building at Coldharrow-edge that will take sixty-three thousand seven hundred and seventy-four, and there is nothing in any form in a building four hundred miles off that will take it either, and the fourth line of the form says so politely and in writing, and a clerk of forty-four has now confirmed from the other end that nine such entries have been made in nine years by people who were in a room and were asked and answered truthfully.**

### THE PEOPLE, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST AND NONE OF THEM IS RELIEVED

**Ottily Roke, forty-one.** Keeps the bell and the board, twenty-five years, ninepence a day for a hundred and forty days, five pounds five shillings a year. **In this batch she read a sheet of four things out loud standing up in a yard because she is the person who reads things out, without being asked; she said a word over a column is the reason nobody can ask a question about anything else in it, out loud, in a yard, to about nine people, and about four of them understood all of it; she refused a seventh figure in a yard twice, in two counties, and gave the same reason both times, and gave a new one the second time, which is that a man of fifty-two doing a sum out loud in a yard would put the only person he has left who can be asked into a column, and that she would rather there were one; and she said, in her own yard, in front of about nine people, both halves of a sentence nobody in eleven villages had said before, which are that a village that has said a sentence has joined a thing and a village that has not joined a thing cannot be asked to leave it, and then she said that Coldharrow would not say one and would not be the one who said it for anybody, and the reason she gave is that this village is the one with the head in the chalk.** `281`, `282`, `284`, `285`, `287`, `288`, `290`

**Ferrow Hask, thirty-eight.** Nineteen years on the eleven-village lane, one shilling and sixpence a day on a hundred and forty days, ten pounds ten shillings a year. **In this batch he was at the end of the ninth cart on a day when a debt was called in at the bar over a load that came up the lane in a day nobody can name; and he is the one man in eleven villages whose nineteen years of wanting a gate were answered in a yard in Chapter 289 by a man of fifty-six with a barrow, and he did not say the answer, and nobody asked him, and he went back to the ninth cart.** `281`, `285`, `287`, `289`

**Wenna Skell, about sixty.** The only living person who was standing on the strip when it was decided. **In this batch she walked nine miles into a yard to find out that four things were being written down and said in advance that nobody was going to ask her about either of the two things only she can say; she had a woman of fifty-one say her grandmother's name out loud in a yard in Ellerby and cannot un-know that anybody has said it; she said a sentence into the register in her own yard about ninety acres and eleven families, which made eleven families know about ninety acres for the first time in four minutes; and she asked for the sentence to be taken off the sheet, and the rules say nobody may go out, and the recordwright wrote down the asking.** `281`, `282`, `285`, `290`

**Isel Cardew, fifty-one. A person of Trope, not produced, not sent for.** Has rung a handbell at the lane end for about forty years and **has been asked three times who taught her and has not answered once, and in this batch she said in a yard that she is not going to start this month, and that if anybody asks her now she is going to say no and go home.** **She is the person who said in Chapter 282 that she was going to be the one who said a name out loud and not the woman of about sixty, because there is no way to put a boundary and a list side by side with a person of about sixty standing in it that does not end with somebody saying one of those two things out loud, and she had looked for about an hour and there was not another way, and she said she was sorry about it and was not going to be asked about it.** **She refused, in her own yard, to have a man in a doorway four miles down the road say her village's sentence for it in a yard nine miles away, and gave as her reason that a village that has said a sentence has joined a thing and a village that has not joined a thing cannot be asked to leave it, and that she would rather be exact for the rest of her life than be asked what a handbell is for.** **She read out the two-syllable word at the head of a clerk's column four hundred miles off, unasked, because she cannot write and somebody had to, and she said on the way up the lane that she had not known that a word could hold a thing up.** **Her gate bar was shut for four seconds on the last day of the batch because a hen was out, and it was opened again inside four seconds and nobody heard it.** `281`, `282`, `284`, `286`, `287`, `288`, `290`

**Sable Venn. A woman. She was in the fiction before Chapter 251 and is not a production, and the March is her country and she has been in it about thirteen weeks.** **A person of a village, a man who owns a door at Nettlebed and makes hurdles and gates, said in a market yard in Chapter 286, to about nine people, that a thing carried by one woman and about thirty people is the shape of the door in the tower with a different name on it, and he was not asking her to stop and he was not asking for it to be led by somebody else. She agreed before anybody asked, in nine words, and said what changes is not the carrying — what changes is that she now knows what she is doing — and that about four hundred places are going to get a paper from her in the next year from a woman who knows, and that nothing has been taken off her bag, and that she is not going to be relieved and is not going to be thanked.** She is on no part of the register in any capacity and did not ask to be, and nobody thanked her. `286`

**Sera Quill, twenty-eight.** Recordwright, expressly not an advocate, forty days in the country. **In this batch she discovered, out loud and in front of about nine people at the top of a stair of eleven worn steps, that the only reason she has ever been right about a thing in six years is that she has been able to go and look at who wrote it, and that there is no second copy of the four words at the head of a door and has not been one for about four hundred years, and that she is the only copy, and that it is the worst thing she has ever felt and she is not frightened of it and has not slept about it and will not pretend to have.** **She had a thing read back to her by a boy of seventeen, said out loud that a read-back is not a witness and that the check is on her hand and not on her ear, and called it the worst record of her life and did it.** **She wrote down a dispute and could not tell whether she had entered a village or recorded a complaint, and said so out loud in a yard before anybody asked, and gave the reason: the rule tells her not to write who said it and she wrote the rule.** **She wrote down a woman of about sixty asking for a sentence to be taken off a sheet, because the rule does not say she may not.** **She read out a two-syllable word and a nine-word question from a letter four hundred miles off and said that a word at the head of a column in a building four hundred miles off had come into a lane that has never had a building in it.** **She said the nine words three times in Chapters 261 to 270 and not once in Chapters 271 to 280 and not once in Chapters 281 to 290.** Her family claim of six years is unfiled and is not going to be filed and was not mentioned. `281`, `284`, `285`, `286`, `287`, `288`, `290`

**Tarin Keel, fifty-two in the chapters.** **In this batch he found that seven is not a signature, and that the reason is that a stranger is three and a market is four; he read the rules of the register out of a cart's side and wrote the first thing that has ever gone into it; he said what one entry is worth and gave three answers and printed the working and said he did not know which one was true; and he refused, out loud, in front of about nine people, to do a sum in four seconds about the levy on a woman's board, and gave the reason, and the reason is that a figure with a sum under it is a column and the heading on that board is the only reason nobody has ever been able to ask her a second question.** He has still not corrected the hundred and forty days, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110. `281`, `282`, `285`, `286`, `287`, `289`

**Bram Orren, seventeen.** CANON. **In this batch he was not hurried and was not asked anything; he read back a thing off the inside of a door for a recordwright of twenty-eight and said *that is not a witness* before she said it and said there is no word in this country for the difference and there ought to be one, and that he would read a thing back for anybody in that lane at any time they wanted and that reading a thing back is not the same as being a witness. HE WROTE NOTHING INTO HIS BOOK AND SAID HE WOULD RATHER NOT BE ASKED ABOUT THE THIRD PLACE IN IT AT THE SAME TIME AS ANYTHING ELSE.** `284`, `287`

**A man of about thirty-one who owns the tower at Nettlebed and makes hurdles and gates.** **A MAN, and `outline/volume-06.md`'s Chapter 283 card calls him a woman, and the chapter is right and the card is wrong. He is not the man of thirty-one from four hundred miles off and is never to be confused with him.** He has a key on a nail he has never used and a tower he has never been up, and in this batch he left the door on the hasp, and he said the volume's question — that a hundred and forty-one people each said no in their own yard and a thing carried by one woman is the same shape as a room — in a market yard to about nine people, and he said the four words a village needs in a doorway, and he said a rule says what people may do and a claim says what is mine and he has got neither. `284`, `286`, `287`

**A man of fifty-six with a barrow**, who has hurdles and a barrow and who told a clerk of forty-four twice that he does not know what a day is and gave two different reasons the first time, **and who in Chapter 289 walked the whole length of the eleven-village lane in a day and a half and came back with the barrow empty and reported that there is no road at the end of it, and who said four words and then stood by a cart and would not sit down.** `283`, `289`

**Garrick Terrel, sixty-three.** Not in this batch. He was in the yard at the fourth day of the sixty-first week and his trade has not been in a yard since.

**A woman of about fifty at a bar.** Twenty-two years at one gate with a stick and a board under her arm, and **AT THE BAR at the top of it, the only heading in eleven villages.** Nine loads a day at threepence. **In this batch she asked, unprompted, for the one thing she has wanted for about four years — to be asked a second question — and said in a yard to nine people that she would like it noticed; she was asked one, once, in Chapter 290, by a man of thirty-four, about what a load is, and she answered it, and it is the only second question she has had in twenty-two years and it was not about the threepence; she was the reason a man of fifty-two did not do a sum out loud in a yard; and she refused a second question about a stranger's reason for coming up the lane in Chapter 281 and said she was not going to say it and did not.** `281`, `283`, `284`, `289`, `290`

**Petrow Sill, thirty-five.** Lives at the end of a bad road called Stennick. **In this batch he came down the lane on a market morning and said nine words and took his cross off the register: he would rather not be on a list than not be asked, and both of those are true of him, and the second one is truer.** He is not on the register in any capacity now. `286`

**Nell Callow, twenty-nine.** The only press in eleven villages, a wife and two children, paid by the day she sells. **In this batch she refused a man of thirty-one a paper about a thing that went right by accident, and gave two reasons and both were better than the one he offered, and told him that the register on her shelf is the only thing she has that is worth more than her press and that she is telling a stranger about it because he walked eleven miles. She is not letting the shelf and is not charging anybody and has still not been asked what it costs her, and she said so herself in the room and nobody asked her again.** `283`, `287`

**Delya Craven, about forty.** Carries other people's paper between eleven villages for nineteen years and has never once opened any of it. Not in this batch and not asked for.

**A sawyer of about fifty-one.** **NOT IN THIS BATCH. He was the carrier in the Batch 0003 ledger and his seat is under the cart and this volume has now used his fact three times and is not going to use it again.**

**A clerk of forty-four. A man, of the Seat, four hundred miles off, canon in eleven chapters of Volume 05, AND NOT NAMED IN ANY CHAPTER OF VOLUME 06 AND MAY NOT BE NAMED BY ONE.** He left the county on a cart on the second day of the sixty-second week and is four hundred miles off and is not coming back. **He sent a letter, which is the first thing he has produced in this volume, and it is not a form, and it was read out in a yard by two other people, and it contains the only second question anybody has been asked about a route out of the March in forty days, and nobody answered it.** `280`, `288`

**Three men from four hundred miles off.** They came up the lane on the sixth day of the sixty-first week about the door and were told only what a document is. **In this batch one of them went home four hundred miles on a cart on the second day of the sixty-second week and came back up the lane on the third day of it, stood nine yards off a yard for about an hour and a half and did not come into it, and was one of nine strangers through the village on the fifth day of that week, and was in the yard at the fourth day of the sixty-third week with about twenty people in it instead of nine.** `281`, `283`, `288`, `289`, `290`

**Unnamed and standing:** about two hundred and ten people; about nine hundred elms; a miller at Trope whose wheel you can see from about four hundred yards, who took his cross off the register himself in a market yard; a man of about thirty-five with a barrow in Ellerby who came and went twice in a yard in Chapter 282 and offered nothing and asked nothing.

### THE THREE CLOCKS, AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID TO THEM

**The Shelf: fifty-two weeks of seven days, day one the second day of the fortieth week. The sixty-second week is 154 to 160, the sixty-third 161 to 167. Batch 0001 ran 126 to 135, Batch 0002 ran 136 to 145, Batch 0003 ran 146 to 155, this batch ran 156 to 165, and Chapter 291 is 166. Nothing turns the grain year here and no file may say that it turns.**

**The bell: four a day, the first, the change, the noon, the last, and a day begins at the first and ends at the last and nobody in eleven villages has ever said what either is a height above.**

**The board: a count, thirty-one years, and a day's figure is a WEIGHTED number, and from Chapter 282 the list of weights is not a set of signatures, and from Chapter 290 the commonest quantity in the county has no weight at all. A stroke takes about a second and four strokes is a good hour and that is not a metaphor, it is the whole of the labour.**

**The year turn: the first bell after the ewes come down off the fell, and it moves about three weeks and it is the only date a stranger can be taught in a morning.**

### THE MONEY AT THE END OF THE BATCH, EVERY FIGURE WITH ITS WORKING
