### THE TWO THINGS A BATCH 0005 WRITER NEEDS AND CANNOT GET FROM ANY FIGURE ABOVE

1. **THAT THE FOUR DISPUTES DID NOT CONVERGE, and that this is the finding and not a failure, and that the batch says so out loud in a yard twice: the not-fitting is the method, and a man who tells you four villages are going to agree is selling you something and it is not evidence.** One of the four closed, and it closed because the object of it does not exist, and not because anybody won.
2. **THAT A REGISTER WITH A RULE IN IT MAKES A PERSON SAY A THING OUT LOUD THAT SHE WOULD NOT OTHERWISE HAVE SAID, and that this is how the eleven families found out about ninety acres, and that it cost a woman of about sixty the only two facts she has and she is about sixty.** The register is the volume's best instrument and the volume's first broken one, and both of those are the same object, and nobody in it is a villain.

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM

1. **Reading a day's rings out of the same row of `outline/volume-06.md`'s corrected table as the morning figure.** The two columns are not on the same row and the reason is at `state/current.md` §1, and a writer who takes them together will be a day out on every figure in the batch.
2. **The chalk count is FIVE for the whole volume so far and all five are in Batch 0001.** This batch added none. The figure in the chalk is 63,510 and it is the figure on the sheet and it has stood for thirty-nine days.
3. **A clean legality sweep.** Thirty-one day-phrases, zero illegal, and the third question found eleven defects in this batch's own first writing, all of them back-references or own-day phrases on the wrong day of the week.
4. **A forty-character carry-over is connective tissue and a seventy-character one is a defect.** This batch's first writing returned one hundred and ninety-one cross-batch and six hundred and thirty within-batch at seventy, and the delivery returns **ZERO AND ZERO**, and the ten sources and the two hedge-sentence sources are the list a later batch should not have to make again.
5. **The hundred and forty days.** The route-man said out loud in Chapter 257 that it is wrong by about seven in the hundred in a year about every seven years and that he has not corrected it, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110.
6. **A well-formed number.** The largest single defect this batch's first writing contained was a levy printed as *three hundred and seventy-eight pounds* where the figure is fifteen pounds fifteen shillings, and the number was perfectly well formed and every scan in `state/current.md` §2 returned it as clean. **Print the unit price in the sentence, find it, and multiply, and do not run a list of numbers and believe it.**

1. **The Batch 0002 live block two blocks below this one said THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW reads *sixty-three thousand five hundred and forty-two* in two places. The figure is sixty-three thousand six hundred and forty-two. That correction was carried forward by Batch 0003 at the foot of the Batch 0003 block and is carried forward again here, and the figure has moved twice since and is now sixty-three thousand seven hundred and seventy-four.** The blocks below are archive and were not edited by this phase; the live figure is at the head of this block.
2. **The Batch 0002 live block also said that *about thirty-one of the about hundred and forty people in the village are not on* the Ellerby board. It does not add up against a village of a hundred and forty, and the Ellerby board is **two hundred and nine names with no figures, no column, no total and no date**, and about seventy of the two hundred and nine belong to people in four other villages, and there is one in a blue coat that nobody here is for.** The blocks below are archive and were not edited by this phase; the correction is here.

## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0003 — Chapters 271–280, *The Midpoint*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0002` block below it, which is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the thirty-one findings is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0003 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the state of the Briar March at the end of Chapter 280, written to be read by somebody who has read none of the batch.** Every figure was re-measured against the ten chapter files after the prose was finished.

### THE GROUND IN IT AS IT STANDS ON THE SECOND DAY OF THE SIXTY-SECOND WEEK

**Coldharrow: about two hundred and ten people in thirty-eight houses, a tower with a bell in it, about nine hundred elms of which about two hundred are dead, one gate which is a post with a bar across it that is not shut and is not shut for the whole of its life, and a board on the north wall of the tower at chest height where a person in the road can read it.** The lane runs from village to village and not from north to south, through eleven villages: Coldharrow, **Ellerby**, **Trope**, **Nettlebed**, Wrenny, Ashgill, Sallow, Cadgerow, Henthill, Birrow, and one not named. **Ellerby is nine miles up the lane from Coldharrow. Trope is about the fourth village on it and Nettlebed about the sixth, and the two of them are about four miles apart. Coldharrow-edge is eleven miles off the lane, is not in the March, and keeps Shelf weeks.** The ground comes up in eleven places in the first mile and the hedges are the problem and everybody who has walked it knows and nobody says so.

**THE BOARD. A plank about the size of a door, painted and unpainted so many times that the grain has come up in long pale streaks. Thirty-one years in two hands: the older hand is the first six years and it is in the grain, the newer is twenty-five years and it is not in the grain and it is the more careful. No year at the head, no column, no mark against any stroke. THE OPENING FIGURE IS 63,510 AND THE WORKING IS 6 × 1,460 = 8,760 PLUS 25 × 2,190 = 54,750.** It reads **sixty-three thousand six hundred and forty-two** at the second light of the sixth day of the sixtieth week, **sixty-three thousand seven hundred and six** at the second light of the second day of the sixty-second week, **and 63,710 is the first morning of Chapter 281 and belongs to Batch 0004.**

**AND THE THIRD HAND, and this is the whole of what is known about it: it has not changed. A figure in white chalk at the foot, written on the first morning of a man's being in this country, reading sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten, and it has stood there for twenty-nine days and nobody has told him to wipe it and he has not offered, and NOBODY WROTE ON THAT BOARD IN THIS BATCH AT ALL.** The figure in it is also the figure at the foot of a sheet in a press eleven miles off and also the figure in a yard two hundred and ten people know at a glance, and nobody has said whose hand it is in.

**THE BELL. Four a day are the clock — the first, the change, the noon, the last — and about two a day on top of it is everything else, and the complete list is: a burial, one. A boundary walked, seven. A debt called in, two. A market, four. A stranger, three. A fire, two, and the second is rung whether or not it is out. They do not ring for a christening or a marriage.** **AND THE TWENTY-FIVE-YEAR ARITHMETIC: 25 × 2,190 = 54,750, and 25 × 1,460 = 36,500, and 54,750 − 36,500 = 18,250, and that remainder is every burial, boundary, debt, market, stranger and fire in twenty-five years, which is about two a day, and about two a day does not touch most days, and the days it does not touch are the days the board says four on.**

**AND THE BATCH'S FIRST NEW FACT ABOUT THE BELL, which is a positive one and the only positive finding the volume has produced about it: a run of nine consecutive mornings of four, found in the grain by a woman of forty-one because the strokes that are not four are longer, is an INTERVAL. It is nine and not ten because there is a longer stroke either side of it, and it cannot be dated, and nobody in eleven villages can say which year of the board those nine mornings are in. A bell can show that a number of days went past and can show nothing else.**

**NONE OF IT CONVERTS INTO THE SHELF AND THERE IS NO FORMULA AND THERE IS NOT GOING TO BE ONE.** A ring is not a week, a March boundary is dated in rings, a Bench order must be dated in the grain year, and the price of the conversion is £1 3s 6d and fifteen days of the road.

### THE DOCUMENTS THAT EXIST IN THE BRIAR MARCH AS OF CHAPTER 280, ONE EACH, AND WHERE EACH ONE IS

1. **THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW.** Above. **63,706, two hands and a third in chalk, and the third has not been added to in twenty-nine days.**
2. **THE SEALED COPY OF THE FOUR PAGES.** Four lines about a dam, a height, a day and a party to pay, copied and sealed at Sallowby at **threepence**, carried four hundred miles by a sawyer of about fifty-one who was given five shillings and four days and has not read it. **It went out of the county on the eleventh day and came back on the twentieth and has been under a cart seat ever since and will be under a seat on the day it goes.** A copy of it is in the hands of a woman of about sixty at Ellerby who asked a woman with a barrow for it because she wanted to hold a thing that is not a field. A second copy is in the hand of a woman of twenty-nine at a press at Coldharrow-edge, and at the foot of it, on the left, under the last line, in pencil, in a hand that is not any hand in eleven villages, is **sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten**, and there is no day anywhere on the sheet, and nobody in this county has said whose it is.
3. **THE SHEET. Two hundred words in SABLE VENN'S OWN HAND — she could not read it back, she put no day on it on purpose, and she wrote it in a kitchen in Ellerby on the eighteenth day and had it put on a barrow at a gate that is not shut and carried down the lane by a woman of about forty who has never opened another person's paper in nineteen years and has said out loud, unprompted, that she has not read it. It is not entered anywhere, it is not owed to anybody, and it is the first route out of the county that anybody chose.** Its bottom two inches were empty when the barrow went out of the gate and were not empty by the next morning, and the figure in them is the number above.
4. **THE FORM 6 OF THE WATER-SEAT OF SALLOWBY.** One printed sheet, dated in a year this county does not keep, brought up on a barrow in a drawer under a cloth with a list of eggs on it, four days late on a day that was not a market day. It is correct about everything and wrong about the place in three ways, and **it asks for a day, and a name, and it says where the name goes, and its fourth line is a door: where no day can be shown, the acknowledgment may be entered without one, and the entry will be marked as made without a day. NOBODY HAS OPENED IT, NOBODY HAS REFUSED IT, AND NOBODY WAS ASKED, and a woman of about fifty at a bar read that line out loud on the seventh day of the sixtieth week because nobody asked her, and said that she was not going to say one word about what is behind it.** **A woman with a board sent for the form eight weeks before she came and told nobody, and a recordwright of twenty-eight carried a copy of the whole of it eleven miles to a town that keeps weeks she has never learned in Batch 0002 and handed the sentence to a clerk, who wrote it down, and she has not written one word of it down anywhere, because the writing of it down is what the sentence is against.**
5. **BRAM ORREN'S LINE BOOK.** A book of order and not of dates, run since he was twelve, four bells at the top of every page, two pages lost in the ninth year, not copied and not shown. **It contains one question, written on the sixth day of the sixtieth week in a doorway with a mark at the end of it, and nobody has answered it and it has not been asked a second time.** **AND AS OF CHAPTER 280 IT HAS A PROBLEM, in the boy's own words: it has two places in it, what crossed and what did not, and there is a third place on a board in a tower in Nettlebed and he can see it and cannot read it, and the order on that board is the order somebody decided and his book is the order things happened in. HE WROTE NOTHING. He said he will think about it and will not be hurried and he can be asked in about six weeks.**
6. **THE BOARD AT ELLERBY.** A plank of the same kind, and **two hundred and nine names on it and no figures anywhere and no total and no date and no column**, in the order they happened. A woman died and a name went on it. A stranger came through and a name went on it, or the best anybody could do about one. A boundary is walked and the men who walked it go on it. A debt is called in and BOTH names go on it, so that one of them cannot be a name on a wall alone. **Nobody has ever struck a name out. About seventy of the about two hundred and nine are people who live in four other villages and can be asked, because a name is an address. There is a name on it of a man in a blue coat who came through in a previous generation and there is nobody in Ellerby who has ever seen him, and they have left it there because a name with nobody at the end of it is still better than a number.** **THERE IS NO BELL-KEEPER AND NO WAGE IN ELLERBY. The bell is in a frame on two uprights in a field behind the last house and it is rung by whoever is nearest, and if you are not the nearest you fetch the one who is, and it is a custom of about four hundred years that everybody in the village can describe and nobody in the village can date, and the man of about thirty-five with a barrow who describes it has said that he says four hundred because that is what you say.** **AND THE BATCH'S FINDING ABOUT IT, which is arithmetic and is in two chapters: the order the names are in holds the same round of eleven villages that the debts go round in at the Coldharrow bar, and it holds for people who were not in Ellerby when it was written, and it is not the order of the ground.**
7. **THE BOARD IN THE TOWER AT NETTLEBED, AND IT IS THE FIRST OF THE THREE TO BE NAMED IN A RECORD AT ALL.** A plank on the **inside** of the door of a tower about four times the height of a house, with a hasp that is not in its staple, and a key on a nail inside a house at the tower foot that a man of about thirty-one has never once used. **A HUNDRED AND FORTY-ONE NAMES, in no column and no row and in an order that is not the order things happened, about seventy of them in the other ten villages, and one in a blue coat, and nobody struck out.** At the head of it, in a hand **dated at about four hundred years by a clerk of forty-four whose trade is dating hands and who refuses to give a year**, four words: **ANY ONE MAY SAY NO.** Under them, in the same hand, a line about a finger long which the clerk could not get all of in a tower and read out the part he could. **It is not a charter. There is no seal, no signature, no witness line and nothing at the foot of it.** **BESIDE THE NAMES THERE IS A COLUMN WITH NO HEADING OVER IT, with a figure in it against every one of the hundred and forty-one, and the figures are all different, and NOBODY ADDED THEM UP, and a man of fifty-two refused to, out loud, in front of about twenty people, on purpose, because a column with nothing over it is not a total. The last nine figures in it are in the hand of a woman of fifty-one's grandmother and the column stops there, and the last figure anybody put in it is about two hundred years old.** **IT WAS NOT REMOVED BY ANYBODY. It is on the inside of a door in a tower in a village that has no bell, and a bell is the only thing in eleven villages that anybody has ever gone and looked for, and there has been nothing in that tower for about two hundred years.** **WHAT THE COLUMN COUNTS IS NOT KNOWN TO ANYBODY, INCLUDING THE FOUR PEOPLE WHO READ IT.**
8. **THE REGISTER.** Four sheets of paper cut out of the back of a bill with a knife, in the hand of a man of fifty-two, with a second hand on the fourth and a cross on the first in the hand of a woman of fifty-one who cannot write. **FIFTY-ONE PLACES: the eleven on the lane — of which one has still never been named — and five off it (Lythe, Ashcombe, Nether Wray, Pyle, Stennick) and thirty-five more, of which four are marked with a cross meaning a person of that place said the word once to a carter or a miller and has not been asked twice.** It is kept in a book on a shelf in the one room in eleven villages, which is a press at Coldharrow-edge belonging to a woman of twenty-nine who sells days, and she is not letting it and is not charging anybody and has not been asked what it costs her. **The recordwright of twenty-eight keeps it and is not in it. The clerk of forty-four is not in it. The man of thirty-one is not in it and put no name to it. NOT ONE OF THE ELEVEN VILLAGES ON THE LANE IS IN IT AND NONE HAS ASKED TO BE, and that is right and is going to stay right until a village says a sentence in its own yard, and nobody has.** **The rules are eleven lines and they are at `278:37` and `278:45` and the substance of them is: anybody of any country may read all four sheets and nobody may stop them; nobody may go in without a sentence said by a person of that village in that village, and the recordwright writes down what was said and nothing else and does not write who said it; and there is no gate, no toll, no bar, no day, no date, no bench, no seat, no room with a door on it except the press, no vote, no meeting, no officer, and no money in it anywhere.**
9. **THE TALY IN A CARTER'S HEAD.** Ferrow Hask has kept one for nineteen years: what a gate is for. **He asked in four counties and there is no answer, and on the fourth day of the sixty-first week a man of fifty-two told a yard that the two villages he has never had a no out of have together been worth exactly a carter's year every year for about two hundred years, and it is his, and nobody had ever told him, and he said one sentence: *Nineteen years. And I have never once asked what a bar is for.***

**AND WHAT IS NOT IN THE MARCH AND WAS NOT IN THE MARCH AND CANNOT BE PRODUCED HERE: a Shelf date.** A Bench order must be dated in the grain year. **There is nothing in any form in the building at Coldharrow-edge that will take sixty-three thousand seven hundred and six, and there is nothing in any form in a building four hundred miles off that will take it either, and the fourth line of the form says so politely and in writing.**

### THE PEOPLE, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST AND NONE OF THEM IS RELIEVED

**Ottily Roke, forty-one.** Keeps the bell and the board, twenty-five years, ninepence a day for a hundred and forty days, five pounds five shillings a year. **In this batch she worked out, out loud and in about a minute, that there is a run of nine consecutive mornings of four in the grain of her own father's hand, and that it is nine and not ten because there is a longer stroke either side of it, and that she cannot say which year of the board those nine mornings are in, and she has been asked twice about it and cannot say once.** She worked out why a heading on a column is a thing that costs a keeper the right to be asked about anything else, and that she would give eleven shillings for a word at the top of her own board and would not know what to do with it. She refused to read a column of figures out loud in a yard because it would be a woman of forty-one reading out a number and there is no column on her board that says whose hand anything was. She refused to say a hundred and forty-one names out loud in a yard, having been a person in a record for one morning, and said she would rather be a coward about it for the rest of her life. She rang a boundary in her own mind for ten days and put a seventh stroke on a plank for the second one in ten days and said there is nothing on the plank that says they are the same walk. `271`, `273`, `274`, `277`, `278`

**Ferrow Hask, thirty-eight.** Nineteen years on the eleven-village lane, one shilling and sixpence a day on a hundred and forty days, ten pounds ten shillings a year. **He gave up the whole of nineteen years of wanting a road in one sentence, in a yard, in exchange for a road that has a day on it, and it is a different sentence from the one he said in Batch 0002 and he is further along.** He called in a nine-shilling debt at the ninth house. He was told in a yard in front of about nine people that the two villages that have never said no to a bell-founder are worth exactly a carter's year between them, that it is his, and that nobody has ever told him; and he said one sentence about himself and it was the only thing he said in twenty-nine days that was about himself. `275`, `277`, `279`

**Wenna Skell, about sixty.** The only living person who was standing on the strip when it was decided. **In this batch she came nine miles to a yard to say *take it out* and then told the yard that there are now TWO things only she can say — where the boundary started, and that the fourth name from the bottom of a four-hundred-year-old list is her grandmother, which nobody has told her, which she has known for four days, which she has not told a soul, and which she is not going to be asked about — and that she is about sixty and there is nobody coming and she would rather there were not nine people in a lane deciding what four hundred years of a list is for.** She still wants the hedge out, and the hedge is still there. `277`

**Isel Cardew, fifty-one. A person of Trope, not produced, not sent for, and the person the whole second half of this batch was about.** Has rung a handbell at the lane end for about forty years and her wife's mother had one before her, and **she has been asked three times who taught her and has not answered once, and the sentence she gives instead is that there is no why and there has not been one since before she could ask one.** She has never been in the tower at Nettlebed, which is about four miles from her gate, and has said out loud that she has not gone up it in thirty years and would like somebody to say she is not to be blamed for that. She put her thumb on the last nine figures in a column and said they were her grandmother's hand and that the column stops there and that her grandmother never told her what any of it was for and was dead when she was eleven. She said the volume's reversal out loud in an ordinary mouth and in plain words: we did not lose it, nobody took it off us, we were never in a room and there was nothing to be in one. She said that nobody in this lane has been able to refuse one single thing at any time anybody can remember, that what they did was stop saying yes, and that it makes a bill and not a right, and somebody kept the bill, and that is all anybody did. She said the fourth thing in Chapter 277, which is that a thing four hundred years old is not the March's to spend. And in Chapter 278 she took a sole authority off a woman with a board in about eleven words, in her own village, and the woman agreed before she was asked. `272`, `274`, `277`, `278`, `280`

**Sable Venn. A woman. She was in the fiction before Chapter 251 and is not a production, and the March is her country and she has been in it about eleven weeks now.** She said the consequence of her own nine words in her own mouth and did not apologise for it and will not. **She said in Chapter 277 that she has carried about four hundred pieces of paper in nine years and has never once asked a single person what they wanted, and that not asking has worked four hundred times, and that the eleven villages have been deciding this for four hundred years without a road and a bell and a bench and are not waiting, and that she is not going to start asking now, and that she would like it written down by a woman of forty-one.** **She is on no part of the register in any capacity and did not ask to be, and a sentence said by a person of a village in a village takes sole authority off her, and she agreed with it before she was asked, and she said she is not relieved.** `277`, `278`

**Sera Quill, twenty-eight.** Recordwright, expressly not an advocate, thirty days in the country. **She walked eleven miles to a town that keeps weeks she has never learned and handed a clerk her own sentence, and did not write one word of it down, and says the writing of it down is what the sentence is against.** She read the four words at the head of a four-hundred-year-old plank three times in one morning and got three readings, all of them right, and said that there was never a fixed one and that a recordwright's whole life is the difference between what a hand says and what a hand has been taken to mean, and that she cannot get this difference and is not frightened of that. She said out loud, before being asked, that she has read a document no bench in this country has ever seen and cannot tell anybody in a room what it is for, that this is the fourth time in her life she has read something she could not account for and the first time she has wanted it, and that a recordwright who puts a use to a thing she cannot account for is the exact thing that happened to the four lines about a river. **She keeps the register and is not in it, and said in a yard that a recordwright who keeps a thing and is in it has a thumb on the scale. SHE SAID THE NINE WORDS THREE TIMES IN CHAPTERS 261 TO 270 AND NOT ONCE IN CHAPTERS 271 TO 280.** Her family claim of six years is unfiled and is not going to be filed and was not mentioned again. `271`, `274`, `276`, `278`

**Tarin Keel, fifty-two in the chapters.** **He printed the batch's money twice and refused to print it a third time, out loud, in front of about twenty people, and gave the reason: a column with nothing over it is not a total, and adding it gives a number that is wrong about the world, and there is one of those about once a week in this county.** He found the run of nines by sending a woman of forty-one into the grain for it. He brought three instruments of the round to a cart's side in a yard and said they do not know about each other. He has still not corrected the hundred and forty days, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110. `271`, `273`, `274`, `275`, `277`, `278`, `279`

**Bram Orren, seventeen.** CANON. **He tried to read a board of a hundred and forty-one names with his line book and could not, and the finding is about his book and not about the board: a book that can order things and cannot date them cannot read a list that was put in order by a decision, and the finest thing such a book can say about that wall is that one of these came before that one, and there are four hundred years between them and not one of them is a day he can name, and he has had five years.** He wrote nothing. He said he would think about it and would not be hurried and he can be asked about it in about six weeks, and that is a proper answer. He went and did his day. `279`, `280`

**Garrick Terrel, sixty-three.** The bell-founder's agent, twice a year, and he was in this county five days out of the two he gets. **He has quoted six pounds fifteen three times in eleven years and has a list in his head of about thirty villages and eleven on this lane and nine of the eleven have a bell. He has never once in eleven years had a village say no to him and he said out loud in a yard that he has wanted one to for about six of those years, and that he is the likeliest of anybody to be the one confused by the answer, and that he is going to quote six pounds fifteen in the spring and is not going to quote it lower, and that is not stubbornness.** He is not a villain and he is not redeemed and he is not thanked and he did not work the price out and nobody made him. `272`, `275`

**A clerk of forty-four. A man, of the Seat, four hundred miles off, canon in eleven chapters of Volume 05, and NOT NAMED IN THIS BATCH AND NOT NAMED IN THOSE ELEVEN, and no chapter of Volume 06 may name him.** He came up the lane on his own account, having walked in at the south-west end of the county, following a copy that left a district eight weeks ago and no acknowledgment; **his whole trade is watching what a document does when it leaves the place it was measured in, and nobody had ever gone after one.** He is not a Bench, not an office, not a body, not a party and not a stage, he brought nobody and no authority, he cannot compel a person, and he cannot spend a good name again because he spent it five weeks ago in a valley in front of about nine people. He dated a hand at about four hundred years and refused a year. He read out the half of a line under the four words that he could get and would not repeat it in a lane. **He said the volume's midpoint as a document-reader: he has never once in twenty-two years found a document that admitted to having taken something, and not one of them has ever said this was here and this is what it cost, and that is because they were written by people who did not know they were in a room, because there was no room.** He is walking down the lane to a town that keeps weeks he has never learned and will be four hundred miles off in about ten days. `273`, `274`, `278`, `280`

**A woman of about fifty at the bar.** Twenty-two years at one gate with a stick and a board under her arm, and **AT THE BAR at the top of it, which is the only heading in eleven villages and the reason nobody has ever been able to ask her a question.** Nine loads a market day at threepence. She has said no to a date about nine hundred times and has never once said the word no to anything else. **She is the fourth person in this county to read the Form 6 and the only one who was not asked, and she read the fourth line out loud word for word, said that whoever built it in was being fair, said that being fair and being useful are two different things, and said she is not opening the door because she cannot get to a press before the noon bell and a day is a pound three shillings and sixpence and she has not got that in a bag this morning and neither has anybody else in this village.** `271`, `276`, `278`

**Petrow Sill, thirty-five.** Lives at the end of a bad road called Stennick, nine farms and a well and a roof that fell in and about eleven people in his house, and walks his lane about forty times in nine years. **He put a lane that is on nobody's list on a cart's side and asked for the two lanes to be put side by side, having heard a man of thirty-one say out loud what the price is.** He is the second lane in the network and the person at one end of it. `278`, `279`

**Nell Callow, twenty-nine.** The only press in eleven villages, at Coldharrow-edge, about forty dates a year, forty-seven pounds, a wife and two children. **In this batch she is not on a stage and not in a scene and she is doing the most load-bearing thing she does, which is holding the only room in eleven villages on a shelf for a book with fifty-one places on it, and she is not letting it and is not charging anybody and has not been asked what it costs her and nobody asked her in this batch either.** `278`

**A man of about thirty-one with a hurdle in his hand** and a key on a nail inside his own house, who has never been up his own tower, and who opened the door because a woman of forty-one had told the stranger he could not ask anybody else, and who asked for that to be on whatever anybody is keeping. `273`

**Three men from four hundred miles off** who came up the lane in the sixth day of the sixty-first week about the door. **One of them is a man of thirty-four who reads things out to about forty people a week and cannot read the thing he reads out, which is canon from Volume 05.** They were told only that a document is a thing that can be produced, and the man of thirty-four said *if it cannot be produced then it has not happened, and I have been saying a version of that out loud for eleven years and I did not know that there was a door in this country with the opposite of it written on it*, and neither of them is a villain and neither is an agent of anything and nobody sent for them. `277`

**Unnamed and standing:** about two hundred and ten people; about nine hundred elms; Delya Craven, about forty, who carries other people's paper between eleven villages for nineteen years and has never once opened any of it and said so out loud in this batch's predecessor; a man of fifty-six with hurdles at Trope who told the clerk twice that he does not know what a day is; a miller at Trope whose wheel you can see from four hundred yards; a woman of about nine years' standing who has never read another person's paper in nineteen years.

**A sawyer of about fifty-one** who is a person of the Red Basin and not of the March, who has bought paper in four counties for nine years, and who has had a satchel under the seat of a timber cart since the third day of the fifty-eighth week and again since the eleventh day and will have it on the day it goes. He is one of the six people in a room in Sallowby who will not remember that a woman with a board stood in it.

### THE THREE CLOCKS, AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID TO THEM

**The Shelf: fifty-two weeks of seven days, day one the second day of the fortieth week. The sixtieth week is 140 to 146, the sixty-first 147 to 153, the sixty-second 154 to 160. Batch 0001 ran 126 to 135, Batch 0002 ran 136 to 145, this batch ran 146 to 155, and Chapter 281 is 156. Nothing turns the grain year here and no file may say that it turns.**

**The bell: four a day, the first, the change, the noon, the last, and a day begins at the first and ends at the last and nobody in eleven villages has ever said what either is a height above.**

**The board: a count, thirty-one years, and a day's figure is a weighted number. A stroke takes about a second and four strokes is a good hour and that is not a metaphor, it is the whole of the labour.**

**The year turn: the first bell after the ewes come down off the fell, and it moves about three weeks and it is the only date a stranger can be taught in a morning.**

### THE MONEY AT THE END OF THE BATCH, EVERY FIGURE WITH ITS WORKING

| Figure | The working, and it is the whole of it |
|---|---|
| **A keeper's year = 1,260d = £5 5s** | 9 × 140 = **1,260d** ✓ |
| **A carter's year = 2,520d = £10 10s, and it is the whole of the margin of the two villages that have no bell** | 18 × 140 = **2,520d** ✓; and 11 × 1,260 = 13,860 less 9 × 1,260 = 11,340 = **2,520d** ✓ |
| **THE BATCH'S ONE NEW DERIVATION: two hundred years of a keeper's wage is 252,000d, which is a thousand and fifty pounds exactly, and that is a hundred and fifty-five bells at £6 15s and three pounds fifteen shillings** | 200 × 1,260 = **252,000d**; 252,000 ÷ 240 = **1,050** ✓; 155 × 1,620 = 251,100; 252,000 − 251,100 = **900d = £3 15s** ✓ |
| A bell, hung, with rope = £6 15s = 1,620d, **not re-derived in this batch** | 31 × 48d = 1,488; + 132 = **1,620d** ✓ |
| A levy at a bar = 3d a load, nine loads on a market day = **27d, said aloud in a yard** | 9 × 3 = **27d** ✓ |
| A Bench date = £1 3s 6d = **fifteen days of the man who walks the road and one shilling**, and it is printed zero times with its working in this batch | 36 + 6 + 240 = **282d**; 282 ÷ 18 = 15 rem 12 ✓ |
| Nell Callow's year = £47 exactly | 40 × 282 = **11,280d** ✓ |
| The board's opening figure, and the split is the father's six and the woman's twenty-five | 6 × 1,460 = 8,760; 25 × 2,190 = 54,750; total **63,510** ✓ |
| **The twenty-five-year arithmetic that gives four its meaning** | 25 × 2,190 = **54,750**; 25 × 1,460 = **36,500**; difference **18,250** ✓ |
| **And the two hundred, which is the volume's whole argument in one subtraction and is not a design** | 63,710 − 63,510 = **200**; 200 ÷ 4 = **fifty days of the four bells** ✓ |

### THE TWO THINGS A BATCH 0004 WRITER NEEDS AND CANNOT GET FROM ANY FIGURE ABOVE

1. **THE COLUMN HAS NOT BEEN ADDED UP AND IS NOT GOING TO BE BY ANYBODY, and the reason is a man of fifty-two's and not an omission: a column with nothing over it is not a total, and adding it produces a number that is wrong about the world.** There are a hundred and forty-one figures in it, all different, and the last nine are in a hand a woman of fifty-one can date at about two hundred years, and **nobody in two hundred and ten people knows what any of the figures counts, and neither does a clerk of forty-four who has handled about four thousand documents, and a man of thirty-one could guess in about four seconds and is not going to.** The panel at `276:19` put the undecided question on the page in the language of a Veyran clerk and it is the second of the volume's three and it decides nothing.
2. **THE ONE THING A PERSON OF A VILLAGE KNOWS AND A MAN OF A ROOM CANNOT LEARN IN THIS BATCH: that the two villages did not lose a bell, they have been refusing one, and the last man in a village who wanted one went without about thirty years before Garrick Terrel was born, and the cause ran the other way, and about four hundred years ago a hundred and forty-one people said no out loud on their own ground and kept a list and paid for it with a bell each, and nobody has been able to prove any of it and nobody can now, and it is a bill and not a right, and a woman of fifty-one said all of that in about four sentences in a tower in a village of about ninety people and nobody was asked her and nobody thanked her.**

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM

1. **Going looking for a formula.** There is none, and there is not going to be one, and the check is the board chain.
2. **Reading the board's opening figure out of a block** instead of computing 6 × 1,460 + 25 × 2,190 = 63,510.
3. **The chalk count is FIVE for the whole volume so far and all five are in Batch 0001.** This batch added none. The figure in the chalk is 63,510 and it is the figure on the sheet and it has stood for twenty-nine days.
4. **A clean legality sweep.** Thirty-three day-phrases, zero illegal, and the third question found nine defects in this batch's own first writing, all of them back-references on the wrong day of the week. **AND THE REVIEW OF BATCH 0003 FOUND A TENTH IN THE DELIVERY ITSELF, at `276:58`, which every sweep in this volume had certified: a bar of twenty-two years' standing put a timber-cart morning on the fourth day of the FIFTY-NINTH week, and it is the fourth day of the SIXTIETH, which is `268:3`, its thirteen and its nine strangers. The fourth day of the fifty-ninth week is `261:3`, where the board went up five and the cart LEFT. Corrected, and `277:47`'s sixth day of the sixtieth week was checked against the same rule and is CORRECT, because the sixth day of the sixtieth week is `270`, and a check that comes back clean is worth printing as well as a check that does not.**
5. **A forty-character carry-over is connective tissue and a seventy-character one is a defect.** This batch's first writing returned three hundred and seven shared seventy-character runs against Chapters 241 to 270 and the delivery returns zero, and the rewordings are the list a later batch should not have to make again. **AND THE REVIEW OF BATCH 0003 FOUND FORTY MORE IN THE DELIVERY THAT THIS BLOCK'S OWN FIGURE OF ZERO HAD HIDDEN, all of them from two sources, `271:117` ← `270:47` and `274:47` ← `270:65`, and both are now reworded and the scan returns zero over all thirty files of the range, WHICH IS THIRTY FILES AND NOT THE TWENTY-NINE THAT THIS BLOCK, THE BATCH SUMMARY AND THE BATCH 0004 HAND-OFF ALL CALLED IT.**
6. **The hundred and forty days.** The route-man says out loud in Chapter 257 that it is wrong by about seven in the hundred in a year about every seven years and that he has not corrected it, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110.

### AND TWO THINGS THIS BLOCK FIXED IN THE BLOCK BELOW IT, WHICH IS ARCHIVE AND WAS NOT EDITED, AND WHICH ARE RECORDED HERE BECAUSE THEY WERE WRONG

1. **The Batch 0002 live block below this one said THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW reads *sixty-three thousand five hundred and forty-two* in two places, at its own item 1 and in its closing paragraph. The figure is sixty-three thousand six hundred and forty-two. The repair of 27 September 2026 removed that from `266:31`, from `266:97` and from two state files and it survived in this one.** It is a sentence that does not add up against a village of a hundred and forty, and the eleven's ten names are what they always were: about seventy of the two hundred and nine belong to people in four other villages, and there is one in a blue coat that nobody is here for. **The block below is archive and was not edited by this phase; the correction is here.**

## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0002 — Chapters 261–270, *The Price Of A Date*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0001` block below it, which is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the findings is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0002 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the state of the Briar March at the end of Chapter 270, written to be read by somebody who has read none of the batch.** Every figure was re-measured against the ten chapter files on the day it was written.

### THE GROUND IN IT AS IT STANDS ON THE SIXTH DAY OF THE SIXTIETH WEEK

**Coldharrow: about two hundred and ten people in thirty-eight houses, a tower with a bell in it, about nine hundred elms of which about two hundred are dead, one gate which is a post with a bar across it that is not shut and is not shut for the whole of its life, and a board on the north wall of the tower at chest height where a person in the road can read it.** The lane runs from village to village and not from north to south, through eleven villages: Coldharrow, **Ellerby**, Trope, Wrenny, Ashgill, Sallow, Nettlebed, Cadgerow, Henthill, Birrow, and one not named. **Ellerby is nine miles up the lane from Coldharrow. Coldharrow-edge is eleven miles off it and is not in the March and keeps Shelf weeks.** The ground comes up in eleven places in the first mile and the hedges are the problem and everybody who has walked it knows and nobody says so.

**THE BOARD. A plank about the size of a door, painted and unpainted so many times that the grain has come up in long pale streaks. Thirty-one years in two hands: the older hand is the first six years and it is in the grain, the newer is twenty-five years and it is not in the grain and it is the more careful. No year at the head, no column, no mark against any stroke. THE OPENING FIGURE IS 63,510 AND THE WORKING IS 6 × 1,460 = 8,760 PLUS 25 × 2,190 = 54,750.** It reads **sixty-three thousand five hundred and forty-two** at the second light of the sixth day of the sixtieth week, and **63,647 is the first morning of Chapter 271 and belongs to Batch 0003.**

**AND THE THIRD HAND, and this is the whole of what changed about it in this batch: it has not changed. A figure in white chalk at the foot, written on the first morning of a man's being in this country, reading sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten, and it has stood there for nineteen days and nobody has told him to wipe it and he has not offered, and NOBODY WROTE ON THAT BOARD IN THIS BATCH AT ALL.** The third hand is the only thing in this country a stranger has written on, and the figure in it has now been inside a piece of paper in two counties.

**THE BELL. Four a day are the clock — the first, the change, the noon, the last — and about two a day on top of it is everything else, and the complete list is: a burial, one. A boundary walked, seven. A debt called in, two. A market, four. A stranger, three. A fire, two, and the second is rung whether or not it is out. They do not ring for a christening or a marriage.** **AND THE TWENTY-FIVE-YEAR ARITHMETIC, new in this batch and the reason a day of four is not a rare day: 25 × 2,190 = 54,750, and 25 × 1,460 = 36,500, and 54,750 − 36,500 = 18,250, and that remainder is every burial, boundary, debt, market, stranger and fire in twenty-five years, which is about two a day, and about two a day does not touch most days, and the days it does not touch are the days the board says four on.**

**NONE OF IT CONVERTS INTO THE SHELF AND THERE IS NO FORMULA AND THERE IS NOT GOING TO BE ONE.** A ring is not a week, a March boundary is dated in rings, a Bench order must be dated in the grain year, and the price of the conversion is £1 3s 6d and fifteen days of the road.

### THE DOCUMENTS THAT EXIST IN THE BRIAR MARCH AS OF CHAPTER 270, ONE EACH, AND WHERE EACH ONE IS

1. **THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW.** Above. **63,642, two hands and a third in chalk, and the third has not been added to since the first morning.**
2. **THE SEALED COPY OF THE FOUR PAGES.** Four lines about a dam, a height, a day and a party to pay, copied and sealed at Sallowby at **threepence**, carried four hundred miles by a sawyer of about fifty-one who was given five shillings and four days and has not read it. **IT LEFT THE COUNTY ON THE ELEVENTH DAY AND CAME BACK ON THE TWENTIETH, and a woman of twenty-nine at a press at Coldharrow-edge copied it out in her own hand on the eleventh day because she copies for a living and a piece of paper that has been in a country for an hour is worth nothing. A copy of that copy is in Ellerby in the hands of a woman of about sixty who asked a carrier for it because she wanted to hold a thing that is not a field. The original is under the seat of a timber cart again and will be under that seat on the day it goes.**
3. **THE SHEET. Two hundred words in SABLE VENN'S OWN HAND — the recordwright's hand is wrong and is corrected, and she was in the Coldharrow yard and not in the kitchen — with the four lines in the middle of it and no name at the foot of it and no day on it, written in a kitchen in Ellerby, put on a barrow at a gate that is not shut, and carried down the lane by a woman of about forty who has never opened another person's paper in nineteen years and who has said out loud, unprompted, twice in two days, that she has not read it. It is not entered anywhere. It is not owed to anybody. It is the first route out of the county that anybody chose.** And at the foot of it, on the left, under the last line, in pencil, in a hand that is not any hand in eleven villages, is **sixty-three thousand five hundred and ten**, and there is no day anywhere on the sheet.
4. **BRAM ORREN'S LINE BOOK.** A book of order and not of dates, run since he was twelve, four bells at the top of every page, two pages lost in the ninth year, **not copied and not shown, because a boy of seventeen refused a man of thirty-one and was right.** **AND AS OF CHAPTER 270 IT CONTAINS ONE QUESTION, the first thing in it in five years that is not a thing that crossed, and it has a mark at the end of it, and nobody answered it.**
5. **THE BOARD AT ELLERBY.** A plank of the same kind, and **two hundred and nine names on it and no figures anywhere and no total and no date and no column**, in the order they happened. A woman died and a name went on it. A stranger came through and a name went on it, or the best anybody could do about one. A boundary was walked and the men who walked it went on it. A debt is called in and BOTH names go on it, so that one of them cannot be a name on a wall alone. **Nobody has ever struck a name out. About thirty-one of the about hundred and forty people in the village are not on it. There is a name on it of a man in a blue coat who came through in a previous generation and there is nobody in Ellerby who has ever seen him, and they have left it there because a name with nobody at the end of it is still better than a number.** **THERE IS NO BELL-KEEPER AND NO WAGE IN ELLERBY. The bell is in a frame on two uprights in a field behind the last house and it is rung by whoever is nearest, and if you are not the nearest you fetch the one who is, and it is a custom of about four hundred years that everybody in the village can describe and nobody in the village can date.**
6. **FORM 6 OF THE WATER-SEAT OF SALLOWBY, ACKNOWLEDGMENT OF A COPY CARRIED OUT OF THE DISTRICT. The delivered batch and four state files spelled it SALLYBY; `259:33` says Sallowby and the chapter is the canon.** One printed sheet, dated in a year this county does not keep, brought up on a barrow in a drawer under a cloth with a list of eggs on it, four days late on a day that was not a market day. It is correct about everything and wrong about the place in three ways, and it asks for a day in the grain year and the name of one person present at a reading, and **it says the acknowledgment will be entered in the district named in the copy, which is four hundred miles off, and it contains a fourth line that is a door: where no day can be shown, the acknowledgment may be entered without one, and the entry will be marked as made without a day.** **A woman with a board sent for it eight weeks before she came and told nobody.**
7. **THE TALLY IN A CARTER'S HEAD.** Ferrow Hask has kept one for nineteen years: what a gate is for. He has asked in four counties and there is no answer.

**AND WHAT IS NOT IN THE MARCH AND WAS NOT IN THE MARCH AND CANNOT BE PRODUCED HERE: a Shelf date.** A Bench order must be dated in the grain year. **There is nothing in any form in the building at Coldharrow-edge that will take sixty-three thousand five hundred and forty-two, and there is nothing in any form in a building four hundred miles off that will take it either, and the fourth line of the form says so politely and in writing.**

### THE PEOPLE, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST AND NONE OF THEM IS RELIEVED

**Ottily Roke, forty-one.** Keeps the bell and the board, twenty-five years, ninepence a day for a hundred and forty days, five pounds five shillings a year. **In this batch she worked out, out loud and in about nine minutes, that the board has gone up fifty-four thousand seven hundred and fifty times in twenty-five years and that thirty-six thousand five hundred of those were the four bells and the other eighteen thousand two hundred and fifty are everything else, and that therefore a day of four is the ordinary day and not a remarkable one. She said the sentence that turns the batch: a number that anybody can move is a number that has to be asked about, and there is exactly one person in this county you can be asked, and for twenty-five years that was a comfort and it is a door. She said the sentence that is the first half of what the batch costs: she did not know until about a minute ago that being the only one who can be asked is a thing that can be spent, and she has not told anybody, and she would like it written down somewhere that is not a wall. She said the last sentence of the batch's climax: a number on a wall is a thing about a village and a number on a piece of paper is a thing about a person, and she has been a person in a record for one morning and did not ask to be one and is not going to be able to undo it and is not going to be told she is sorry for it. She is right about the board every single time and she is not relieved and nobody has promised her anything past the hundred and fortieth day.** `261`, `262`, `264`, `265`, `266`, `267`, `270`

**Ferrow Hask, thirty-eight.** Nineteen years on the eleven-village lane, one shilling and sixpence a day on a hundred and forty days, ten pounds ten shillings a year. **He said the volume's central pressure out loud in one word — use it — and gave the whole of his reason, and worked out in a yard that a road wants four dates a year and four dates is the woman's year less eleven shillings, and nobody in that yard had ever said it. He asked a woman of twenty-nine for a date for the lane, and was told: tell me the day it was laid out; he said it was always there; she said: then I cannot help you. He bought a date for the day a piece of paper was read out in a yard instead, which is no use to him whatever, and stood in a yard and said he would stand there and listen to what anybody had to say about it.** He is the honest opponent of the whole volume and not a villain. `263`, `265`, and Chapter 260's unanswered question

**Wenna Skell, about sixty.** **She has a name and it was said out loud in a lane on the sixth day of the fifty-ninth week for the first time in nine years.** She was twenty-one and somebody's aunt the day the boundary was walked; she can say where it started and she does not know what any of the days are called. **She wants the hedge taken out and the two elms taken out and the line taken out, and the reason is her own life and not the record: being the only one who can say where it started is not an honour, it is a job nobody gave her and nobody can take off her and she is about sixty. She was told by a man of thirty-one that he had not made her feel better about it and that she would remember that. She asked a woman with a barrow at a gate for a piece of paper because she wanted to hold a thing that is not a field, and was given a copy of four lines about a river four hundred miles off, and read them, and said: there is no river in this county, and these four lines are worth nothing in this village because nobody in this village was standing near enough to see them. She read the four lines aloud in a doorway and in a kitchen because she was asked to, twice, by a woman of nine years' standing who does not read other people's records.** She is not relieved, not redeemed and not thanked. `263`, `265`, `266`, `268`

**Sable Venn. A woman. She was in the fiction before Chapter 251 and is not a production, and the March is her country and she has been in it about ten weeks now.** **She sent for the form eight weeks before she came and told nobody. She worked beside the man of thirty-one for the first time in Chapter 268 and it went badly, and the badness is that the method works. She said the consequence of her own nine words in her own mouth and did not apologise for it: you have made a woman of sixty read one out, and she is not a document, and I know the difference, and it has taken me nine years and a copy to learn and it has taken you eighteen days, and it has cost her nothing because nobody asked her either, and I was one of the people who did not ask her. And then the thing underneath: it worked four hundred times because of what she would not know and not because of what she could not do, and there is a difference, and a MAN OF THIRTY-ONE — the delivery said twenty-eight, which is the recordwright's age — asked her what happens to a piece of paper and she could not tell him, and he said *and yet*.** **She is not thanked, she is on no paper, she did not ask to be, and nobody in the March knows what she is except about four people.** `264`, `268`, `269`

**Sera Quill, twenty-eight.** Recordwright, expressly not an advocate, twenty days in the country. **She said the nine words a third time in Chapter 270 and it came out word for word as it had twice before, and she said that was the whole of the point of it, and she is going to carry the sentence to a town that keeps weeks she has never learned and hand it to a clerk and would like him to have a bad afternoon.** **She wrote the two hundred words in Chapter 268 and put no day on them on purpose, and said that the not putting of it is the only protection anybody in that kitchen has got, and that it is hers, and that it is the first useful thing she has done in eighteen days.** **Her family claim of six years is unfiled and is not going to be filed and is not mentioned again in this batch.** The split is not resolved and is not resolved by being agreed. `263`, `266`, `268`, `270`

**Tarin Keel, fifty-two in the chapters.** **He printed two dates on the side of a press bench in about four minutes and it is the batch's money. He refused his own test: he said on the first day of that week that he would come back and put a day of four in chalk, and on the fifth day of that week he said he was not going to, because the only way to keep a thing in this country is to write it on somebody else's board and he is not the fifth hand on a board in a village he has been in eleven days. He went and asked a man of about forty at a door what day it was, in about nine seconds, and got the same answer four men from counties with roads in it have got out of the woman of forty-one in twenty-five years. In Chapter 270 he printed a hundred and thirty-two on a cart's side, which is the whole of nineteen days' rings, and read out the nineteen, and then said that the last of them was a day of four, and four is what the board says about most days in a year, so the one day nearest the sheet is the one day you could not find with a lamp and a boy.** `262`, `263`, `270`

**Bram Orren, seventeen.** CANON. Rings what he crosses and says the March's rule before anybody can accuse him. **In this batch he said the thing nobody in a yard had thought of, and said it standing up, and then said that it does not help and that he wanted that said before anybody uses it: he has run a line since he was twelve, and in five years he has never once crossed the strip, and if it is a boundary then it is a boundary that nothing has ever gone over. And at the end of the batch he asked the volume's question a second time, in a doorway, to a person, and wrote it in a book that has never had a question in it because a question is not a thing that crossed, and it had a mark on the end of it. Nobody answered him and the man of thirty-one said I do not know and the boy said that is two of us.** `265`, `270`

**Nell Callow, twenty-nine.** The only press in eleven villages, at Coldharrow-edge, about forty dates a year, forty-seven pounds. **In this batch she charged a pound three shillings and sixpence for a date and did not ask a question, and she copied the four lines out in her own hand because a copy that has been in a country for an hour is worth nothing, and she said that a copy has to be marked with the number and the day of the thing it is a copy of or it is not a copy, it is a second thing, and a second thing is a lie about there being two. And she said: put the number or do not pay me. And he put the number, and it was the wrong one, and nobody asked him whether it was a number of anything, because in this county nobody has ever asked anybody that, and the only question anybody asks of a figure is whose hand it is in.** `263`, `269`

**Delya Craven, about forty.** Has run letters and small things up and down the eleven villages for nineteen years, makes her living carrying other people's paper, and **has never once opened any of it. She said so out loud, unprompted, in two different yards on two different days, and she did it the first time so that nobody could say later that she had.** `263`, `265`, `268`, `270`

**Marda Skell, about thirty-five.** Thirty-five years in this county and a knee that will not get up a ladder, a gate bar that has never been shut and that she is not going to start shutting, and hens. She rang once on the fourth day of the fifty-ninth week and would ring again and cannot get up the ladder herself. `261`

**A man of about thirty-five with a barrow in Ellerby** who told a stranger that the custom of ringing by whoever is nearest is about four hundred years old and asked to have it noticed that he said it because it is what you say. `266`, `267`

**A man of about fifty in a shed** who put a hand on a rope on the fourth day of the fifty-ninth week, does not know why, would do it again tomorrow, and has not worked out anything. `261`

**A man of about thirty-one with a grey coat, a handcart, a dog and a stick** who came the other way on the third day of the sixtieth week, said his name when the woman at the bar asked him to, and was written on a wall in Ellerby without anybody asking. `267`

**Unnamed and standing:** a woman of about fifty at the gate with a stick and a board; about nine people who know the third hand at a glance; a man of about forty at the first house in the lane who does not know what day it is; a man of about forty-two who counted carts on roads for twenty-two years; a man of about sixty in a yard eleven miles off who teaches boys the March's rule; a man of about sixty-one who puts marks on elms; a woman of about sixty-three who kept a hen out of a barley bed and said nothing about it; about nine hundred elms; and about two hundred and ten people.

**A sawyer of about fifty-one** who is a person of the Red Basin and not of the March, who has bought paper in four counties for nine years, and who has had a satchel under the seat of a timber cart since the third day of the fifty-eighth week. He is one of the six people in a room in Sallowby who will not remember that a woman with a board stood in it.

### THE THREE CLOCKS, AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID TO THEM

**The Shelf: fifty-two weeks of seven days, day one the second day of the fortieth week. The sixtieth week is 140 to 146. Batch 0001 ran 126 to 135 and this batch ran 136 to 145. Chapter 271 is day 146. Nothing turns the grain year here and no file may say that it turns.**

**The bell: four a day, the first, the change, the noon, the last, and a day begins at the first and ends at the last and nobody in eleven villages has ever said what either is a height above.**

**The board: a count, thirty-one years, and a day's figure is a weighted number. A stroke takes about a second and four strokes is a good hour and that is not a metaphor, it is the whole of the labour.**

**The year turn: the first bell after the ewes come down off the fell, and it moves about three weeks and it is the only date a stranger can be taught in a morning.**

### THE MONEY AT THE END OF THE BATCH, EVERY FIGURE WITH ITS WORKING

| Figure | The working, and it is the whole of it |
|---|---|
| **A carter's day = 18d, on 140 days = £10 10s** | 18 × 140 = **2,520d** ✓ |
| **The levy = 3d a load, at 9 loads a day, on 140 days = £15 15s a village** | 140 × 9 × 3 = **3,780d** ✓ |
| **And the excess of the levy over the walking = £5 5s, and the keeper's wage at 9d × 140 = £5 5s, TO THE PENNY** | 3,780 − 2,520 = **1,260d**; 140 × 9 = **1,260d** ✓ |
| **And the whole of the margin across eleven villages = £57 15s** | 11 × 1,260 = **13,860d** ✓ |
| **A bell, hung, with rope = £6 15s; and a bell is £1 10s more than a year of the woman** | 31 × 48d = 1,488d; + 132d = **1,620d**; 1,620 − 1,260 = **360d = £1 10s** ✓ |
| **A Bench date = £1 3s 6d = fifteen days of the man who walks the road and one shilling** | 36 + 6 + 240 = **282d**; 282 ÷ 18 = 15 rem 12 ✓ |
| **TWO DATES, bought twice in this batch, and the only new figure in it: £2 7s exactly; thirty-one days and sixpence; a year of the woman who keeps the count less £2 18s** | 2 × 282 = **564d**; 564 − 480 = 84d = 7s ✓; 31 × 18 = 558, 564 − 558 = 6d ✓; 1,260 − 564 = **696d**; 696 − 480 = 216d = 18s ✓ |
| **Four dates = her year less eleven shillings**, which is the price of using the lane | 4 × 282 = 1,128d; 1,260 − 1,128 = **132d = 11s** ✓ |
| **Nell Callow's year = £47 exactly** | 40 × 282 = **11,280d** ✓ |
| **The board's opening figure, and the split is the father's six and the woman's twenty-five** | 6 × 1,460 = 8,760; 25 × 2,190 = 54,750; total **63,510** ✓ |
| **AND THE TWENTY-FIVE-YEAR ARITHMETIC THAT GIVES FOUR ITS MEANING, new in this batch** | 25 × 2,190 = **54,750**; 25 × 1,460 = **36,500**; difference **18,250** ✓ |
| **And the figure on the sheet against the figure on the board: 132, which is nineteen days' rings** | 69 + 63 = **132**; 63,510 + 132 = **63,642** ✓ |
| **The four pages, copied and sealed at Sallowby = threepence; and a copy of that copy, made at a press in Coldharrow-edge in a woman's own hand, also threepence of paper** | CANON for the penny, and it is the cheapest thing in the volume |

### THE TWO THINGS A BATCH 0003 WRITER NEEDS AND CANNOT GET FROM ANY FIGURE ABOVE

1. **THE QUESTION IS NOW ASKED TWICE AND ANSWERED TWICE BY "I DO NOT KNOW", AND THE SECOND ASKING IS THE ONE THAT COUNTS, because it was asked in a doorway, to a person, with a mark on the end of it, by a boy of seventeen who has a book that can hold order and not days, and he wrote it down. The carter's asking was in a yard, to nobody, without a mark, at the last bell, on the third day of the fifty-ninth week, and it was twenty-five words.** Nobody has answered either. The System has not been asked and has not answered. **And the sentence a man of thirty-one has the standing to say, which is the one that keeps the people in a building and the people in a room apart, was available in that doorway on the sixth day of the sixtieth week and he did not say it, and it is the only thing he has ever had that could have been a better answer than the one he gave, and the one he gave was four words.**
2. **A COUNT LEAVES A WALL AND GOES INTO A PIECE OF PAPER, AND IT IS THE THIRD HAND'S OWN FIGURE, AND NOBODY HAS SAID WHOSE IT IS.** A woman of twenty-nine asked for a number and he had a figure and no day and gave the figure, and the figure is the one he wrote in chalk on the first morning of his being in this country, and it is the only figure in this country that anybody could put a name to without being told whose it is, and **a number with a hand in it is a person, and a person is what the two hundred words and the missing day and the recordwright's whole professional pride were spent to avoid.** He did not take it back. He did not explain. He did not apologise. **Nobody in two hundred and ten people has been told, and about nine of them know, and the one person who can be asked about it knows and has not said it and is not going to be told she is sorry for it.**

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM

1. **Going looking for a formula.** There is none, and there is not going to be one, and the check is the board chain.
2. **Reading the board's opening figure out of a block** instead of computing 6 × 1,460 + 25 × 2,190 = 63,510.
3. **The chalk count is FIVE for the whole volume so far and all five are in Batch 0001.** This batch added none. The figure in the chalk is 63,510 and it is the figure on the sheet in Chapter 270.
4. **A clean legality sweep.** Seventeen day-phrases, zero illegal, and the third question found five defects in this batch's own first writing.
5. **A forty-character carry-over is connective tissue and a seventy-character one is a defect.** This batch's first writing returned two hundred and thirty-one shared seventy-character runs against Chapters 201 to 260 and the delivery returns zero, and the rewordings are the list a later batch should not have to make again.
6. **The hundred and forty days.** The route-man says out loud in Chapter 257 that it is wrong by about seven in the hundred in a year about every seven years and that he has not corrected it, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110.
