# Continuity State — **open `state/index.md` first.** The live record is the top block, `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0001`; everything below it, from `## VOLUME 05 IS CLOSED` down, is archive.
## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0001 — Chapters 251–260, *The Bell, And A Count With No Date In It*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 05 IS CLOSED` block below it, which is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the twenty-six findings is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 06 BATCH 0001 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the state of the Briar March at the end of Chapter 260, written to be read by somebody who has read none of the batch.** Every figure in it was re-measured against the ten chapter files on the day it was written and not read out of a block.

### THE GROUND IN IT AS IT STANDS ON THE THIRD DAY OF THE FIFTY-NINTH WEEK

**Coldharrow is about two hundred and ten people in thirty-eight houses and it has a tower with a bell in it, and the tower is not a church and nobody will say what it is in a wrong voice. It has no market day: it has a market on the third day of a month of a year counted from the day the ewes come down off the fell, and that moves about three weeks in any year, and it is the only date in this country a stranger can be taught in a morning. About nine hundred elms and about two hundred of them dead. Two hundred and ten people and about nine hundred of the elms are inside the hedge ring and ten people are out of it in four farmsteads. There is one gate, and it is a post with a bar across it that is not shut, and a woman of about fifty stands at it on a market day with a stick and a board and counts loads and not people.**

**The lane runs from village to village and not from north to south, through eleven villages between Coldharrow and Coldharrow-edge: Coldharrow, Ellerby, Trope, Wrenny, Ashgill, Sallow, Nettlebed, Cadgerow, Henthill, Birrow, and one more that is not named in this batch. The ground comes up in eleven places in the first mile and the hedges are the problem and everybody who has walked it knows and nobody says so. The last four miles of the way in from the Shelf road is not a road and no cart goes up it, and the cart turned about at a posting yard with a wall round it.**

**THE BOARD, which is the only public record in this village and the instrument the whole volume is built on.** A plank about the size of a door, painted and unpainted so many times that the grain has come up through it in long pale streaks, on the north wall of the tower at about chest height where a person in the road can read it. **Thirty-one years in two hands. The older hand is the first six years and it is in the grain; the newer is twenty-five years and it is not in the grain and it is the more careful. There is no year at the head of it, there is no column, and there has never been a column, and there is no mark against any individual stroke. **THE OPENING FIGURE AND THE WORKING, because this block's first writing printed 50,370 off a twenty-four-and-seven split that no chapter supports: six years of the father's hand at the four clock bells a day is 6 × 1,460 = 8,760, and twenty-five years of hers at about six a day, which is what `252:17` says it has been for as long as she has been counting, is 25 × 2,190 = 54,750, and 8,760 + 54,750 = 63,510, and 6 + 25 = 31.** It reads sixty-three thousand five hundred and seventy-nine at the second light of the third day of the fifty-ninth week, which is the figure the tenth morning of the batch went out on and Chapter 260 prints as going on for tomorrow, and there is a third hand in it in chalk at the foot, and that hand belongs to a man who is not a person of the village and is not on anything and put no name to it.**

**THE BELL, and the arithmetic of it.** Four bells a day are the clock — **the first, the change, the noon, the last** — and about two a day on top of that is everything else, and the complete list of everything else is: **a burial, one. A boundary walked, seven. A debt called in, two. A market, four. A stranger, three. A fire, two, one to raise it and one to say it is out, and the second is rung whether or not it is out.** **They do not ring for a christening or a marriage.** Four a day is 1,460 a year and about six a day is 2,190 a year, and **2,190 ÷ 11 goes in a hundred and ninety-nine times with one over, so a year of one board is a hundred and ninety-nine whole passages of a cart and one ring, and the one ring is most likely a stranger and might be anybody.**

**AND THE FINDING, in a woman of forty-one's own mouth at `258:60`:** *a quiet day and a day with nine strangers on it are four rings apart, and I have been counting for twenty-five years and nobody has ever asked me which of them it was.* **And therefore a day's figure is a WEIGHTED number, and a weighted number cannot be turned back into what was done, and a March village can prove how many times and can prove before or after and cannot prove when.**

### THE DOCUMENTS THAT EXIST IN THE BRIAR MARCH AS OF CHAPTER 260, ONE EACH, AND WHERE EACH ONE IS

1. **THE BOARD AT COLDHARROW.** Above. Thirty-one years, two hands and a third in chalk. **The third hand is the only thing in this country that a stranger has written on.**
2. **THE SEALED COPY OF THE FOUR PAGES, IN A SATCHEL UNDER THE SEAT OF A TIMBER CART.** Four lines about a dam, a height, a day and a party to pay, copied at Sallowby at threepence for a sealed copy, and carried four hundred miles by a sawyer of about fifty-one who was given five shillings and four days and did not know what it was and was told it was a thing to carry and not a thing to read, and who has not read it and is not going to. **It is block-quoted in full at `260:37`–`43` and its four lines are: ONE, the order of the hundred and fifty-fifth day stands and no person in that basin is to dispute it; TWO, the schedule named in it is the schedule named in it and a schedule is not a river; THREE, the height named in it is eleven feet and NO SILL IS NAMED IN IT and a height with no sill named in it is a height above whatever the reader is standing on; FOUR, the party named in it shall pay at the closing of every year until the matter is reviewed and the review is not appointed.** **Nobody in the March has done anything about it and nobody was asked to and no bell could have been rung about it, and that last clause is the entire reason nothing happened.**
3. **BRAM ORREN'S LINE BOOK.** A book about the size of a hand under an oiled cloth cover with a corner turned up, run by him since he was twelve. **The four bells at the top of every page and a mark against the one he is at. What crossed, and when, and in what order. And what did not cross. Two pages lost in the ninth year, both days he was ill, and his father told him a book with a hole in it is a book with a hole in it.** **It is a book of order and not of dates, and the finest thing it can say about a thing is that it happened before this and after that, and it was NOT copied and NOT shown, because the boy of seventeen refused and was right.**
4. **THE TALLY IN A CARTER'S HEAD.** Ferrow Hask has kept one for nineteen years: what a gate is for. He has asked in four counties and there is no answer and he has stopped expecting one.

**AND WHAT IS NOT IN THE MARCH AND WAS NOT IN THE MARCH AND CANNOT BE PRODUCED HERE: a Shelf date.** A Bench order must be dated in the grain year. **There is nothing on any form in the building nine days from Coldharrow-edge, and there is nothing in any form in that building, that will take sixty-three thousand five hundred and seventy-nine.** The copywright at Coldharrow-edge has one press and no columns.

### THE PEOPLE, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST AND NONE OF THEM IS RELIEVED

**Ottily Roke, forty-one.** Keeps the bell and the board at Coldharrow and has for twenty-five years, on ninepence a day for one hundred and forty days, which is five pounds five shillings a year, and it was eightpence before that and she never asked for the extra penny because she did not know it was extra. **Her father kept it six years before her, started it, and is four years dead.** She is the person this volume's arithmetic is about, she says the price before the fact, and **she has never once been asked what the number is a number of and has answered nobody because nobody has asked.** She was asked in this batch, twice, and gave the same answer twice. She was not sent for and she is not a production. `252`, `253`, `257`, `258`

**Ferrow Hask, thirty-eight.** A carter with a handcart, nineteen years on the eleven-village lane, one shilling and sixpence a day on a hundred and forty days, ten pounds ten shillings a year, and about one hundred and ten days in a year about every seven years which he has never worked out and is four pounds. **He counts loads at a gate, is out by one in a month, and argues for a minute and is wrong.** He wants the lane to be a road because a road has a gate on it and a gate takes a toll. **He asked in Chapter 260 the question the whole volume is built on, in twenty-five words, at the last bell, to nobody, and nobody answered him and he did not appear to want one.** `255`, `257`, `259`, `260`

**Nell Callow, twenty-nine.** Keeps the only press in eleven villages, at Coldharrow-edge, which is not in the March. Paid by the date she sells, about forty a year, forty-seven pounds, about nine times a keeper's year. **She cannot make a date for a boundary and has made fourteen in six years and every one of them went against the man who asked, and she did not stop to find out what the second walking was for, and she has not gone looking for the other four.** A wife and two children. `256`

**Garrick Terrel, sixty-three.** The bell-founder's agent, twice a year, in a coat that had been good eleven years ago. **Metal at four shillings the pound on thirty-one pounds, frame and rope eleven shillings, £6 15s hung; it was four pounds twelve when he started quoting it and five pounds six after that.** He is the price in a mouth and he is not a villain and he is not redeemed, and he volunteered the sentence nobody in a yard would say: *nobody has ever set the bell against the woman, and you would have to be a fool or a bastard to do that in a yard.* `253`

**Bram Orren, seventeen.** CANON. Bell-runner, apprentice mapmaker, courier. Rings what he crosses and says the March's rule before anybody can accuse him of touching the rope, which he was told by a man of about sixty in a yard eleven miles off in the same words in the same number of seconds, and that is how a thing travels in a country with no paper in it. **He refused Ilyan, was right, and Ilyan did not ask again and said why in his own words.** `251`, `253`, `254`, `260`

**Sable Venn. A woman. She was already in the fiction and is not a production, and the March is her country and she has been in it nine years.** She said nine words in a yard before she was asked: *I put four lines into the March and I did not read them.* She has carried about four hundred such things, eleven of them are walls, and she does not read other people's records and never has. **She is not thanked. She is on no paper in the March and did not ask to be. She was in the room at Sallowby on the fifth day of the fifty-sixth week, and four people in that room will remember her to the end of their days and about six will not, and the man of fifty-one with the satchel is one of the six.** `259`

**Sera Quill, twenty-eight.** Recordwright, expressly not an advocate, nine days in the country. **She said the volume's split in nine words and does not improve on it, has said it twice, and knows she has.** Her family claim of six years is unfiled and is not going to be filed and was given once in about eleven words because he asked in front of a witness. **She refuses to write down the unprovable boundary and says so as a thing she has done and not as a thing she has refrained from.** `254`, `255`, `258`, `259`, `260`

**Tarin Keel, fifty-two in the chapters, forty-six in `bible/characters.md`; the chapter file wins and this is the second time in this project.** With a rule on a string round his neck. **Counted milestones out of a posting yard and was told to stop. Does every sum out loud on a cart's side, gets it wrong once, and says where the figure came from, and says the figure came from a man of fifty-two in a yard four hundred miles off who was wrong and whom he has not corrected.** `255`, `256`, `257`, `258`

**Unnamed and standing:** a woman of about fifty at the gate with a stick and a board; a man of about thirty-one with a cartload of timber and two men with a barrow; a man of about thirty-eight with a barrow who came to a cottage; a woman of about sixty at the ninth door; a woman of about sixty in Ellerby who was twenty-one when the boundary was walked and was somebody's aunt that day and does not know what any of the days are called; the boy who rang and ran off with a dog; a man of about sixty in a yard eleven miles off who teaches boys what the March's rule is; a man of about sixty-one; about nine hundred elms; and about two hundred and ten people.

**A sawyer of about fifty-one** who is a person of the Red Basin and not of the March, who was in a room in Sallowby eight weeks ago and does not remember Sable Venn, who has bought paper in four counties for nine years, and who has a satchel under the seat of his cart. **He is not a production and he is not a villain and nobody in the March is afraid of him and he is the cheapest and the most expensive thing in the volume.**

### THE THREE CLOCKS, AND WHAT THE BATCH DID TO THEM

**The Shelf: fifty-two weeks of seven days, day one being the second day of the fortieth week. The fifty-eighth week is 126–132 and the fifty-ninth is 133–139. Volume 05 ran 71 to 120. This batch ran 126 to 135, and the five days between are not a gap and are not printed as one. Nothing turns the grain year here and no file may say that it turns.**

**The bell: four a day, the first, the change, the noon, the last, and a day begins at the first and ends at the last and nobody in eleven villages has ever said what either is a height above.**

**The board: a count, thirty-one years, and a day's figure is a weighted number.** A stroke takes about a second and four strokes is a good hour and that is not a metaphor, it is the whole of the labour.

**The year turn: the first bell after the ewes come down off the fell, and it moves about three weeks and it is the only date a stranger can be taught in a morning.**

**NONE OF IT CONVERTS INTO THE SHELF AND THERE IS NO FORMULA AND THERE IS NOT GOING TO BE ONE, and that is the batch's finding and not a gap in the record: a ring is not a week, a March boundary is dated in rings, a Bench order must be dated in the grain year, and the price of the conversion is £1 3s 6d and fifteen days of the road, set by how far away somebody else's count agrees with yours.**

### THE MONEY AT THE END OF THE BATCH, EVERY FIGURE WITH ITS WORKING

| Figure | The working, and it is the whole of it |
|---|---|
| **A carter's day with a handcart on the lane = 18d, on 140 days = £10 10s** | 18 × 140 = **2,520d** ✓ |
| **The levy at a gate = threepence a load, at nine loads a day, on 140 days = £15 15s a village** | 140 × 9 = 1,260 loads; × 3d = **3,780d** ✓ |
| **The excess of the levy over the walking = £5 5s, and the keeper's wage at 9d × 140 days = £5 5s, TO THE PENNY** | 3,780 − 2,520 = **1,260d**; 140 × 9 = **1,260d** ✓ |
| **And the whole of the margin across eleven villages = £57 15s** | 11 × 1,260 = **13,860d** ✓ |
| **A bell, new, hung, with rope = £6 15s; and a bell is £1 10s more than a year of the woman** | 31 × 48d = **1,488d**; + 132d = **1,620d**; 1,620 − 1,260 = **360d = £1 10s** ✓ |
| **A Bench date, bought = £1 3s 6d = fifteen days of the man who walks the road and one shilling; and four dates is a year of the keeper less eleven shillings** | 36 + 6 + 240 = **282d**; 282 ÷ 18 = 15 rem 12 ✓; 4 × 282 = 1,128d; 1,260 − 1,128 = **132d = 11s** ✓ |
| **Nell Callow's year = £47 exactly, about nine times a keeper's year** | 40 × 282 = **11,280d**; 11,280 ÷ 240 = 47 ✓ |
| **The bell's own arithmetic and the board's opening figure, and the split is the father's six and the woman's twenty-five** | 4 × 365 = 1,460 ✓; 6 × 365 = 2,190 ✓; 6 × 1,460 = 8,760 and 25 × 2,190 = 54,750 and 8,760 + 54,750 = **63,510** ✓; 2,190 ÷ 11 = 199 rem 1 ✓ |
| **The four pages of the hundred-and-fifty-fifth day, copied and sealed at Sallowby = threepence; five shillings and four days to a man of fifty-one to carry it four hundred miles** | CANON for the penny, and it is the cheapest thing in this volume and the most expensive |

### THE TWO THINGS A BATCH 0002 WRITER NEEDS AND CANNOT GET FROM ANY FIGURE ABOVE

1. **NOBODY HAS DONE ANYTHING ABOUT THE FOUR LINES, and that is not inertia and it is not a decision anybody took.** A bench that is sent nothing does not write nothing down; it writes that it was sent nothing; and that is a sentence about a place and not about a person, and there is nobody in it to answer it, and **a place with no party is a place that can be done to.** And the second line of the paper is a schedule and not a river, and a bell is a schedule, and the first bell of a Coldharrow day is a height above nothing, and a height with no sill named in it is a height above whatever the reader is standing on — so the paper is load-bearing here and not a curiosity, and there are two hundred and ten people inside it and the paper does not say so.
2. **HE SAID OUT LOUD, IN A YARD, THAT HE WAS GOING TO PAY FOR A DATE AND HAVE SOMEBODY ELSE PAY IT TOO SO THAT IT IS NOT HIM, AND HE HAS TO DO IT.** Nobody thanked him and nobody is relieved. He also said that two is worse than one and that when there are two of them they will be able to be argued with, and the recordwright did the sum on him in four minutes and told him she had said her nine words twice and would like it noticed. **And a chalk figure in his own hand has been on somebody else's board for ten days, on five of the ten mornings, and nobody asked him to put it there, and he is going to be asked.**

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM

1. **Going looking for a formula.** There is none, and there is not going to be one, and the check is the board chain.
2. **Reading the board's opening figure out of a block** instead of computing 6 × 1,460 + 25 × 2,190 = 63,510, **and the twenty-four and seven that this block printed on its first writing is not derivable from anything in the fiction and is the reason this item now names the two lines that carry the split.**
3. **The chalk count is five and the morning-figure count is nine of ten, and they are counts of two different things.** Print what you are counting.
4. **A clean legality sweep.** Fourteen day-phrases, zero illegal, and the third question found four date defects in this batch's own first writing.
5. **A forty-character carry-over.** It is connective tissue. A seventy-character one is a defect, and one was found and reworded.
6. **The hundred and forty days.** The route-man says out loud that it is wrong by about seven in the hundred in a year about every seven years and that he has not corrected it, and a later pass may not quietly correct it and may not quietly use 110.

## VOLUME 05 IS CLOSED — Chapters 201–250, *The Red Basin*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0005` block below it, which is archive and is not edited by this phase. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working, the one hundred and forty-eight findings and the eighteen handed-on threads is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 05 IS CLOSED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the state of the Red Basin at the end of Chapter 250, which is the end of Volume 05, written to be read by somebody who has read none of the volume. Every figure in it was re-measured against `chapters/volume-05/chapter-0201.md` to `chapter-0250.md` on 27 September 2026, after the last save to those files, and NOT read out of a list.** The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working, the one hundred and forty-eight findings and the eighteen handed-on threads is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 05 IS CLOSED`, and it outranks this block wherever the two differ.

### THE GROUND AND THE WATER IN IT AS IT STANDS ON THE LAST DAY OF THE FIFTY-SEVENTH WEEK

**The Sallow is running. The water-book has a second hand in it and a penny a day behind it, and the girl of seventeen writes the day at the top of every page in her own hand in a book that is a hundred years old and is in four hands before hers. The gate at Hollow Sallow is hung and opens at the closing of the water, a hundred and twelve times a year, and it cost five pounds eighteen and a man on ten shillings a week hung it in a day on his own time and told nobody and was not thanked for it. The Anner dam is at eleven feet to the Anner's own sill and four men and a barrow hold it, and the causeway at Hollow Sallow is shut and has been since the order of the hundred and fifty-fifth day, and about nine carts a day go the long way round the top of the pass at a morning and a half each way, and the Anner bears the whole of it. There is a second frame behind the first one at Hollow Sallow, newer, that came at the same time and does nothing at all.**

**The ground: about four hundred and ten people in about ninety houses, and one of those houses had new doors on it in the winter before the water came and the other was a barn in the year the marks were cut, and nobody has named either of them. The salt-pans above Coldbeck are eleven of them on a shelf of flat ground where the red clay has been dug out to about the depth of a person's shin. Anner Pit is about four hundred strong in the working years, and the low end of its old workings is four hundred and six feet below the sill stone at the mouth, and the last hundred and forty feet of the water there has been under to the roof since before a woman of twenty-six was seventeen. Sallowby has four hundred people in it, one market day a week, a street about the length of a lane at Ryefold, a chandler at one end and a saddler at the other, a scrivener over the chandler's, a water-seat over a shuttered shop, a press in the water-seat's one room, and a gate at the top of the pass with nothing at the other end of the road behind it.**

**And the water: the Sallow came up the ninth doorpost on the last day of the fifty-first week and stood about two feet in the lane, and nineteen houses of the twenty that could be counted were under, and the order of the hundred and fifty-fifth day said eleven. It turned in the night of the fourth day of the fifty-second week into the fifth and fell about a foot a day, and by the fourth day of the fifty-third week it was a foot and a bit in the bed on the seventh and last day of that week, which is where it had been on the fourth day of the week before, and the mud is not down with it. Nobody drowned. Nineteen roofs are wanted at nine pounds each and a man of fifty-six will not split nine pounds, and four of them came off in the fifty-fourth week and the rest are a year's work. And there is a rule in chalk on the north post of the frame at Hollow Sallow in a woman's own hand, with the day written over it, and it is a mark of a thing the water did and not a mark of a height anybody decided to be above.**

### THE DOCUMENTS THAT EXIST IN THE RED BASIN, ONE EACH, AND WHERE EACH ONE IS, AS THEY STAND AND NOT AS THEY WERE EXPLAINED

1. **THE CHARTER. One sheet, one hand, on the wall of the Seat's one room in Sallowby.** It says in its own first line that it can be ended, and that any person of this valley may go from it at any time and shall lose nothing by going but the charter, and that it is reviewed at the closing of the water one year after the day written at the head of the page in the water-book for that day, by any person of this valley who brings it. **It carries no name and no dash.** Nobody drafted it in a room: eleven people said one sentence each in their own houses and a scrivener wrote down what was said in her own hand and the clerk copied it out fair, and there is not a clause in it that anybody in a room improved.
2. **THE THIRD DOCUMENT. One sheet, one hand, three copies, in three places:** in the press of the Seat's one room; in a lane at the bottom of a hill, in the hands of a man of thirty-four who cannot read it; and on a table under the chain-book at Anner Pit, where a joiner with a rule put it. It names **two sills**, it puts **eleven feet above each of them**, and it writes down that **the two lines are four feet apart** and that nobody in this valley has agreed to the difference and nobody has refused it. It is on the strength of the chamber's own second ruling of nine years ago that no order may name a height without naming the sill it is taken from. **It cost five pence, the Seat paid it out of the press, and the three copies are in three places and the three people who can hold them cannot read one another's.**
3. **THE ELEVEN-HAND SETTLEMENT. On the wall of the Seat's one room, eleven hands, six lines in Sallowby and a seventh that exists only in a lane.** A struck line with three hands on it that do not agree. **A space at the foot of it with nothing in it and no dash in it.** Its name is off and stays off. **Its height is off and stays off, and the eleven feet is not on it, and the reason is not a secret and is not anybody's fault: the settlement is eleven people's and the height is the one thing about it that eleven people do not agree on.** The copy that goes down the hill is in the clerk's one hand and is kept in the lane by a person of the lane, and that person is a man of thirty-four who cannot read it, and **there is no second copy anywhere.**
4. **THE FOUR PAGES of the hundred and fifty-fifth day, on the wall, still correct on their face, not touched by the charter.** They name a dam, a height, a day and a party to pay, and they name nobody else, and their second line is a schedule and not a river. They breach the chamber's own second ruling on their face and are not void, because the ruling is about a mark on a doorpost and not about a height named in an order.
5. **THE A PAGE OF THE CHAMBER'S OWN PRINTED INDEX, the original and not the hand-copy, with a heading of two words of which the second is the ordinary word a clerk writes at the head of a column that means not sent.** About four hundred and sixty entries in the three columns beside it, going back to the first shaking in a small hard hand that gets worse as it comes forward; one rule ruled under all four; and under the fourth, **four lines, all four of them about this basin.** Nine printed sheets came down in a rider's bag, of which this was the ninth. **It is open. It was opened in a room on a table nine feet by fourteen, held open at the back page by one finger, on the fourth day of the fifty-sixth week, and a copy of its four lines went up on a wall at a penny.**
6. **THE WATER-BOOK OF THE SEAT OF SALLYBY. A hundred years old, kept in four hands, and a fifth since the girl of seventeen was paid a penny a day for the second hand at nine shillings and fourpence a year.** It has four columns and they belong to the four hands. The back of one leaf carries the list of what nineteen people answered, with a mark under every answer, and it is the first document anybody in this basin has ever written about itself.
7. **THE WATER-BOOK OF THE MILL. The sluice-keeper's own book, and on the ninth she wrote *the water is in the hollow* three weeks and a day before the water came, and every year, and nobody has ever asked her how she knows.**
8. **THE CHAIN-BOOK OF THE ANNER, about the size of a door and a hundred and thirty years old in its binding, about nine hundred pages, in a hand nobody in this valley can read.** The last entry in it is in the hand of the under-manager's father. The position beneath the basin is in it, and the position is also in a second book in a room four miles off, and the two men who put them in cannot read one another's entry and both can read their own.
9. **THE BOND. Lodged with the water-seat and on a wall, three sentences, of which the first two are a promise by the Anner to the village the mine had promised to protect, and the third is a penalty of forty pounds that stopped being paid in the fourth year.** The promise was kept exactly by the party it names.
10. **THE STANDING ORDER OF THE SLUICE, which names a weather and not a day: *the day the pans are turned at Coldbeck*.** It is the eleventh salting of the forty-ninth year and the bond is the eleventh salting of the fortieth, which is the same salting nine years along.
11. **THE RIDER'S BAG papers. Thirty-two papers went nine days up a road in nine years and were never answered, of forty-one that came out of the gap in the wall, and nine of the forty-one were answers.** The second paper went in on the first day of the fifty-sixth week, paid for by the Anner out of its own road cost, with the amount, the payer and the thing wanted written on the outside of the sheet in a clerk's hand, because a matter nobody is paying for comes back in nine days; and a man with a bag comes twice in the year, so **it cannot come back before the eighteenth day from the day it went in.**
12. **THE SIX LINES AND THE STRIKE. The struck first line of the seven-line draft is left on the paper, at a scrivener's request, because anybody who comes in that room in a year will spend a month working out who took it back and why.**

### THE ELEVEN FEET, WHERE IT IS, WHERE IT IS NOT, AND THE RULED GEOMETRY THAT MAKES THE DIFFERENCE OF FOUR FEET LEGIBLE

**THE RULED GEOMETRY OF VOLUME 05, and it is the authority over every other table in this file, and IT WAS RULED BY THE REVIEW OF BATCH 0005 ON 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 AND NO PASS MAY RE-DERIVE IT. The Anner's sill is the dam's own sill and every number in this basin goes back to it. The frame's sill at Hollow Sallow is NINE FEET ABOVE IT. The bed below the village is THIRTEEN FEET BELOW THE FRAME'S SILL and is therefore FOUR FEET BELOW THE ANNER'S SILL. The bond's line is ELEVEN FEET ABOVE THE ANNER'S SILL and is therefore TWO FEET ABOVE THE FRAME'S SILL, which is about the height of the floors of the houses on the upper half of that lane. The water's high mark in the chamber's own return is TWO FEET FOUR INCHES ABOVE THE SILL OF THE FRAME, which is ELEVEN FEET FOUR INCHES ABOVE THE ANNER'S SILL, FOUR INCHES ABOVE THE BOND'S LINE. And therefore the difference between two lines that are both *eleven feet* is exactly the distance between the two sills the third document names, which is FOUR FEET, which is FORTY-EIGHT INCHES.**

**The eleven feet is in the third document, in three copies, in three places, with both sills named, on the strength of the chamber's own second ruling.** It is in the bond, eleven feet above the Anner's own sill. It is on the four pages, and it is the number that put two feet of water into nineteen houses. It is in a height a man of seventy-four cut on eleven doorposts in one day with a stick and a string and a bucket of tar, in a year when the Sallow came up nine feet in a night, and a mark cut on a doorpost is not a measurement. **IT IS NOT ON THE ELEVEN-HAND SETTLEMENT, AND A CLOSE MAY NOT MOVE IT THERE AND MAY NOT CALL THE THIRD DOCUMENT A RESOLUTION OF THE HEIGHT QUESTION, BECAUSE IT IS NOT ONE: it is where the disagreement is written down.** A bad answer in three copies can be argued with by three people and a good answer in one can be argued with by nobody.

### THE POSITION BENEATH THE BASIN, AS A BEARING, A DEPTH AND A PAGE, AND NOTHING ELSE

**A bearing: nine degrees and a quarter off the Anner's own heading, at more than four hundred and six feet, in the floor along the eleventh chain of the oldest trench, in a straight line from the red stone at the north end to the low end of the pit at the south.** **A depth: four hundred and six feet is the pit and is not the void, and under the low end there is a void of depth not yet found.** **A page: the fourth turn of the tithe book, the first of the take-outs, being eleven chains and sixteen links.** The six cuts in that floor are on the sixth salting of the eleventh pan-book year, the drain that was aimed along them was cut in the ninth turn and is the later thing by a span this basin has no instrument for naming, and the man who aimed it is not in either book and is not in this basin.

**It was identified in the fifty-first week by two ordinary records that do not meet, and it has not moved since, and nobody went down. The last hundred and forty feet of the water at the low end of the pit has been under to the roof since before a woman of twenty-six was seventeen; her name is Alo Vearn, she is twenty-six, she is at the pit this week, and she does not know that a sentence about her is in a book six miles off that nobody has ever been in this basin to write.** No pass may reach this, may describe reaching it, and may not say that a later batch will.

### THE PEOPLE OF THE BASIN, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST AND NONE OF THEM IS RELIEVED

**Ilma Treen, thirty-eight**, keeps the sluice at Hollow Sallow and has for eleven years, on nine shillings a week for the sixteen weeks the slow runs and nothing the rest of the year, four shillings and sixpence of it from the pit and four from the seat, and three years of it were paid in one week in an envelope at the closing of the water and she counted it twice on her own sill. She wanted the figure in the return of the water at the frame for six of the eleven years and got it in Chapter 247, read out in a lane by a man who cannot read, and she put a rule to the mark herself at the eighth hour and wrote the day over it in chalk and did not write the eleventh year. **She has said out loud, before she was asked, that the end of the charter is her wage, and she gave the working, and she would rather have a paper she can see the end of than a paper she cannot.**

**Jory Vellan, fifty-two**, under-manager at Anner Pit, nineteen years in it, keeps the chain-book. The volume's honest opponent and not a villain and says so about himself. He paid the five pounds and six out of the Anner's own road cost and said out loud in a room that he was buying a date and not a conscience and would like it written that he is not asking to be thanked. He gave up in public the right to stand in a room in a year's time and say the chamber got this valley wrong, on purpose, out loud, and the chapter says he gave it and did not have it taken. He has been given a number and never a reason, and he is right, and he has never been thanked for holding a dam exactly.

**Hollis Nance, forty-four**, clerk of the water-seat at Sallowby, eleven years, eleven seats of which eight have never had anybody in them, an index of indexes with a fourth column at the head of it that is empty, and a sentence he has said three times and it is *nobody has hidden anything*. He says the price of taking a paper to Orison and bringing it back is five pounds and six, and says it in his first nine words. He wrote the amount, the payer and the thing wanted on the outside of a sheet. He read four lines out in a room because a man with nothing in his hands asked him to and read the numbers twice.

**Wenna Skell, thirty-three**, scrivener over a chandler's, a penny a page and threepence a sealed copy, about six hundred and forty deeds in eleven years and about ninety of them for the Anner line, who says the price before the fact because the price before the fact is the only honest part of it. She asked that a struck line not be left on a piece of paper. She said, in nine words, that a paper with a name at the foot of it is a paper about a man from four hundred miles off. She found the form and said out loud that it has one box on it and the box is for a figure and there is no box for who is to answer or for how long.

**Fen Tregerran, sixty-one**, keeps the salt-pans and the count of saltings, thirty years, and it is the only clock in this basin that has never been out. He refused in about nine words to date a paper for a man of thirty-four. He came four miles to say his own count had been counted wrong, stayed four hours, and asked the only question in that chapter that turned anything over. **Nobody had ever asked him what comes up off a road.**

**Tarin Keel**, field partner, came because a road in this basin does not arrive where it set out, has counted carts on roads for nineteen years, and cannot help himself and says the number out loud. He is right four times in the last batch and wrong none, and he would not say the word *scandal* again in a room on purpose.

**Coll Ferren, thirty-four.** One of the eleven hands on the settlement, the fifth of the nineteen, in the second house from the bottom of the lane since he was nine, and **he cannot read.** He reads the settlement out in a road at the closing of the water about forty times a week and nobody has ever checked one word of it. He is the finding twice over: a man who lost a house is not a house, and a paper nobody is on cannot be asked anybody anything.

**Garrow Lee**, the joiner, with a rule, who has been trying since the sixth year after the marks were cut to get somebody to measure them, and who said the volume's last civic finding: a bad answer in three copies can be argued with by three people and a good answer in one can be argued with by nobody.

**Brann Slee, fifty-six**, does the roofs, nine pounds a roof and will not split it, and asked the first question anybody in this basin has asked the man of thirty-one about a thing he is carrying, and got nine words, and was not helped, and ended the argument in a lane in nine words of his own: *a roof does not care and neither do I.*

**The woman of about forty who washes the pen.** Nine years in the Seat's room, not paid by the seat or by the pit, the only person in it who knows what a document is. She has refused this basin three times in a volume and been right three times — about a sheet under a board in a cart shed, about a man's name at the foot of a thing, and about herself — and on the third occasion she was asked about a different thing, which was herself, and her reason was that the room in this market town has one clerk in it who knows what a document is and that is exactly one too few already.

**Sera Quill, twenty-eight.** Recordwright of a public record, expressly not an advocate, at the back of this valley for about six weeks, who has not put a hand to a thing in this basin and was not going to. She had a sentence ready in Chapter 246 and did not say it, on the way out, to nobody, in about nine words: a public thing that can be spent is not a thing anybody can be protected by.

**The girl of seventeen.** Paid a penny a day for every day the water runs, keeps the water-book in the fifth hand, writes the day at the head of every page, and was given the number a man of thirty-one put at the head of his own pages on the first day of the fifty-fourth week and has never been told where it came from and has never asked. She copied the figure off a lath in chalk into her own hand. She is on no paper at all except the water-book.

**A woman of about thirty-one with two children and a house at the ninth door**, the only hand on the settlement that argues for something. **A man of about thirty-four who cannot read the copy he keeps** — see Coll Ferren. **A man of about forty with a crowbar.** **A man of about forty-two from the cart yard at Sallowby**, who established that nobody in this basin has been in the building. **A man of about sixty who washes the pen's bench and turns the board over** is the woman of about forty. **Alo Vearn, twenty-six**, at the pit. **Ilya Kever, seventy-four**, who cut the marks. **The man who comes through the north twice a year**, a man with a bag, who has been in the Seat's one room for ten minutes in fifty chapters. **And the thirty-odd other people of the basin, unnamed, who are the standing of every document in it.**

**Sable Venn** came into this market town on foot from the north on the fifth day of the fifty-sixth week with a board under her arm, made a good offer, did not win, did not lose, is not on any paper in this valley, and did not ask to be. She is the sixth and last of the six people who arrived on a day nobody fixed, and the full state of her is in `state/character-state.md`.

**And the people four hundred miles off: about thirty clerks in a building with a yard at Orison and a bench of four above them, who did their jobs properly, whose good name is now a public thing that has been spent in public by a man from four hundred miles off, and who have not been in this valley once in nine years and have been right three times in nine years. A grocer at Coldbeck whose petition of nine years ago produced the ruling this basin has had on a wall since before the water. Nesh Arrad, thirty-nine, of the district of Ryefold, four children, a stove and no paper at all, whose clock is still running. And a reeve, a scrivener, a boy of nineteen who is the only instrument a class of three hundred and thirty-nine has, and a woman of thirty-nine with four children who is not on any of it.**

### THE THREE CLOCKS, AND WHAT THE VOLUME DID TO THEM

**The Shelf's grain year: fifty-two weeks of seven Shelf days each, Shelf day one being the second day of the fortieth week. The fiftieth week is days seventy to seventy-six, the fifty-first seventy-seven to eighty-three, the fifty-second eighty-four to ninety, the fifty-third ninety-one to ninety-seven, the fifty-fourth ninety-eight to one hundred and four, the fifty-fifth one hundred and five to one hundred and eleven, the fifty-sixth one hundred and twelve to one hundred and eighteen, and the fifty-seventh one hundred and nineteen to one hundred and twenty-five. Volume 05 opened on Shelf day seventy-one, which is basin day one hundred and forty-nine, and closed on Shelf day one hundred and twenty, which is basin day one hundred and ninety-eight, on the formula basin day = Shelf day + 78. Week fifty-two is days eighty-four to ninety; nothing in Volume 05 turns the grain year and no file in this project may say that it turns, and the four hundred and eighty-five pounds is named in Chapter 250 once, in a hand, in nobody's mouth, and that exception is spent.**

**The pan-book year of Hollow Sallow: fifty saltings, more or less, and a salting is a drying and has no length — seven weeks in a dry year and eleven in a wet one. Fen Tregerran has kept the count for thirty years and it has never been out. This is the clock that makes the sluice's standing order arrive on the wrong day every year, and the eleven salting of the forty-ninth year and the eleventh salting of the fortieth are the same salting nine years along.**

**The turn: twelve years from the first shaking, turn nought being the first shaking, and this is turn twelve. The order of the hundred and fifty-fifth day is the second turn. The take-out in the tithe book is the fourth turn. The overflow in the chain-book was cut in the ninth turn, which is three years back, and the six cuts in the floor are on the sixth salting of the eleventh pan-book year, which is before there was a turn at all, so the drain is the later thing and there is no way whatever on earth to say how much later.**

**The day: a day out of the water-book since the second shaking, and it is the only date in the Red Basin that anybody keeps. It has four hands in it and a fifth since the girl of seventeen was paid a penny a day, and a second hand at a penny a day for a hundred and twelve days a year is a hundred and twelve pence. A man of thirty-one has written eighteen of these figures at the head of eighteen pages in his own hand in a penny exercise book in a cart shed behind two houses, and he has put nothing down under any of the last three.**

**NONE OF THE THREE CLOCKS CONVERTS INTO ANY OTHER, AND THE VOLUME PROVED IT TWICE AND THAT IS ITS ENGINE. A basin day is a Shelf day plus seventy-eight. A pan-book year is a year and nine is a year, and a salting has no length. A height is a height above something, and there are three sills inside four miles and the same figure means two different things two rooms apart. And a no order may name a height without naming the sill it is taken from, which is the chamber's own second ruling of nine years ago, and it is the reason a third document exists.**

### THE MONEY AT THE END OF THE VOLUME, EVERY FIGURE WITH ITS WORKING, AND THE MONEY IS THE SAME MONEY

| Figure | The working, and it is the whole of it |
|---|---|
| **A matter carried to the chamber at Orison and brought back = £5 6s, paid by the Anner out of its own road cost** | 240d + 960d + 72d = **1,272d = £5 6s** ✓ (`242:7`) |
| **The settlement of this valley, recurring = £178 13s 4s a year** | 19 × 2,160d + 1,728d + 112d = **42,880d**; 42,880 − 42,720 = 160d = 13s 4d ✓ |

### THE TWO THINGS A VOLUME 06 WRITER NEEDS AND CANNOT GET FROM ANY FIGURE ABOVE

1. **NOBODY IS RELIEVED AND NOBODY IS FORGIVEN.** The carrying is off nothing. The blank in the third line is a blank. The second notice of five shillings and fourpence is unpaid. The claim of forty-five pounds two shillings and eightpence is unproducible and the man is right. The dead man in the fourth line of a list of two hundred and forty is dead and his wife does not know. The clerk of thirty-four's letter is in a bundle on a shelf in a room in Sallowby with a knife-cut end on the string and it is not opened. Nesh Arrad's letter has not arrived and cannot have arrived. The Anner is to be asked again in one year and the asking is a year off. And the volume ends on a person doing their work, which is not a resolution and is not to be called one.
2. **NOBODY WENT DOWN, AND THE SECOND HALF OF THE VOLUME'S QUESTION WAS NEVER ASKED.** The position is a bearing, a depth and a page, and it has not moved. The way down is a chamber with water in it. **The first missing anchor position was identified in Chapter 209 and has not moved since, and the second half of the volume's question is not this volume's and is not going to be anybody's until a volume is written to carry it.**

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM, AND TWO OF THEM ARE NEW SINCE THE BATCH 0005 BLOCK BELOW

1. **A day-phrase sweep is a legality check and not a truth check, and the legality question has now been asked across ninety-nine phrases in this volume and returned zero illegal, and the truth question has been asked and caught four: two in Batch 0005's own first writing, one in the Batch 0003 record, and two more in Chapters 231 and 238 by this close.** The cheapest second instrument is fifty chapter days against ninety-nine day-phrases, read side by side, and it takes ten minutes.
2. **THE DAY-PHRASE FIGURE IN THE BLOCKS BELOW IS WRONG IN TWO PLACES AND BOTH WERE MEASURED.** Batch 0003 is printed as twenty-eight and is **fourteen**, which is exactly double and is therefore a doubled count. Batch 0005 is printed as nineteen and is **twenty-two**. Batches 0001, 0002 and 0004 printed 11, 26 and 26 and are right. **Run the pattern yourself and do not copy any of them.**
3. **THE DUPLICATE SCAN IS A SPAN SCAN AND NOT A LINE SCAN, AND THE UNIT MUST BE DECLARED.** A scan for duplicated **lines** returns zero over this volume and always will, because a line in these files is a whole paragraph. Run the span instrument in the form printed at `state/current.md` §2 item 7 — normalised whitespace, every window of exactly *k* characters at every position, deduplicated within each chapter — and at seventy characters it returns 4,147 distinct repeated spans and 4,901 repeat occurrences over the fifty chapters, 519 and 558 over Batch 0005, and 1,067 of Batch 0005's spans are shared with Chapters 231–240. **Expect this volume's three deliberate formulae to dominate it and read what is left, because what is left is the only useful part, and what is left here is nothing that is not a formula.**
4. **A MONEY CHAPTER WITH A LONG RUNNING FIGURE IN IT WILL DRIVE THE COUNT OF THE WORD *NINE* UP, AND THE FIX IS NOT TO ABBREVIATE THE FIGURE.** The count is **466 whole-word occurrences of *nine* over the fifty chapters, 443 of them standalone and 23 inside a compound, and 448 of *eleven*, every one of them standalone**, and the residue in both cases is real quantities in the fiction and not filler.
5. **THE BOLD INVENTORY AND THE ITALIC INVENTORY NEED THEIR SPLITS PRINTED, OR THE COUNT IS A COUNT OF A THING WHOSE UNIT IS NOT STATED.** Six bold lines and zero emphasis spans; forty-four italic spans of which nineteen are his own hand and twenty-five are textual citation.
6. **A BLOCK-QUOTED DOCUMENT IS PROSE IN CAPITALS AND THE UPPERCASE SCAN LOOKS AT IT, AND A CHAPTER PROMPT CAN BE WRONG ABOUT A CHARACTER'S SEX AND THE CHAPTER FILE IS THE FACT.** It has happened in this volume, twice in two batches, and the state file was the thing that was wrong the first time.

## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0005 — Chapters 241–250, *The Redacted Archive, And A Rule-Free Alternative*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0004` block below it, which is archive and is not edited. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the eighteen handed-on threads is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0005 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the state of the Red Basin at the end of Chapter 250, which is the end of Volume 05, and every figure in it was re-measured against `chapters/volume-05/chapter-0241.md` to `chapter-0250.md` after the last save and NOT read out of a list.** The volume's day-count is EIGHTEEN, not the fourteen the blocks below this one say in places; the panels are three, not four; the produced-person count is SIX, not five; and the batch prompt directories are twenty-three, not twenty-two. **All four of those were corrected in `state/current.md` and `state/index.md` by measurement, and the correction is labelled as a correction so that a later phase does not mistake it for planning.**

### THE GROUND AND THE WATER IN IT AS IT STANDS AT THE END OF CHAPTER 250

**The Sallow is running. The water-book has a second hand in it and a penny a day behind it, and the girl of seventeen writes the day at the top of every page. The gate at Hollow Sallow is hung and opens at the closing of the water, a hundred and twelve times a year. The Anner dam is at eleven feet to the Anner's own sill and four men and a barrow are putting forty loads of clay on it in the order they have gone on in for eleven years.** **Nineteen roofs are wanted and a good many are still wanted; the sixth came off on the fifth of the fifty-fifth week and a man of fifty-six is four days on each and there are about a hundred and forty days of this weather in front of the lane.** The causeway at Hollow Sallow is shut and the high road is carrying the Anner's carts with a morning and a half added each way, and the Anner bears the whole of it, and nobody in this valley can say whether it is paying for eight carts a day or nine. **The height is still not on the settlement sheet and the eleventh notch of the volume is not the only notch: a twelfth was cut at the salt-pans on the second day of the fifty-sixth week and it is a count of notches and not a count of days.**

### THE COLUMN IS OPEN, AND WHAT IT COST, AND IT IS NOT A REVELATION

**The chamber's own printed index — the original, not the hand-copy — was opened on a table nine feet by fourteen in the Seat's one room on the fourth day of the fifty-sixth week, basin day one hundred and ninety-three, by a man of thirty-one who has no authority, out loud, in front of about nine people, and nobody had asked him and nobody was asked.** Four lines came out of it and all four are about this basin and one of the four is a printed rule the chamber worked under for nine years and that this basin has had on a wall in its own words since before the water. **One page of them was copied out in the clerk's hand and went up on the wall of the Seat's room beside the four pages of the hundred and fifty-fifth day, at a penny, paid out of the press.**

**THE COST, IN FULL, AND IT IS NOT PAID BACK:** a bench of four's good name is a public thing, it does not survive the asking, it cannot be spent twice and it does not need to be. **Nobody thanked him. Nobody was relieved. Some of what is in the four lines is the fault of about thirty clerks in a building with a yard and some of it is the fault of one clerk in this room who put a good deal of it in the gap himself in nine years without once asking what it was for, and he said that out loud himself and nobody asked him to. He could have said the sentence that splits the thirty from the four and he did not, and he says in his own words that he did not, because the sentence that separates them is the sentence that protects the institution.**

**AND NOBODY HID ANYTHING, WHICH IS THE SENTENCE THE BATCH HAD TO SAY AGAIN IN THE ROOM WHERE IT COSTS SOMEBODY SOMETHING, and it was said by the same clerk and the thing it cost him was that then it was a thing in a book and now it is a thing in a bag a man from four hundred miles off has opened on his table.** There is no secret room, no frightened clerk, no man who did it, no second face of the device, and no new enemy in this world.

### WHAT IS STANDING AT THE END OF THE VOLUME, AND IT IS A LIST AND NOT A RESOLUTION

1. **A revocable local charter**, one sheet, one hand, on the wall of the Seat's one room, **with no name on it and no dash on it**, which says in its own first line that it can be ended, and that any person of the valley may go from it at any time and shall lose nothing by going but the charter, and that it shall be reviewed at the closing of the water one year after the day written at the head of the page in the water-book for that day, by any person of this valley who brings it. **It is worse than the ruling a man of thirty-one proposed in a cart shed in the fifty-first week and better than the flood, and both of those are true.**
2. **The eleven-hand settlement**, eleven hands, six lines in Sallowby and a seventh in a lane, a struck line with three hands on it that do not agree, **and a space at the foot of it with nothing in it and no dash in it.** Its name is off and stays off. Its height is off and stays off.
3. **A third document**, one sheet, one hand, three copies, in three places: the press of the Seat of Sallowby, a lane at the bottom of a hill in the hands of a man of thirty-four who cannot read it, and a table under the chain-book at the Anner Pit. **It names two sills, puts eleven feet above each, and writes down that the two lines are four feet apart, that no house in this valley stands on those four feet, and that nobody in this valley has agreed to the difference and nobody has refused it.** It cost five pence.
4. **The four pages** of the hundred and fifty-fifth day, on the wall, still correct on their face, not touched by the charter, and the second line of them still a road.
5. **The column, open.** A page of a printed index with a heading of two words, and a copy of its four lines on a wall.
6. **The mine.** A mine. Bound in advance to a figure of a thousand and forty tons written down in a city four years ago by a man who has never been in this valley, to have what goes out of its gate and what comes in written down four times a year in a book the seat keeps, at four days a year of a pitman's time, and it has given up the right to say in a room in a year that the chamber got this valley wrong.
7. **The wage.** Nine shillings a week for the sixteen weeks the slow runs, the three years paid in an envelope, and the whole of the pit's half of it at risk of ending with the charter, which a woman of thirty-eight said out loud before she was asked.
8. **The position beneath the basin.** A bearing, a depth, a page number, in two books whose two writers cannot read one another's entry. **Four hundred and six feet is the pit and is not the void, and the void is more by a quantity nobody in this basin has an instrument for naming, and the last hundred and forty feet of the water at the low end of the pit has been under to the roof since before a woman of twenty-six was seventeen, and nobody went down.**

### THE SIXTY TONS, WHICH IS A QUESTION AND NOT A SCANDAL, AND THE FIRST NUMBER IN A MONTH THAT GOT SMALLER

**The chamber's figure for this basin's trade in the eighth year is one thousand and forty tons and it is four years old and a stranger's. This basin has been saying about eleven hundred tons for nineteen years. The difference is sixty tons and at seven pounds a ton that is four hundred and twenty pounds a year, which is five and seven-tenths in the hundred of the seven thousand two hundred and eighty pounds the seven pounds a ton comes to.** The finding of Chapter 243 is that the sixty tons is probably not sixty tons of anything: **there is no weigh-book at the pit, at Sallowby, in the Seat's room or in a cart shed, a ton in the chamber's book is a ton on paper and a ton in this valley is whatever came off a cart that day, and neither of the two men was lying.** Sixty tons is twelve hundred hundredweights, and at a hundredweight and fourpence a hundredweight of salt at the pans that is two hundred and one thousand six hundred pence, which is eight hundred and forty pounds, and nobody in this basin has ever bought twelve hundred hundredweights of anything. **A man of sixty-one has counted what goes into his own pans every day for thirty years and has never once been asked what comes up off a road, and nobody in this valley has written down a single load coming in.**

### THE PEOPLE OF THE BASIN AT THE END OF CHAPTER 250, AND NONE OF THEM IS AN ANTAGONIST

**Ilma Treen, thirty-eight**, keeps the sluice and has for eleven years, nine shillings a week for the sixteen weeks the slow runs, four shillings and sixpence of it from the pit and four from the seat, and she now knows what the water did at her own frame in the eleventh year and put a figure in chalk on the north post of it in her own hand. **Jory Vellan, fifty-two**, under-manager, nineteen years, who bought a date and said so, and who gave up in public the right to say the chamber got this valley wrong, and who put three of the four barrows himself. **Hollis Nance, forty-four**, clerk of the Seat, who said nobody has hidden anything twice and said it harder the second time, and who has a bundle with a knife-cut end on it on a shelf and a good name spent in public and a chair that goes back against a table leg. **Wenna Skell, thirty-three**, scrivener, six hundred and forty deeds in eleven years, who copied the four lines out fair for the lane and was not asked to. **Fen Tregerran, sixty-one**, who keeps the pans and the count of saltings and was asked what comes up off a road and gave the only sentence in this volume that has never been said before. **Tarin Keel**, who counts carts, has counted them for nineteen years, and who is right in four chapters running and wrong in none. **Coll Ferren, thirty-four**, who cannot read the paper he reads out forty times a week in a road, and who said the finding about a paper nobody is on. **Garrow Lee**, the joiner, with his rule, who has spent nine years trying to get a line of chalk measured and who said the volume's last civic finding. **Brann Slee, fifty-six**, who does roofs and who asked the first question anybody asked the man of thirty-one. **Sera Quill, twenty-eight**, recordwright, who has not put a hand to a thing in this valley and was not going to. **Sable Venn**, who came on foot from the north with a board, and who is not on any paper in this valley and did not ask to be.

### THE THREE CLOCKS, AND WHAT THE BATCH ADDED TO THEM

**The pan-book year of fifty saltings, more or less, a salting being a drying, seven weeks in a dry year and eleven in a wet one, and no length in a salting. The turn, twelve years from the first shaking, turn nought being the first shaking. The day, a day out of the water-book since the second shaking, which is the only date in this basin that can go on the outside of a bag.** None of the three converts into any other and the finding that none does was made once, at `202:45`, in a salt-pan's mouth. **What this batch added is the first document in this basin that is dated in a way its own readers can check: the charter's review date is a reference to the head of a page in the water-book and not a week, a month or a turn, and the basin put a date in chalk on a post and did not write the eleventh year on it.**

### THE THIRTY-TWO PAPERS, THE FORTY-ONE, THE NINE ANSWERS AND THE SIXTY TONS, WHICH ARE THREE HOLES IN A RECORD AND NOT A HOLE IN A WALL

Forty-one papers out of the gap in the wall in nine years and nine answers, of which two were the three rulings in one bundle, so thirty-two were never answered at all. **The second paper went into the gap on the first day of the fifty-sixth week, paid for by the Anner out of the Anner's own road cost, with the amount and the payer and the thing wanted written on the outside of it in a clerk's hand, and a man with a bag comes twice in the year and what went in that gap cannot come back before the eighteenth day from now and the chapter that says so is the chapter in which the answer does not arrive.** The first of the four lines is a return of the water in the Sallow at the frame at Hollow Sallow, taken in the eleventh year, made for the chamber's own use, and the water stood at two feet four inches above the sill of the frame on three days together, and the mark by which it is known is on the inside of the north post of the frame and there is nobody living in this valley who put a rule to it. The third of the four is a sheet of the tonnage and the prices of the trade of this basin in the eighth year. The fourth of the four is a printed notice of one line: no matter of a Seat is to be sent out of its own district.

### THE ELEVEN FEET, AND WHERE IT IS AND WHERE IT IS NOT, AND WHY

**It is in a third document in three copies in three places, with both sills named, on the strength of the chamber's own second ruling of nine years ago that no order may name a height without naming the sill it is taken from.** The line in the bond is eleven feet above the sill of the Anner's own dam. The line in the new document is eleven feet above the sill of the bed below the village of Hollow Sallow, which is thirteen feet below the sill of the frame at the sluice, which is nine feet above the sill of the Anner's own dam, and is therefore four feet below that sill. The difference is four feet. **AND THE WHOLE OF THE DIFFERENCE IS THE DISTANCE BETWEEN THE TWO SILLS THE DOCUMENT NAMES, which is the reason the document is a bad answer and not a good one: two lines that are both eleven feet are as far apart as the ground between them, and the ground between them is four feet.** **THE CHAIN "THIRTEEN FEET APART, THIRTEEN LESS ELEVEN IS TWO FEET BELOW THE FRAME'S SILL, TWO ABOVE AGAINST TWO BELOW" THAT STOOD HERE UNTIL 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 WAS A NON-SEQUITUR AND COULD NOT YIELD FOUR FROM THE FIGURES IT NAMED. IT IS REPLACED BY THE RULED GEOMETRY IN THE TABLE BELOW, WHICH IS THE AUTHORITY.**

**It is NOT on the eleven-hand settlement, and the reason is not a secret and is not anybody's fault: the settlement is eleven people's and the height is the one thing about it that eleven people do not agree on, and there are three hands on a struck line under it and they do not agree, and a man who is not one of the eleven put a pencil in a coat in the fifty-fourth week and did not put the figure in and he was right.**

**AND THE FINDING OF CHAPTER 247 IS THE ONE THAT MAKES THE ARITHMETIC OF IT A HEIGHT AND NOT A NUMBER: a return of a river is not a height. Two feet four inches above the sill of the frame is twenty-eight inches, and the frame's sill is a hundred and eight inches ABOVE the Anner's, and a hundred and eight and twenty-eight is a hundred and thirty-six inches, which is eleven feet and four inches, and the highest the water has ever stood at that frame is eleven feet four inches above the sill of the Anner's own dam, which is **four inches above the line in the bond**, and the water did that on three days together in the eleventh year with nobody in this valley deciding anything at all. **THE SIGN, THE MINUS AND THE SIX FEET EIGHT INCHES THAT STOOD HERE UNTIL 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 WERE WRONG AND ARE CORRECTED HERE, AND CHAPTER 247 NOW SAYS THAT THE RIVER EXCEEDED THE HEIGHT THE VALLEY HAS BEEN ARGUING ABOUT, WHICH IS THE FINDING THE CHAPTER WAS BUILT TO CARRY.** The chamber's ruling says do not put a number down without saying what you are measuring it from, and a valley which has spent a month with one figure of eleven feet in it had never once been told what the water does.**

### THE EIGHTEEN FINDINGS THIS VOLUME HAS NOW MADE, ONCE EACH, AND WHAT CARRIES THEM WHOLE

1. **The two calendars do not convert** — 202, in a salt-pan's mouth. **2. A deed is an agreement between two papers** — 203, in a scrivener's mouth. **3. The person who holds the burden in this basin is the worst-paid person in it and has done the most good** — 204, in her own mouth. **4. A contract keeps a thing true by costing somebody money, and it stopped costing and nothing changed** — 205, in ochre dust. **5. Eleven households are not a category, and a height means two different things four miles apart** — 206, in a joiner's mouth. **6. The quick ruling is four complete lines with no hole in any of them, and that is the problem** — 207. **7. A record can be perfectly kept and still be wrong about a place** — 208, said the first time about a building and the second time, in Chapter 248, about four people standing in a room, and the second is better and is in a worse voice. **8. The first missing anchor position, identified by two accurate records that do not meet** — 209. **9. The new question, carried whole** — 210. **10. The second line of the order is a schedule and not a river** — 238, in a carter's mouth. **11. The excess of the claims over the whole of the trade is exactly the settlement** — 239. **12. A document in a valley is administered by being read aloud by the person it is about, and the reader is the copy** — 231. **13. A thing that is true in every room and absent from every page is a thing that has no owner, and there are about nine of them and one of them is a height** — 235, in a woman's mouth in a lane. **14. A good name is a public thing and it is free and it cannot be spent by the people it is about** — 236, in nine words from an under-manager and again in nine words from a recordwright on the way out. **15. The first thing a valley does with a withheld record is to ask somebody who has been there, and there is nobody** — 241, in a cart yard. **16. A second paper puts a second date on a matter and a second date is a thing a bench can use to close a first one** — 242, by an under-manager who has been in the building. **17. A figure four years old cannot be reconciled to a memory of nineteen years by anybody in the valley, and nobody has ever counted what comes up off a road** — 243, and a man of sixty-one. **18. The standing of a valley is not in a person but in a document, and a paper nobody is on cannot be asked anybody anything** — 244, by a man of thirty-four who cannot read the paper it is about. **AND THE TWO OF CHAPTERS 245 TO 250: a rule-free valley cannot be given anything, and the honest name for a person in that position is a person who is not in the instruments** — 246; **and a bad answer in three copies can be argued with by three people and a good answer in one can be argued with by nobody** — 249, in a joiner's mouth, and it is the volume's last civic finding.**

### THE SIX THINGS A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE THEM, AND TWO OF THEM ARE NEW IN THIS BLOCK

1. **A day-phrase sweep is a legality check and not a truth check, and this batch is the third in a row in which a date was legal, in a legal band, and wrong.** The third question — *is the named day the day the event happened on?* — caught two in this batch and the pattern could not see either. **The cheapest second instrument for this volume's calendar is to put every chapter's own day next to every day-phrase in it and read the two lists side by side, which is ten rows against nineteen and takes a minute.**
2. **A money chapter with a long running figure in it will drive the count of the word NINE up, and the fix is not to abbreviate the figure.** Batch 0004's arithmetic drove that count to one hundred and five; this batch's nineteen roofs drove it to one hundred and twenty-four in the first writing and twenty-six of those were cut, and what was cut was the phrase and not the figure and the working is still printed in full and in order.
3. **THE WITHIN-BATCH DUPLICATE SCAN IS NOT ENOUGH AND THE CROSS-BATCH SCAN IS THE ONE THAT CATCHES A CARRY-OVER.** Both return zero over Chapters 241–250 at forty characters and above, and a chapter can pass both and still have carried a line over from Chapter 233, and this batch's one was caught only because a scan was run against Chapters 201–240 and not only against the ten files in front of it. **Run the cross-batch scan on every batch. It costs one command and it is the instrument that caught five of Batch 0004's review findings.**
4. **A block-quoted document is prose in capitals and the uppercase scan looks at it.** One uppercase CORRECT was in this batch's first writing inside its own charter, and the prohibition on the 179-and-189 shape is a rule about the whole file and not about the running prose.
5. **A narrator's word list will always have a blind spot and the blind spot this batch had was the word BOOK.** The list carried *the manuscript* and *the manuscript* did not match *this book*, and one of the six instances this batch caught is a narrator pointing at the volume it is inside. **A sweep that reports zero is a fact about a range and not about the book, and the range is stated every time.**
6. **A CHAPTER PROMPT CAN BE WRONG ABOUT A CHARACTER'S SEX AND THE CHAPTER FILE IS THE FACT.** The Batch 0005 card for Chapter 250 says *a man of twenty-six is at the pit* in a sentence about her, and the prompt's own preamble and the repaired Chapter 240 both establish that the person is Alo Vearn, a woman of twenty-six. This is the second time in two batches that a hand-off document has been wrong about her and the state file has been the thing that was wrong the first time.

The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the seventeen handed-on threads is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0004 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**
