The receipt is `state/current.md` §14, in the Batch 0004 block, and it is the authority on every line number in this one. The repair changed no plot, no finding, no turn, no ending and no scheduled chapter; it fixed eleven P0 defects, eleven P1 and eight P2, and left one P1 standing on purpose and says which. WHAT IS CORRECTED IN THIS BLOCK, AND EVERY FIGURE BELOW WAS RE-MEASURED AGAINST `chapters/volume-05/chapter-0231.md` TO `chapter-0240.md` AND NOT READ OUT OF A LIST: **the day-count is THREE instances in this batch, not two — `235:53` (183), `239:59` (187) and `240:21` (188) — and the volume total is FIFTEEN, not fourteen; about thirty people in the basin know the column exists, not four; nineteen roofs, not twenty; ten debts on the Shelf half of the penny exercise book, not six, and five days, not six weeks; the person at the pit is Alo Vearn, a woman of twenty-six; and the new question is NOT raised anywhere in this batch, which the delivered text broke once at `240:21` and the repair put right without replacing it with a gesture at it.** Two things no permitted editor may fix are recorded in `state/current.md` §10: `workspace/volume-05/batch-0004/.done` does not exist and the next invocation will re-select this batch, and `state/phase-ledger.json` still reads `batch-0002`.


### THE GROUND AND THE WATER IN IT AS IT STANDS AT THE END OF CHAPTER 240

**The Sallow is running. The water-book has a second hand in it and a penny a day behind it, and the girl of seventeen writes the day at the top of every page. The gate at Hollow Sallow is hung and opens at the closing of the water. The Anner dam is at eleven feet to the Anner's own sill and four men and a barrow are putting forty loads of clay on it in the order they have gone on in for eleven years.** **Nineteen roofs are wanted, two are on or half on, and the fifth came off on the fourth of the fifty-fifth week and went on about four days later than the first did, and there are about a hundred and forty days of this weather in front of it.** Two houses in the bottom half of the lane are not going to stand through a winter and nobody has money to pay a shilling an hour for a man to look at a wall. **The causeway at Hollow Sallow is shut and has been for about a fortnight, and the high road is carrying about nine carts a day with a morning and a half added each way, and the Anner bears the whole of it, and as of the second of the fifty-fifth week there were eight, because one cart went over the shoulder.** **The height is still not on the settlement sheet and the eleventh notch of the volume has not been cut and no one in this basin has thought about the notch this week.**

### THE THING THAT IS TRUE IN EVERY ROOM AND ABSENT FROM EVERY PAGE, WHICH IS THE BATCH'S REAL DISCOVERY

**There are about nine of them in this basin. A woman of thirty-eight named the class at the sixth hour of the first day of the fifty-fifth week in a lane, said she had counted them twice, said she was not counting them a third time, said nobody in that lane asked her which nine, and named one of them: a height.** One of the nine was found on the evening of the same day by a girl of seventeen in her own hand in her own book — a figure of the water against the frame at Hollow Sallow that she wrote off the frame herself at the closing of the water and that nobody asked her for, and there is nowhere in this basin to put a number a person wrote themselves. **The class is not a conspiracy and not an error and not a sin. It is a thing that has no owner, and the honest list is four or five of them and the chapter does not print the other four.** The ones this batch names: the eleven days a sluice is opened early every year that nobody was told to; the two hours the mill hand and the Seat's hand disagree about the frame by, both of them written down and neither of them looked at; the tonnage of this valley's trade, which is in a building nine days away and was not in this valley; the height; and a number a girl of seventeen wrote off a frame.

### THE SEALED COLUMN, AND IT IS THE FIRST TIME IN THIS VOLUME THAT ANYBODY HAS NAMED IT OUT LOUD

**The Seat's own hand-book in Sallowby is two inches thick and has been kept up to date for nine years by one clerk alone. It has four columns and the heading of the fourth is printed and not written and it is two words, and the second of them is the ordinary word a clerk writes at the head of a column of things that did not go, and about four hundred and sixty entries are in the three columns beside it, going back to the first shaking in a small hard hand that gets worse as it comes forward, and one rule runs under all four, and under the fourth there is nothing at all.** A clerk of forty-four named it on the sixth day of the fifty-fourth week in an ordinary voice, the way he says a price, and said that he had had his eye on that heading every working day since he came, because it is the last page and you cannot tell a finished book from an unfinished one without it.

**The chamber's own printed index came up the high road in a rider's bag on the same morning and it is the original of which the hand-book is a hand-copy, and under the same heading it has four lines and no more, and every one of the four is about this basin.** The hand-copy is empty and the original is not, and the reason is not concealment: **a hand-copy of what was sent has nothing to copy, and the material under a column headed not sent is exactly the material that was not sent.**

**The four lines, as read out in a room with about seven people in it:**
1. **A return of the water in the Sallow at the frame at Hollow Sallow, taken in the eleventh year, made for the chamber's own use. There is a figure in it. Nobody in this valley has seen that figure, including the woman who opens the frame, and nobody in this batch does.**
2. **A list of the papers the Seat of Sallowby has sent to the chamber, being forty-one, and of the answers, being nine. Thirty-two of them were never answered at all. Nobody in this valley knew that anybody counted.**
3. **A sheet of the tonnage and the prices of the trade of this basin in the eighth year, which is four years back, being one thousand and forty tons, at four pounds a ton at the mouth and eleven at the market.**
4. **A printed notice of one line, which is the rule the first of the three rulings was made under, and the rule is that no matter of a Seat is to be sent out of its own district.** This basin has that rule on a wall in its own words and has had it for nine years and has never once read it as a reason for anything.

**A rider's bag with nine printed sheets and a return folded twice at the bottom of it is on a shelf under the window in a room with a press in it, beside a bundle with a knife-cut end on the string that has been on that shelf since the second of the fifty-first week. Neither is in the press, because the press is for deeds and bonds and things that are owed, and a printed sheet is not owed to anybody. A man of thirty-one put his hand on the table about a foot from the printed index on the sixth of the fifty-fourth week and did not touch it, and nobody in that room saw him decide anything, and no person in this basin has asked him when he is going to.**

### THE PEOPLE OF THE BASIN AFTER CHAPTER 240

**Nobody in this basin has forgiven anybody anything and nobody has been forgiven for anything.** About nine people in a valley of about four hundred and ten are frightened of a bench of four they have never met, and being frightened is not a matter and there is nowhere in this basin to enter it, and about four of the nine have said so out loud in four places. **The Anner is to be asked again in one year, and asked to be asked, and the asking is a year off.** A woman of thirty-eight keeps the sluice, is paid nine shillings a week in full and on the day, and has been paid three years of arrears in an envelope. A girl of seventeen keeps the second hand of the water-book at a penny a day and has begun a third book of her own with one line in it. A man of thirty-four keeps the copy of the settlement in a lane, cannot read it, reads it out at the closing of the water, and has decided that he will read it out of his own head if he does not have the paper in his hand, and nobody stopped him and nobody stopped him when he had it either. A man of fifty-six does roofs and does not split nine pounds. A man of fifty-two is under-manager and holds the height and has worked out at about four in the morning that a good name is a public thing and it is free. **A road in chalk on the side of a cart at Sallowby has about nine men's hands on it and no money behind it, and a board with a sum on it is against the outside of a cart shed and is going in about a month.**

### THE THREE CLOCKS, UNCHANGED, AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID TO THEM

**The Shelf keeps a grain year of fifty-two weeks. The Red Basin keeps a pan-book year of fifty saltings more or less, a turn counted from the first shaking, and a day out of a water-book counted from the second shaking, and none of the three converts into any of the others, and the finding that there is no conversion was made ONCE, at `202:45`, in a salt-pan's mouth, and forty-eight chapters are about what people do about a thing that has already been found out.** The turn in this batch was written on paper for the first time — the eleventh year on the chamber's return, the eighth year on the sheet of tonnage — **and nobody in this valley can place either of them, and a man of thirty-one said so out loud at `239:61` and did not put the only date he had on it, because a man may put a day on his own act and may not put one on another person's speech, and what he did that evening was write a sum down.** **The grain year is not turned in this batch and no chapter of it says the year turns.**

### THE DOCUMENTS, OF WHICH THIS BATCH ADDED ONE, AND THE TOTAL IN THE VOLUME IS ELEVEN

1. **The four-page order of the hundred and fifty-fifth day.** On the wall of the Seat's one room, four complete lines, correct on its face, and nobody in Hollow Sallow had read it until a woman of about forty read the second line out loud in a lane on the fourth of the fifty-fifth week. Not block-quoted again.
2. **The eleven-hand settlement sheet.** On the same wall. Six lines, a struck line with three hands on it that do not agree, and a space at the foot where a name used to be and is not now, and nothing in the space and no dash in it. Not block-quoted again in either version.
3. **The lane copy of that sheet.** In the hands of a man of thirty-four, who cannot read it, and it has a seventh line with no figure on it that is not on the paper in Sallowby. **As of Chapter 231 the finding about it is that it has no second copy and its reader is the copy.**
4. **The abstract of three rulings.** Folded in four printed sheets with a clerk's letter tied into the fourth, both on a shelf in the Seat's one room, neither opened. Not block-quoted again.
5. **The standing order of the sluice.** Not block-quoted again.
6. **The bond of forty pounds.** In the press, under a floor at Coldbeck. Not block-quoted again.
7. **The chain-book of the Anner.** A hundred and thirty years old, kept in a hand that is not the under-manager's, never checked against a deed, and now carrying the Anner's money in four lines in the under-manager's own hand that about four men in the working years can read and none of them will read again.
8. **The water-book.** A hundred years old, four hands, and since the settlement a second hand paid a penny a day, and not one of the four original hands has ever stood at the frame.
9. **The Seat's own hand-book.** Two inches thick, four columns, a printed heading of two words at the head of the fourth, about four hundred and sixty entries in the three beside it and nothing under the fourth. **Block-quoted in Chapter 233 and not quoted again.**
10. **The list of drains.** A man of twenty-two, and the ninth line wanted a turn against it and has had it since the fourth of the fifty-first week.
11. **The chamber's own return of the eighth year.** Folded twice, found at the bottom of a rider's bag, dated in a turn nobody here can place, and carrying the only figure anybody anywhere has ever written down about what this valley trades. **Described in prose in Chapter 235 and not block-quoted.**

**And the new book: a third book begun by a girl of seventeen, with one line in it, and the line is a number she wrote herself off a frame and there is nowhere in this basin to put a number a person wrote themselves.**

### THE FINDINGS OF THIS BATCH, ONCE EACH

1. A document administered by being read aloud is not a ceremony, and the copy kept in a lane by a man who cannot read it has no second copy, and the reader is the copy (231).
2. A route that does not go through Orison exists, it is four miles of shoulder and a bend at the second ford and a market four days short with no court in it, and the gate at the top of the pass is the reason, and the water-seat is the reason there is a seat and not the other way round (232).
3. The word that means not sent is a printed heading that a clerk has had his eye on every working day for nine years, and the material under it is about this basin, and it was not sent to this basin (233).
4. Thirty-two papers went nine days up a road and were never answered, and nobody in this valley knew anybody counted, and nobody has hidden anything, and not being sent is a worse thing for a valley than being lied to because nobody did it (233).
5. The basin's answer to a record that was not sent to it is to go on doing the work, and that is neither indifference nor heroism (234).
6. Being frightened of a bench you have never met is not a matter and cannot be entered anywhere, because a person cannot be entered anywhere for a thing they have not done (234, 236).
7. A thing that is true in every room and absent from every page has no owner, and there are about nine of them in this basin and one of them is a height (235).
8. The chamber holds the only figure anybody has ever written down about what this valley trades, and it is sixty tons out on this basin's own memory, and nobody can say where sixty tons went (235).
9. A mine with a sheet has a standing interest in a number, and a number a body pays every year to keep true is a number that body is frightened of losing (235).
10. A good name is a public thing and it is free, and a public thing that costs nothing cannot be spent by the people it is about (236).
11. A name travels four miles in about two years on about four mouths and there is nothing anybody could produce if anybody wanted to argue about it, and no pass may supply a column for it (237).
12. A piece of paper that four hundred and ten people have never read all the way through depends on where a cart goes, and a carter found that out from a woman reading a line aloud in a lane (238).
13. The settlement and a second road are the same money counted once, and the excess of the claims over the whole of the trade is exactly the settlement, to the penny, and there is nothing behind the difference (239).
14. A copy is a document. Not the same copy twice (240).

### WHAT A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE IT

1. **A day-phrase sweep is a legality check and not a truth check.** `237:47` named the sixth day of the fifty-second week for an event of the fourth day of the fifty-first week. Both were inside a legal band. The pattern cannot tell you whether the named day is the day the event happened on. **Put every chapter's own day next to every day-phrase in it and read the two lists side by side.**
2. **A number printed beside a sentence is a claim about the sentence.** This batch's first writing had a hand-book that had been kept up for "nine years" in a sentence that is also a man saying *since I came*, and a gap of "five days" where 187 − 183 is three, and a fraction of "fourteen out of a hundred and eighty-seven" that is not a statement about anything. **Read the sentence, not the table.**
3. **The word NINE is at one hundred and five over this batch against ninety-eight, eighty-eight and eighty-four over the three before it, and the reason is one chapter's arithmetic and not carelessness.** Fifteen of the twenty-five stand inside a single subtraction in Chapter 239 and the phrase *nine hundred and ninety-eight thousand four hundred* is printed twelve times because the number being subtracted from has to be printed every time it is used. **A batch that finds this count high and cuts the digits out of an arithmetic has made the money uncheckable, and the direction of that trade is always the wrong way.**
4. **A case-insensitive weekday or month scan is a different instrument from the case-sensitive one, and the volume's CORRECT instrument is the case-sensitive one.** A case-insensitive CORRECT scan over this batch returns one hit and it is the ordinary English word in an ordinary sentence.
5. **The basin's day-count instances are spelled out in the prose and not printed in digits, exactly as Chapter 210 printed one, and a scan for three digits returns ZERO and would certify this batch's day-count at nothing.** Measure the words.
6. **Nine days' ride is about four hundred miles, and this batch put both figures in the same mouth on the same day, from a carter, who is the only person in the fiction entitled to reconcile them.** A later pass that wants to use the miles does not have to argue with the days.

## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0003 — Chapters 221–230, *After The Water, And The Ordinary People*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0002` block below it, which is archive and is not edited. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the fourteen handed-on threads is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0003 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

### THE GROUND AND THE WATER IN IT AS IT STANDS AT THE END OF CHAPTER 230

The Sallow has been out of the lane of Hollow Sallow since about the seventh day of the fifty-second week. The mud is firm enough to walk in the lane by the second hour. **Nineteen houses are ruined and under repair, and about a hundred and forty people live in them, and they are no longer under water and are not any the less ruined for it, and nobody in the basin has a word that holds both.** The rest of the village had a foot and a half in the lane and had it out by the noon light. **Nineteen roofs: one is off and back on, the second is coming off this week, and the rest are timbered in part and waiting.** Two houses in the bottom half of the lane are not going to stand through a winter and one of them is going to have to come down and be put up again. **The gate at Hollow Sallow is hung, at five pounds eighteen, and opens at the closing of the water, and does the only thing a gate in that frame has ever been able to do.** The Anner dam is at eleven feet to the Anner's own sill and the wet workings are dry. **The height is not on the settlement sheet.**

### THE PEOPLE OF THE BASIN AFTER THE SETTLEMENT

**Eleven hands are on a sheet on the wall of the Seat's one room, and the copy in the lane is in one hand, and the copy is read out in the lane.** Coll Ferren, thirty-four, four people, is the eleventh hand and keeps the lane copy and cannot read it. Ilma Treen, thirty-eight, is the sluice, is paid nine shillings a week in full and on the day by the Anner, and has been paid three years of arrears in an envelope. **Elsa Quint, seventeen, is the second hand of the water-book, is paid a penny a day for every day the water runs, writes the day at the head of every page, and has the basin's day-count from a man of thirty-one and has never asked him where the number came from.** Brann Slee, fifty-six, is on the second roof. Garrow Lee, forty-one, hung the gate. Jory Vellan, fifty-two, pays for all of it and asked to be asked again in a year. Wenna Skell, thirty-three, copied both sheets for nothing. Ewan Troke, twenty-nine, is not in this batch. Fen Tregerran, sixty-one, put a hand to the sheet and refused to date it. Hollis Nance, forty-four, wrote both fair copies and named the count of eleven out loud and did not carry the paper to Orison. **Sera Quill, twenty-eight, put no hand to anything and witnessed the copies and said her family's claim of six years is not filed and is not going to be filed in a valley.**

### THE THREE CLOCKS, UNCHANGED, AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID TO THEM

The pan-book year: fifty saltings, a drying of seven weeks in a dry year and eleven in a wet one. The turn: a year from the first shaking, turn nought, this year turn twelve. The day: a day since the second shaking, out of the water-book. **They do not convert, the finding was made once at `202:45` in a salt-pan's mouth, and this batch is the first in the volume in which the basin put its own day-count onto a document eleven other people wrote, at `225:59`, and the reason is that the only man in the basin whose count has never been out refused to date a paper about water in a count of dryings, in nine words, and put his hand to it anyway and said a hand and a date are two different acts.**

### THE DOCUMENTS, OF WHICH THIS BATCH ADDED TWO, AND THE TOTAL IN THE VOLUME IS TEN

1. The chain-book copy of the marl-strip deed (`201`).
2. The two halves of the marl-strip deed (`203`).
3. The bond, forty pounds, lodged in the eleventh salting of the fortieth year and void since the death of the man who lodged it (`205`).
4. The four-page order of the water-seat, the hundred and fifty-fifth day, four complete lines, on a wall in Sallowby, with a copy in the lane kept by a person of the lane (`207`).
5. A printed abstract of three chamber rulings (`208`).
6. A chain-book page of a trench and six marks (`209`).
7. The standing order of the sluice, made in the eleventh salting of the forty-ninth year, handed to the keeper and not copied (`212`).
8. A page of the Seat's water-book in two hands (`216`).
9. **THE DRAFT OF A REMEDY, seven lines with the first struck through and left on the paper, and three words under a name at the foot (`226:9`–`17`).** The name is out. **The space is not filled and is not dashed and no chapter may fill it or dash it or have anybody put it back for him.**
10. **THE SHEET OF THE ELEVENTH HAND, six lines, one hand, with three hands on a struck line that do not agree, printed on the face of the paper (`228:13`–`20`).**
**AND TWO DOCUMENTS THAT ARE NOT BLOCK-QUOTED AND MAY NOT BE: the list of nineteen answers, which has no heading, no addresses and no date, and is under a board in a cart shed with a stone on it; and the seven lines a man of thirty-four added in a road, of which the seventh has no figure on it and exists only in a lane.**

### THE FINDINGS OF THIS BATCH, ONCE EACH

1. A list of what nineteen people need is not a list of work, and eighteen of the nineteen answers cannot be bought (`221`).
2. The roof is second (`221`).
3. A list of houses is a list of people, and the next house is the house that is not on the list (`222`).
4. A list with addresses on it can be argued against the wrong door in a person's own front door (`222`).
5. The thing the mine can pay is not the difficulty: two and three-tenths in the hundred (`223`).
6. A mine that pays a wage once a week is a different kind of mine, and that is not a reason to refuse (`223`).
7. It is not the four and six; it is the day the money comes on (`224`).
8. A book kept by people who have not stood at the frame keeps what they were told (`224`).
9. A man who lost a house is not a house (`225`).
10. A paper with a man's name on it is read out in a year when he is gone (`225`).
11. Nobody said it out loud to him, and that is the finding (`226`, `227`).
12. A copy kept in a lane by a man who cannot read it is a different document every time it is read out (`226`).
13. A paper with no name on it cannot be asked anybody anything, and he wanted his name on it for that reason (`227`).
14. A change made aloud cannot be attributed to a hand (`228`).
15. A document can acquire a line in a road in four seconds and a clerk of the Seat cannot do one single thing about it, because the man who acquired it is a person of the lane (`228`).
16. Two words and one sentence cost nothing and the sentence has no figure and will not get one (`228`).
17. The settlement costs more than the flood, and a hair under one part in eight of the whole thing is not roofs (`229`).
18. The paper is not going to Orison, and the reason is a bench that is seized of the matter and a clerk who has found a reason not to spend five pounds and six in nine years (`229`).
19. A book that has kept the same four hands for a hundred years has kept what those four were told (`224`, and the same fact at `229`).
20. **Not one of them names the height, and he had a pencil out and did not put it in (`230`).**

### WHAT A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE IT

1. **THE RUN IS TWENTY-NINE DAYS, NOT NINETEEN AND NOT THIRTY.** Arrival is basin day 149 and Chapter 230 is basin day 178, and 178 − 149 = **29**. The Batch 0002 record's nineteen days was right for Chapter 220 and is wrong here, and the same error was made and repaired once in this volume already, at the review of Batch 0002, where three prose figures and one record figure were corrected from seventeen to nineteen. **This item's own heading said thirty until 26 September 2026, which is the fourth time this project has written a heading that its own arithmetic in the next nine words contradicts, and the fix for that class is to write the subtraction before the word that names it.**
2. **THE RECURRING FIGURE OF THE SETTLEMENT IS £178 13s 4d AND NOT £171 16s 8d.** 41,040d + 1,728d + 112d = 42,880d; 42,880 − 42,720 = 160d = 13s 4d. **The first writing of this batch added 88d, which is not a figure in this basin, and the outline and the prose were both corrected at the site before delivery.**
3. **THE FIRST YEAR IS £195 7s 4d AND NOT £188 10s 8d**, and the excess over the flood is **£24 7s 4d AND NOT £17 10s 8d**: 46,888d − 41,040d = 5,848d; 5,848 ÷ 240 = 24 remainder 88d.
4. **THE EXCESS IN SLUICE-KEEPER YEARS IS THREE YEARS AND ABOUT SIX WEEKS, NOT TWO AND SEVEN WEEKS**, and the check is 5,848 ÷ 1,728 = 3.38 and the remainder is 664d ÷ 108d = 6.15. **The figure of about twenty-four years, which is the whole flood against her whole year, is 41,040 ÷ 1,728 = 23.75 and it is a DIFFERENT NUMBER for a DIFFERENT THING and the two may not be swapped.**
5. **THE SECOND HAND OF THE WATER-BOOK COSTS 9s 4d A YEAR AND IS ABOUT A FIFTEENTH OF THE WAGE, NOT A FOURTEENTH**: 112d; 1,728 ÷ 112 = 15.4.
6. **THE ELEVEN HANDS ARE ELEVEN PEOPLE AND ARE NOT THE ELEVEN OF ANYTHING ELSE**, and the chapter says so out loud at `227:62` in a clerk's mouth. **A later pass that puts an eleventh house, an eleventh mark or an eleventh foot on that sheet is undoing the one joke this volume is allowed.**
7. **THE INTERVAL FROM THE COUNTING TO COLL FERREN'S REFUSAL IS EIGHT DAYS, NOT ELEVEN: 173 − 165 = 8**, and `225:3` prints the subtraction.
8. **THE GRAIN YEAR IS NOT TURNED IN THIS BATCH AND MAY NOT BE TURNED WITHOUT A DECISION.** Shelf day 91 is the first day of a new grain year on the Shelf, because week fifty-two is days eighty-four to ninety; Chapter 220 says the other side's year turns in about forty weeks and refuses to print the figure; **this batch says nothing about the turn and puts the four hundred and eighty-five pounds only in a list of six debts that are not stopping.**
9. **THE SHEET HAS SIX LIVE LINES IN SALLOWBY AND SEVEN IN THE LANE, and the seventh has no figure on it, and the difference between the two sheets is a man of thirty-four's mouth and cannot be attributed to a hand.** No chapter resolves it and none may.
10. **THE COUNT OF PEOPLE WHO HAVE NOW BEEN NAMED IN VOLUME 05 IS FIVE, NOT FOUR**, and the fifth is Coll Ferren at `221:41`, and the class is defined in `outline/volume-05.md` guardrail seven and in `state/current.md` §8.6.
11. **A DATE INSIDE A LEGAL WEEK BAND CAN STILL BE THE WRONG DAY OF THAT WEEK, AND THE DAY-PHRASE INSTRUMENT CANNOT SEE IT.** The review of this batch found `222:51` naming the second day of the fifty-first week for an event that happened on the fifth day of it, and `state/current.md` §1 had certified it, because the sweep asked two questions — is the day number of seven or less, and is the named week inside its own band — and both answers were yes. **A third question is required and no regex can ask it: is the named day the day the event happened on.** The four calendar facts a later pass needs and cannot get from a pattern: **the flood was the third night of the fifty-second week and is *last week* from Chapters 221 to 227 and *the week before last* from 228 to 230, and `230:9` is right and the other seven were not; the nineteen answers were taken on the sixth day of the fifty-second week; the counting of the nineteen houses was at the first light on the fourth day of the fifty-second week, which is basin day one hundred and sixty-five, and 165 + 8 = 173, which is `225`'s day; and the girl of seventeen read the four pages out to a neighbour with one eye on the fifth day of the fifty-first week, which is basin day one hundred and fifty-nine, and that is Chapter 211 and not a day in this batch.** **The cheapest second instrument for this volume's calendar is to write every chapter's own day in a column and every day-phrase in it in a second column and read the two side by side.**
12. **A NUMBER PRINTED BESIDE A SENTENCE IS A CLAIM ABOUT THE SENTENCE AND NOT ABOUT THE WORLD, and five of them in this batch were wrong and one of them was arithmetic.** `223:15` printed 216d twice beside a hundred and seventy-one pounds that is right; `225:37`, `225:3`, `225:5`, `222:23` and `227:11` printed word counts that were not the word counts of the sentences they sat next to. **Every figure in a document or a speech in this volume is checkable by counting, and the project's own money instrument — print the unit price in the sentence, find it, multiply — extends to the volume's other stock device without being asked to, and the extension was not made until a review made it.**

## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0002 — Chapters 211–220, *The Four Pages, And The Water*. **The fact base for everything after it, and it supersedes the `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0001` block below it, which is archive and is not edited. The live record with the map, the instruments, the money with the working and the thirteen handed-on threads is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0002 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the new canon, in one place, with a chapter and a line against every claim, so that a later pass can check a thing instead of believing it.** The standing instruction is unchanged and it is the whole of what makes this usable: **re-run every scan against the chapter files and do not re-read a list.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the new canon, in one place, with a chapter and a line against every claim, so that a later pass can check a thing instead of believing it.** The standing instruction is unchanged and it is the whole of what makes this usable: **re-run every scan against the chapter files and do not re-read a list.**
>
> **AND THE REVIEW OF THIS BATCH REPAIRED FOUR FIGURES IN IT AND IN THE CHAPTERS, and they are corrected here rather than left to be inherited wrong: the four of them arrived on basin day 149, so Chapter 214's day of 162 is THIRTEEN days in the valley, the four pages went on the wall on basin day 155 and the girl of seventeen read them on day 156, which is SEVEN days and SIX days before Chapter 214 (`214:27`, `214:39`, `214:57`; all three said eight); the mill-hand entry of basin day 139 and the slow of basin day 153 are A FORTNIGHT apart exactly and not a fortnight and a day (`212:70`, and this file's live block did not carry it but `state/current.md` section 3 did, with a tick beside the wrong figure); and the run at the end of the batch is NINETEEN days, not seventeen and not sixteen, because 168 − 149 = 19 (`220:33`, `220:61`). The count of nine day-count instances is untouched, because it is a count of instances and not of days. The receipt is `state/current.md` section 14.**

### THE GROUND, AND THE WATER IN IT, AS IT STANDS AT THE END OF CHAPTER 220

**The world is exactly one notch bigger than it was at Chapter 201 and not bigger than that. No new city, no new institution, no new faction, no new magic and no new people with a new name.**

- **The Sallow**, a river that runs about sixteen weeks a year. **IT CAME UP OUT OF THE FLATS ON THE EVENING OF THE SIXTH DAY OF THE FIFTIETH WEEK, which is basin day one hundred and fifty-three, and it had been down six days and six nights by day one hundred and fifty-nine, and it was still the highest anybody could remember on day one hundred and sixty-four (211:17).** It turned in the night of day one hundred and sixty-four into one hundred and sixty-five, and was falling about a foot a day (218:3), and was **three feet in the bed at the Anner on day one hundred and sixty-six (218:69), and a foot and a bit in the bed at Hollow Sallow on day one hundred and sixty-eight, which is where it had been on the fourth day of the fifty-first week (220:3).**
- **The water in the lane, in order and all of it measured in this batch:** two feet on day 159 (211:17); against the ninth doorpost on day 161 (213:3); over the top of the sluice frame on the night of day 164 (216:63); in the lane and impassable to carts by day 166; **the water out of the lane by day 168, with eight of the nineteen houses still under about a foot and a half (220:3, 220:5).**
- **THE SILL STONE AT THE MOUTH OF THE ANNER IS THE DATUM EVERY NUMBER IN THIS BASIN GOES BACK TO, AND THE FRAME AT HOLLOW SALLOW IS NINE FEET ABOVE IT, and the whole of the volume's flood is that subtraction, and Chapter 216 prints it in full where a reader can do it in their head (216:43, 216:45).** There is a third sill: the bed below the village, **thirteen feet below the frame and four feet below the Anner's sill, cut by hand**. **THE SENTENCE THIS BULLET CARRIED UNTIL 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 SAID THE SILL STONE WAS THE HIGH ONE AND THE FRAME NINE FEET BELOW IT AND THE BED A FOOT AND A BIT BELOW THE FRAME. ALL THREE CLAUSES WERE WRONG, THE FIRST TWO INVERTING THE RELATION THAT THE VOLUME'S FLOOD RUNS ON AND THE THIRD CONTRADICTING `204:3` AND `216:23`, WHICH BOTH PUT THE BED NINE FEET BELOW THE SLUICE. SEE THE RULED GEOMETRY IN THE BATCH 0005 TABLE ABOVE.**
- **Hollow Sallow**: about four hundred and ten people, about ninety houses, **nineteen of them under water and about a hundred and forty people in them, for about four months** (217:5, 216:57). **Eleven of the nineteen are on the upper half of the lane and the eight extra ones are houses that came up after the marks were cut, one of them a barn that had a door, a hearth and a family in it by the year the third house from the top was rebuilt (217:9).**
- **Sallowby**: about four hundred people, one market day a week; the water-seat, the pit's head office, a chandler's shop with a room over it, the mill, and **four pages of an order on the wall of the Seat's one room, and a second copy on the doorpost of the third house from the top in Hollow Sallow, and a third copy in Wenna Skell's own hand on a flat board on her window sill (211:35, 214:65, 203:29).**
- **Coldbeck**: the salt-pans, and **the sixth salting came out in the fifty-second week and the pans went in and the pan-book year turned over, with a hundred and sixty-five notches in the upper edge of a board cut one notch a day for thirty years (218:71).** The salt-pan year is a real year and it turned inside this batch, and it is not the Shelf's grain year and it does not convert into it.
- **Anner Pit**: about eleven hundred tons a year, **about four hundred men in the working years and about sixty in the off ones, and four of the four hundred are the four the pit can spare and have been on the dam in the working years of every year since the bond was lodged (216:3).** A dam held to exactly eleven feet all the way along, which took a man with a barrow and a string and most of a morning to make so (215:11).
- **Orison, nine days away**: a city of about four hundred thousand people, a chamber of plenty with about thirty clerks in a building with a yard, and **about thirty of them did their jobs properly and not one of them is going to do a ninth day's work for a basin that has not sent anybody to ask (218:21).**

### THE PEOPLE OF THE RED BASIN AFTER CHAPTER 220, and none of them is an antagonist and none of them was produced

- **Ilma Treen, thirty-eight.** Keeps the sluice, eleven years. **£7 4s a year, of which the Anner gives four and six and the Seat owes four and six for three years, which is £10 16, and she is paid for a hundred and twelve of the hundred and forty days she works, and the twenty-eight is four weeks, and four weeks at four and six is eighteen shillings exactly (212:46, 212:48).** Her house is the third from the top, the highest of the eleven, and it is the driest of the nineteen, and she has water in it. **She is the person who said the volume's finding about the unnamed, in nine words, in her own mouth, at the second hour of day 165: it named a dam, it named a height, it named a day, it never named the water (`217:48`).** She has been opening the sluice eleven days early in every one of eleven years and it is on the record why. **Her whole life in this volume now includes writing *I have no more lamp oil. Nobody came.* in the Seat's own water-book on the night of the flood (`216:64`), and she has never once been asked what she is writing on it (`212:68`), and she is owed £10 16 and has not chased it and will not.**
- **Jory Vellan, fifty-two.** Under-manager, nineteen years, son-in-law to the man who lodged the bond, which is void and has been for eleven years. **He is right, and being right is the whole of the danger.** He made the dam eleven feet exactly all the way along on purpose (`215:11`); he said *I have been given a number and never a reason*, nine words, in ochre dust (`215:43`); he said the height is for the road (`215:41`); and he said that a man with men enough to hold a dam is not looking at eleven houses and that that is not a fault, that is the arithmetic (`215:55`). **He is not a villain, he is not redeemed, and every number he says is true. He is asleep in a shed four miles up the river at the end of Chapter 219 because he was on a dam until the middle of the night.**
- **Hollis Nance, forty-four.** Clerk of the water-seat, nine years, and his first words in every room are that he is not a Bench clerk, not a recordwright and not an advocate and that there is no instrument in that room that makes eleven seats decide anything. **He has now done THREE things in eleven days that he has no authority to do, and he says out loud each time that the sentence has not got any shorter because nothing in this valley has changed to make it shorter: he opened a rider's bag (`208:7`); he read a four-line order out in a lane, on eleven chairs, and nobody signed anything (`214:55`, `214:57`); and he opened a letter of two paragraphs from Orison in front of six people (`220:15`). He paid five pounds and six out of his own money for a matter out of a column in his own index that does not exist (`211:73`), and neither he nor Ilyan's five is going to be paid for or thanked for. The keys to the only press in the room are in the inside pocket of his coat where they live (`218:17`).** He keeps a hand-book up to date on the last working day of every fourth week and has never once asked what is in the fourth column of it, and **he named that column out loud on day 166 and said he was not going to ask about it (`218:25`).**
- **The woman of about forty who washes the pen and turns the board over at the end of a week.** Established at `206:93`, `207:63`, `210:51`, `214:65`. **In this batch she brought the lane's finding up to the Seat's room (`211:35`); she said the four pages have to stay on the wall and why, in the plainest sentence anybody said that day (`218:51`); she took the list of nineteen answers down the hill in her own two hands on the back of a leaf of the Seat's own water-book (`219:5`); and she told a man of thirty-one that he is not a party to it and that if he puts his name at the bottom of her sheet it stops being a list of what nineteen people need (`219:61`). She is the closest thing this batch has to an ordinary person authorising a document, and she has no authority whatever and says so three times.**
- **Garrow Lee, forty-one.** A joiner, measuring the same frame for a gate he cannot sell at £5 18s. **THE BATCH'S ONLY MAN WHO HAS ASKED FOR A NUMBER SO THAT HE CAN PUT SOMETHING DOWN, and he has asked three times and been given it three times and it has turned out to be a problem (`216:33`).** He measured the old marks with his own shoulder on day 161 and says they are a foot apart, and the water says they are two, and he is right about his shoulder (`213:31`). He said the eleventh feet is not eleven feet and the mark is not a level. His lath with the price on it is in the frame at the end of the batch and he is not measuring it again until there is a fund (`220:57`).
- **Ilya Kever, seventy-four.** Born in the second house up the lane at Hollow Sallow. **He cut a new tar line at the water's height on two doorposts in front of about nine people on day 161, and it is the only time in this batch that an ordinary man got a number anybody in that village could check by looking, and he would not say whether he cut the eleven and nobody asked him (`213:45`).** He has a boy of nineteen at the Anner who mends a winch. **CARRIED DEFECT, RECORDED AND NOT BUILT ON: `206:7` and `206:49` give the eleven marks to a man of sixty-eight at Coldbeck whom `206:9` names as Davan Treloar, and `207:11` names Ilya Kever as *the man with the string*. Chapter 213 asserts nothing about which of them cut them, and the Volume 05 close should decide it. See `state/current.md` section 12 item 1.**
- **Elsa Quint, seventeen.** The grocer's daughter at the top of the lane in Hollow Sallow, who has been in it since she was born. **She read the fourth line of the four pages out to a neighbour with one eye on day 159 and said the batch's first finding — it says how high the water is and it does not say high as from where (`211:29`) — and she is not a lawyer and has never been in a room and never will be.** She read the third line out loud five times in that lane (`213:51`) and about twenty times to twenty people standing in two feet of the Sallow on the night of the flood (`216:55`). Her father's shop is where the joiner said he heard it. **She is a person of the basin and was in a house the whole time; naming her is not producing a person, and the chapter says out loud that she was born in that lane.**
- **Brann Slee, fifty-six.** Does roofs on that lane and on the eleven above it and about forty more, and has done nine of the nineteen and four of them more than once. **He is the batch's only source of a price and he gives one figure and will not break it into parts, and the reason is the last time he did for a man in a good coat (`217:23`, `217:29`).** A roof, wet through, is about £9. **He speaks once in this batch, in a doorway in the Seat's one room at `226:41`, and what he says is that the frame was measured four hours ago, and he is the ladder on the shoulder. The first writing of that line had him as *a man of about forty with a ladder on his shoulder*, which put a second ladder in the batch and let him override the joiner on a frame he had never measured, and the review of the batch caught it; there is one ladder in this basin and it is his.**
- **The nineteen of the flood, persons not previously named**, in the order the woman of about forty walked them (`219:45`). Their answers are in a cart shed under a board with a stone on it, and a man of thirty-one has not read it and is not going to.
- **Fen Tregerran, sixty-one**, who kept the salt-pans, drew a board through the eleventh pan on the evening of day 166, and did not cut a notch because the day was not over (`218:71`). He said the volume's central finding out loud on a bank in Chapter 202 and it is not restated.
- **The nine people in the Seat's one room on day 166** (`218:3`): Hollis Nance, Ilyan Vester, Sera Quill, Tarin Keel, Ewan Troke, the woman of about forty, Garrow Lee, Jory Vellan, and a girl of seventeen who came because she could not get a thing out of her head. **Nobody in that room asked a man of thirty-one for one more sentence (`218:43`), and about six of them knew that his name is on nothing in this valley, and not one of them said so (`218:63`).**
- **And nobody in this basin is produced, and the count of people who arrive on a day of their own is STILL FOUR** — Ewan Troke (`201:19`), Fen Tregerran (`202:5`), Alo Vearn (`209:5`), and the man who comes through the north twice a year (`210:53`). **A courier with a letter who was going to Coldbeck for a horse (`220:13`) is a fact about a letter and not a person in a room, on the definition in guardrail seven of `outline/volume-05.md`.**

### THE THREE CLOCKS, unchanged, and the engine of the volume is still arithmetic and not politics

**The finding that the two calendars do not convert was made ONCE, at Chapter 202, in a salt-pan's mouth, and it is not restated, and this batch is about what people do about a thing that has already been found out.**

- **The pan-book year: fifty saltings, more or less.** A dry-year salting about seven weeks, a wet-year one eleven. **THE SIXTH SALTING RAN ELEVEN WEEKS THIS YEAR, which is why the pans were turned late, which is why the standing order of the sluice was already wrong on the ninth (`212:76`), and the salt came out of the sixth salting in the fifty-second week and the pans went in and the year turned over (`218:71`).**
- **The turn: a year counted from the first shaking, which is turn nought, twelve years back, and this year is turn twelve.** The first eight were numbered on a door frame by a man four short, and he corrected it himself in the front of his own book in a better hand because he was frightened of it, and about nine people know it and about four hundred do not care.
- **The day: a day since the second shaking, out of the water-book, which is the Anner's and has been kept in four hands.** **IT IS THE ONLY DATE IN THIS BASIN THAT CAN BE PUT ON THE OUTSIDE OF A BAG GOING NINE DAYS TO ORIZON, and Chapter 211 does exactly that and gives the reason: a grain year does not exist in this basin and a paper that arrives with one on it is a paper about nothing (`211:87`).** Nine instances of it in the volume, at `201:78`, `202:83`, `204:93`, `207:76`, `210:19`, `211:89`, `213:69`, `216:73` and `220:33`, on days 149, 150, 152, 155, 158, 159, 161, 164 and 168, and every one satisfies basin day = Shelf day + 78. Inside a day: the first light, the noon light, the closing of the water.
- **AND THE DAY-PHRASE FORM IS THE BASIN'S, NOT THE SHELF'S, and this batch uses it twenty-six times and every one is legal: nineteen name a day number of seven or less, four are the *last day* form and three are the *last week* form.** A notch count is a different kind of number and one is printed in this batch at `218:71`, running one behind the day on this volume's own convention.

### THE DOCUMENTS, EIGHT OF THEM IN THE VOLUME, AND EVERY ONE IS A PHYSICAL OBJECT

**Batch 0002 adds two and no more.**

7. **The standing order of the sluice, one page, four lines including a head (`212:21`–`24`).** **It is dated in the ELEVENTH SALTING OF THE FORTY-NINTH YEAR and the bond is the ELEVENTH SALTING OF THE FORTIETH, and that is the same salting of a year nine along (`212:28`).** It says the gate is to be open from *the day the pans are turned at Coldbeck* and stood shut again when the water is out of the lane, and that the keeper shall do it in the ordinary way and shall not be asked for a reason. **The day in it is a day of the pan-book and the order has no other date and none has been asked for. There is no name on it anywhere, at the foot, at the back, or in the Seat's index, and the clerk does not know who made it and says a thing can be valid without anybody being able to say whose hand it is (`212:50`).** It is the only order in this basin anybody has ever had to keep to and it has been kept to every year for three years without anybody knowing it existed.
8. **A page of the Seat's water-book, three lines (`216:63`–`65`), in the Seat's own hand and the mill hand.** The Seat's hand: the third of the fifty-second week, the gate open since the sixth day of the fiftieth and not shut, and no order of the Seat has changed it, and the water at the frame at the closing is above the top of the oak and going over. The mill hand: **I HAVE PUT THE SIDE DOWN ON THE FOURTH HOUSE AND IT IS UP TO THE SILL. THE NINTH HOUSE IS UNDER TO THE LAMP. I HAVE NO MORE LAMP OIL. NOBODY CAME.** And under both: **there is no hour against either line and no column in this book for an hour.** **The last thing in that book before the flood was of the ninth, three weeks and a day before Chapter 212, in the same mill hand: shut sluice, the pans are on and the water is in the hollow, nobody come (`212:64`).**
**AND WHAT IS NOT BLOCK-QUOTED IN THIS BATCH, DELIBERATELY: the list of nineteen answers is a document, it is the first document anybody in this basin has ever written about itself, and it is rendered in running prose with quotation marks at `219:45` because the batch has two block-quoted documents and not three and the volume has no third category.**

### THE FINDINGS, once each, and none of them is the same finding twice

- **A height on a document and a height on a doorpost are the same number of feet and not the same height** — and the girl who finds it is seventeen and has never been in a room (`211:29`).
- **The order breaches the chamber's own second ruling on its face, and the under-manager says it is not void and he is right, and the finding is that there is no form for asking a chamber whether its own order breaks its own ruling** (`211:49`, `211:55`, `211:61`).
- **The only date this basin can put on a paper that is leaving it is a day out of a river** (`211:87`).
- **A standing order that names a weather instead of a day has been obeyed for three years and the person obeying it has never been asked why** (`212:34`, `212:76`).
- **The same salting of a year nine along, and nobody has ever done that sum** (`212:28`).
- **A height is not a level, and two careful measurements of the same two marks by two careful men differ by a foot, and neither man is lying, and nobody can say which is wrong because neither has had water on both in the same hour** (`213:25`, `213:31`). **The direction of the discrepancy is NOT asserted in this batch, because `206:37` and `206:45` contradict each other on it. See `state/current.md` section 12 item 2.**
- **The first question anybody in Hollow Sallow has ever asked about a document was asked on a bank by a woman who is not a lawyer and got an answer in the wrong shape** (`213:53`).
- **Asking eleven people costs three days, and three days is not a figure, and a basin that cannot put a cost in a minute is a basin that does not ask** (`214:21`).
- **A thing that is void and unread is not a protection, it is an absence** (`215:27`).
- **I have been given a number and never a reason** — nine words, in ochre dust, by the only man in the basin with men enough to hold a dam, and he is right (`215:43`).
- **THE MIDPOINT, AND IT IS ONE SUBTRACTION AND NOBODY FORGED ANYTHING: the frame at Hollow Sallow is nine feet above the Anner's sill, eleven feet above the Anner's sill is therefore two feet above the frame's sill, and two feet above the frame's sill is about the height of the floors of the houses on the upper half of that lane. The eleven feet is the dam's number and not the lane's** (`216:43`, `216:45`, `216:49`). **THE SENTENCE THIS BULLET CARRIED UNTIL 27 SEPTEMBER 2026 HAD THE DIRECTION INVERTED — "the Anner's sill is nine feet above the frame" — WHICH MADE THE SUBTRACTION ILLEGIBLE AT THE READER'S END AND WAS THE RESIDUE OF THE BATCH 0002 REVIEW REPAIR THAT FLIPPED A WORD WITHOUT FLIPPING THE ARITHMETIC. THE SUBTRACTION 11 − 9 = 2 IS CORRECT ONLY WHEN THE NINE AND THE ELEVEN ARE MEASURED FROM THE SAME SILL DOWNWARDS AND UPWARDS RESPECTIVELY, WHICH IS WHAT `218:49` SAYS AND WHAT `206:37` AND `216:43` NOW SAY.**
- **THE VILLAGE THE MINE HAD PROMISED TO PROTECT IS FLOODED BY THE FULFILMENT OF THE MINE'S PROMISE, and nobody broke anything: the bond's first two sentences are the promise and the Anner kept both of them exactly, by the party it names, in the way they were written** (`217:13`). **No chapter of this batch softens that into an accident and no later batch may.**
- **It named a dam, it named a height, it named a day, it never named the water** — nine words, in the mouth of the woman who opens the gate for four and six a week (`217:48`).
- **A rule that names no bystander is carried by the nearest body with hands** — in his own mouth, about this wall, and NOT the sentence he said once in another county, which he does not repeat and does not explain and is not relieved of (`218:39`, `218:43`).
- **A basin with no document on a wall is worse than a basin with a wrong one, and the person who says so is a woman of about forty with no authority whatever** (`218:51`).
- **The money is not the difficulty, and the proof is nineteen houses and nineteen answers and not one sum in any of them** (`219:47`).
- **The Bench is seized of the matter and will consider it in its own time, and it took ten pounds and twelve and nine days to be told so, and a clerk did it properly** (`220:21`).
- **AND THE SYSTEM PUTS NOTHING UP AND SAYS NOTHING WHATEVER ABOUT ANY OF IT** (`220:61`). **Anchor 8 remains missing beneath the Red Basin until Volume 14 and nobody went down. The second half of the volume's question — why it answers from beneath Orison — is not asked out loud in any chapter of this batch and is not even a whisper.**

### THE INHERITANCE, and none of it moved

**`state/current.md` §7 lists the thirteen threads and how each one stands after this batch. In one line each, for this file:** the new question is on a page in a penny exercise book in a cart shed and is not raised again and is not answered; the carrying is off nothing and he carried the Ryefold matter through all ten chapters; the blank in the third line is a blank and he read it out loud on a bank and it is still a blank; the fourth hand is unasked and the clerk of thirty-four's letter is still tied on a shelf; Nesh Arrad is named in a list read aloud and no day figure is printed for her; the second notice is unpaid; the claim of forty-five pounds two shillings and eightpence is not paid and the carter of fifty-one is right; the dead man in the fourth line and his wife are untouched and Ruth Vellan in this basin is a different dead person; the box of about nine hundred and forty forms is not found; the ninety-one numbered sheets are in a yard in another county; the class of three hundred and thirty-nine and its one boy of nineteen are untouched and there is no boy of nineteen in this batch; the minute in a drawer is unasked for; and four hundred and eighty-five pounds a year falls due at the close of that grain year and does not stop.

### WHAT A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE

1. **The count of the people who know he cannot read is not printed in this project and is not printed here. Refer to `154:53` and `164:49` and to nothing else, and do not add to it in the fiction.** This batch added nothing to it, and the limit is exercised four times: the standing order is read out by a chainman against the gate; the water-book page is read out on a wet bank by the same chainman, twice, and he notices the two readings differ in nothing; the nineteen answers are taken down in a hand that is not his; and the four pages are never once put into his hands.
2. **The leg does not come back in this volume and that is a consequence of the carrying, not a rule and not a cure.** It is used once, at `216:51`, as a fact and not a discovery, and the reason is not given. **No file and no chapter in Volume 05 may state a count of days since the leg cleared, and none does.**
3. **The day-count is NINE instances in Chapters 201–220 and the figure stands on exactly one line in each of the nine chapters, and a notch count is not a day-count instance.** The one in this batch is at `218:71` and is a hundred and sixty-five notches on a hundred and sixty-sixth day, which is the volume's own convention.
4. **The panel count is TWO and the bold count is FOUR over Chapters 201–220, and every one of the four is a panel line. A bold scan that finds a fifth is a defect.**
5. **The block-quoted documents are EIGHT in the volume, in eight distinct physical objects, and two of them are this batch's. A ninth is not permitted in this batch.**
6. **The repetition bar's own numbers for this batch, measured: the word nine stands eighty-eight times over Chapters 211–220, and *a minute and a half* stands zero times.**
7. **The two block-quoted documents in this batch are a standing order with NO NAME ON IT and a water-book page with NO HOUR AGAINST EITHER LINE, and both of those absences are load-bearing. A later pass that supplies a name or an hour has destroyed a finding.**

## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0001 — Chapters 201–210, *The Red Basin*. **The fact base for everything after it. The live record, with the map, the money with the working, the instruments and the nine handed-on threads, is the top block of `state/current.md` under `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0001 IS DELIVERED`, and it outranks this block. Nothing below this block is edited.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS: the new canon, in one place, with a chapter and a line against every claim, so that a later pass can check a thing instead of believing it.** The standing instruction is unchanged and it is the whole of what makes this usable: **re-run every scan against the chapter files and do not re-read a list.**

### THE GROUND, AND WHO IS ON IT

**PLANNED AND DELIVERED, and the world is exactly one notch bigger and not bigger than that: a dry southern basin of red clay, salt flats and old earthworks with a mine, a market town, a headwater village and a river that runs sixteen weeks a year. No new city, no new institution, no new faction, no new magic and no new people with a new name.**

- **The Sallow**, a river that runs about sixteen weeks a year and is a dry bed the other thirty-six (201:3, 204:3). **It is nine feet from the sill of the sluice at Hollow Sallow to the bed** (204:3).
- **Hollow Sallow**: about four hundred and ten people, about ninety houses, **eleven of them marked on the doorposts** (201:25, 206:3).
- **Sallowby**: about four hundred people, one market day a week; the water-seat, the pit's head office, a chandler's shop with a room over it, and the mill (203:3, 205:3).
- **Coldbeck**: the headwater village, the salt-pans, and a chest under a floor in a house belonging to a family that has held that ground since before the first shaking (202:3, 203:79).
- **Anner Pit**: salt and red ochre, about eleven hundred tons a year, about four hundred men in the working years and about sixty in the off, four miles above the village, with a dam, a chain-book, a strong-room and a press (205:32, 209:3).
- **The marl ridge**: about a quarter of a mile wide, running with the water, and the only ground in the basin a house will stand on above the flood line (201:25).
- **Orison, nine days away**: the chamber of plenty sits there and has made three rulings about this basin in nine years (208:3).
- **And the road**: nine days long from the last stile on the Shelf, and it arrives four miles east of the stile and about a mile and a half south (201:3, `201:9`).

### THE PEOPLE OF THE RED BASIN, and none of them is an antagonist and none of them was produced

- **Ilma Treen, thirty-eight.** Keeps the sluice at Hollow Sallow, eleven years in it, paid nine shillings a week for the sixteen weeks the Sallow runs and nothing the rest of the year, which is £7 4s. **The Anner gives four and six and the Seat owes four and six and has owed it for three years, which is £10 16.** Her whole year is a hundred and forty days of water and she is paid for a hundred and twelve of them, and the hundred and twelve is sixteen weeks of seven days, and the twenty-eight days she is not paid for is four more weeks of water she holds, and four weeks at four and six the week is eighteen shillings exactly (204:11, `204:21`, `204:77`, `204:91`, `204:97`). **This line said *paid for ninety*, out of a chapter that has been corrected: ninety and a hundred and forty leave fifty days, and the chapter now says a hundred and twelve and twenty-eight.**
- **Jory Vellan, fifty-two.** Under-manager at the Anner, nineteen years in it. **His father lodged the bond in the third salting after the first shaking and is dead eleven years; his wife Ruth has been dead four years, which is the eighth turn, and her name is on a half-deed drawn in the ninth turn in a hand that is not his and not the scrivener's (203:88, 205:9).** He is right that the dam has to be held and wrong that a mine is a charter. **He knows what the four pages will cost and has never once said it out loud in a room that was writing it down (207:61).**
- **Wenna Skell, thirty-three.** A scrivener at Sallowby over the chandler's shop, eleven years, about six hundred and forty deeds, about ninety of them for the Anner line, a penny a page and threepence a sealed copy (203:3, `203:11`). **She said the volume's thesis in her own mouth while copying and did not know she had said it (203:70), and she is owed eleven pence by a woman two streets down who has said every market day for two years that she will pay it on this market, and she has never written the eleven pence down (203:105).**
- **Fen Tregerran, sixty-one.** Keeps the salt-pans and a notched board, one notch a day, and a heap of eleven stones a day, and has for thirty years (202:11, `202:13`). **He said the volume's central finding out loud on a bank and did not know he had said anything (202:45; `202:43` is the line above it, where the silence sits, and is not the finding), and he has a board from the eleventh pan-book year and another from the wet one and the wet one is the board his mother sold a pig for.**
- **Hollis Nance, forty-four.** Clerk of the water-seat, nine years in it, and **his first nine words in the volume are that he is not a Bench clerk, not a recordwright and not an advocate, and that the price of taking a paper to Orison and bringing it back is five pounds and six (205:7).** He keeps an index by himself on the last working day of every fourth week and it has a fourth column in the head of it with no entries under it (208:85).
- **Ewan Troke, twenty-nine.** A chainman of the water-seat, who came up the Shelf road with a case of papers because he heard about them at a market and made up his own mind, and who was not sent for (201:19, `201:21`).
- **Garrow Lee, forty-one.** A joiner, measuring a frame for a gate he cannot sell at £5 18s (206:15).
- **Alo Vearn, twenty-six.** At the Anner since she was seventeen, and nobody has ever asked her what she thinks the six cuts in the oldest floor are, and she has thought about it exactly as much as you would expect (209:23).
- **Ilya Kever, seventy-four.** The man with the string who cut the eleven marks, with a boy of nineteen at the Anner who mends a winch, and who came to a room he was not sent for because somebody told him there was going to be a number (207:11, 206:69).
- **The woman of seventy at the door at Coldbeck**, who said her grandfather asked the same question in the first year and let it go, and that she was not going to be the one to start it (203:75).
- **And nobody in this basin is produced, and three people arrive on a day of their own: Ewan Troke with a case, a man who works at the Anner and came into the Seat's room he was not asked into, and a man who came through the north and told them about a woman (201:21, 207:9, 210:55).**

### THE THREE CLOCKS, and this is the volume's engine and it is arithmetic and not politics

**The finding is made ONCE, at Chapter 202, in a salt-pan's mouth, and it is that there is no conversion between any of them and the reason is not secrecy.** It is that a salting has no length, a pan-book year has no first drying, and the day count was not started on the first of a year by anybody who was keeping a pan-book.

- **The pan-book year: fifty saltings, more or less.** A dry-year salting about seven weeks; a wet-year one eleven. Deeds before the first shaking are dated like *the eleventh salting of the fortieth year* (202:37, 203:29).
- **The turn: a year counted from the first shaking, which is turn nought, twelve years back; this year is turn twelve.** The first eight were numbered on a door frame by a man who was four short and corrected it himself in the front of his own book, in a better hand, because he was frightened of it (`202:31`, `202:33`). **About nine people in this basin know that and about four hundred do not care.**
- **The day: a day since the second shaking, which was 149 days before Chapter 201's day.** Inside a day: the first light, the noon light, the closing of the water. **A sluice-keeper's day is the closing of the water (204:107).**
- **A day with no chapter is a thing the year does.** No chapter of this batch prints a gap, names a gap, refers to the days before it, or says a number of days passed. The decision is in `outline/volume-05.md` and not in the prose.

### THE DOCUMENTS, six of them, and every one is a physical object

1. **A chain-book page of the water-seat, copied by Ewan Troke with a rule and a flat stone and a piece of lead** (`201:37`–`40`). It records that the ridge is called the common in every deed and that no deed says whose, that the road crosses it at eleven chains from the north end by a causeway put in by persons unnamed, **that the word common is defined in the margin of each deed and not in the body of either, and that the older margin is eighty-two years old and the newer is nine turns old**, and that the crossing is not by right and not by gift.
2. **The two halves of the marl-strip deed, side by side, in Wenna Skell's hand** (`203:29`–`32`, four lines, and the two that agree word for word are `203:30` and `203:32`). **They agree word for word down to the dash and differ in the margin, and the seam is in both and the two seams do not meet.** The strip is longer than a skin; the two halves were sewn by the man drawing it and he drew the half he could see from where he was standing. **A deed in this basin is not a paper. It is an agreement between two papers, and a person who loses one of them has not lost a thing — they have become one of two people each holding half, and neither half is a lie (`203:70`).**
3. **A bond of forty pounds, on two skins, folded so the seam does not show** (`205:17`–`20`). It binds the Anner to hold the dam to eleven feet above its own sill. **It is void on the death of the person who lodged it, which is written in at the back in a different hand and has been read once.** And **the ink has run down the middle of the second line and the foot of the first, so the words about not being answerable are under about an inch of it (`205:40`).**
4. **The four pages of the order of the water-seat** (`207:37`–`41`, a head and four numbered lines). One dam, one height, one day, one party; the causeway shut and the high road at the Anner's cost; **eleven feet and the eleven houses below it and nobody to be asked to leave a house**; read in the room and in the lane and the lane's copy kept by a person of the lane. **All four lines are complete and there is no hole in any of them and he says so out loud before anybody else has worked it out (`207:45`) and nobody notices (`207:57`).**
5. **A printed abstract of three rulings of the chamber of plenty, four sheets, threepence the sheet, delivered twice a grain year to a Seat that has paid for sixty-six sheets at a time for nine years and opened two bundles** (`208:17`–`21`). **The first says the Seat is competent and needs no seal and the nine days are not to be spent on a matter the Seat can hear. The second, entered in the third turn, says a height marked on a doorpost is not a measurement and that no order may name a height without naming the sill. The third, entered in the ninth turn, says the first half of a deed, being sewn to its second, is the deed, and the Seat is directed to require the production of both halves before it enters any matter of ground. And the referral upon all three is of one name, P. Holloway of Orison, who has not been in the basin since the first shaking and is not a party to any of them.**
6. **Page eleven of the chain-book of the Anner** (`209:33`–`35`). The oldest workings were taken out, not sunk: eleven chains and sixteen links along one straight line, and **no column in that book for anywhere**. Six marks cut in the floor along that line in a hand not the hand of the page, on the sixth salting of the eleventh pan-book year, **which is a date the book has no means of entering.** The low end of the pit is four hundred and six feet below the sill stone at the mouth, and under the low end there is a void of depth not yet found, **and the figure of four hundred and six is the figure of the pit and not of the void.**

### THE FINDINGS, once each, and the shape of what is coming

- **A word with two meanings** (201:39). The old margin: common is common to every house standing on it, every beast may go over it, no beast may be stopped. The new margin, nine turns old, in the hand of a man in Sallowby who is not a lawyer: common is ground held of the water-seat and worked by the Anner, no house stands on it, no beast goes over it. **The last part is in the wording because somebody asked, in the ninth turn, and there is no note of it anywhere in this basin and Ewan Troke has looked for one for two of the three years.**
- **The clocks do not convert** (202:45).
- **A deed is two papers** (203:70).
- **A contract keeps a thing true by costing somebody money, and the money stopped in the fourth year and the thing did not stop** (205:32, `205:38`). **The dam has been held for eleven years by four men and a barrow at £3 5s of material a year, and the forty pounds has sat in a box under the floor since the first winter after the first shaking and nobody has thought about it, and the Seat has relied on it for twelve years and never checked that it was void.**
- **The eleven houses are not a category, and asking them costs three days and three days is not a figure** (206:57).
- **A height is a height above something, and there are three sills inside four miles** (206:33). The Anner's sill is nine feet above the frame at Hollow Sallow. **Eleven feet above the Anner's sill is two feet ABOVE the frame's sill, and two feet above that sill is about the height of the floors of the houses on the upper half of the lane, which is the whole of the volume's flood in one subtraction, and the mark on the ninth doorpost is a foot higher than the mark on the fifth, and the mark on the eleventh house that was rebuilt in the year of the mark is a foot lower than the other ten, because a different man put it on a new doorpost without a string (`206:73`).**
- **The quick ruling is four complete lines and the problem is that they are complete** (207:45, `207:49`).
- **A perfect record can still be wrong about a place, because nobody who kept it was ever in it** (208:49).
- **The first missing anchor position**, identified by two ordinary records that do not meet: the chain-book's straight trench and the water-seat's list of drains, and **the ninth line of that list is the Anner overflow, dated the ninth turn, and the drain was aimed along a line older than anything in this basin can date, and **the interval between the two is a span this basin has no instrument for naming and the chapter does not name it, which is the volume's engine and not an omission**, and nobody in the basin has ever noticed that it was aimed** (`209:53`, `209:63`). **This line said *a hundred and ten years older than it*, out of a corrected line of the chapter: a hundred and ten is less than the hundred and thirty the chain-book's binding is old, and the six cuts are older than the page that found them, and `209:53` says out loud that there is no way whatever on earth to say how much later.** **The position is nine degrees and a quarter off the Anner's own heading, at more than four hundred and six feet, and the water is at the low end and the last hundred and forty feet of it has been under to the roof since before Alo Vearn was seventeen, because the pit has been pumping the Sallow's water down a pipe into it since the first shaking and nobody has ever asked where it goes (`209:41`, `209:79`).**
- **AND THE STONE IS NOT REACHED AND NOBODY STANDS IN FRONT OF IT, and the System puts nothing up and says nothing whatever about it (`209:83`).** Anchor 8 remains missing beneath the Red Basin until Volume 14. **The second half of this volume's question — why it answers from beneath Orison — is not asked out loud in any chapter and is not even a whisper.**

### THE INHERITANCE, and none of it moved

**`state/current.md` §7 lists the thirteen threads and how each one stands. In one line each, for this file:** the new question is on a page in a penny exercise book in a cart shed and is not answered; the carrying is off nothing and he is carrying it in this valley for all ten chapters; the blank in the third line is a blank and he wrote in his own book that he could fill it and will not; the fourth hand is unasked and the clerk of thirty-four's letter about it is in a tied bundle in Sallowby and is not opened; Nesh Arrad is named in his book and no day figure is printed for her; the second notice is unpaid; the claim of forty-five pounds two shillings and eightpence is not paid and the carter of fifty-one is right; the dead man in the fourth line and his wife are untouched and Ruth Vellan in this basin is a different dead person; the box of about nine hundred and forty forms is not found; the ninety-one numbered sheets are in a yard in another county; the class of three hundred and thirty-nine and its one boy of nineteen are untouched and there is no boy of nineteen in this batch; the minute in a drawer is unasked for; and four hundred and eighty-five pounds a year falls due at the close of that grain year and does not stop.

### WHAT A LATER PASS GETS WRONG IF IT DOES NOT MEASURE

1. **The volume's day-count is FIVE instances in Chapters 201–210 and it starts at zero for this volume, and Volume 04's twenty-two is a count of a different thing in a different volume and the two are not to be added, subtracted or compared.** The origin is the days since the second shaking and the formula is **basin day = Shelf day + 78**. The five are at `201:78` (149), `202:83` (150), `204:93` (152), `207:76` (155) and `210:19` (158), and all five agree with their chapter's Shelf day under the formula. **`210:69`'s hundred and fifty-seven is a notch count and runs one behind the day, on this volume's own convention, and is not a sixth instance. This line used to cite `210:69` as the fifth instance, which was a notch count standing in for a day, and the day itself was not in the chapter at all until the review repair put it at `210:19`.**
2. **The panels are ONE and that is a count of panels, and it is two bold lines, and the bold inventory for the batch is two lines and zero emphasis spans.**
3. **The duplicate scan returns one distinct duplicated line and one extra occurrence at both the seventy-character and the forty-character threshold, and the units agree at both because the line appears exactly twice, and the line is the two halves of the deed at `203:30` and `203:32` and the duplication is the device.**
4. **The weekday scan is zero, and it was three before delivery and the three were taken out.**
5. **The month-name scan is one and it is the proper noun *Briar March* at `210:55` and it is not a month.**
6. **The day-phrase instrument returns eleven instances and all eleven are legal and none carries a day number of eight or more.**
7. **THE PLAN SAID FOUR DAY-COUNT INSTANCES AND THE DELIVERY IS FIVE, and the plan also said five block-quoted documents and then six, and both changes are in `outline/volume-05.md` where they belong.** A plan that is edited to match its own delivery teaches a later pass nothing.
8. **Two of the batch's own drafts were wrong in ways a scan caught and a careful writer did not:** three weekdays, and a narrator's aside in Chapter 206 that named his reading within one sentence of a disclosure. **Both are recorded because the class is the class: a figure read out of a draft is not a figure, and a sentence near a bar is not on the other side of it.**
