**HE STATES THE NUMBER OF DAYS HE HAS BEEN ON THAT COAST TWENTY-FOUR TIMES, IN SIXTEEN CHAPTERS, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM AGREES WITH THIS TABLE. The three accounts were checked against each other and not merely against one another: for each of the sixteen chapters the tide arithmetic (day *D* of tide *T* is `7T + D − 13`), the Day column above, and the figure the chapter says out loud all give the same number, and there is no disagreement in either direction — no chapter states a figure the table does not give it, and no chapter that states one has been left off this list. 14 (110, twice, at lines 59 and 144), 29 (120, four times, at 90, 116, 126 and 128), 30 (121, once, at 89), 32 (123, once, at 109), 38 (129, once, at 56), 39 (130, once, at 119), 40 (131, once, at 21), 43 (134, once, at 95), 44 (135, once, at 57), 46 (137, once, at 87), 47 (138, once, at 109), 49 (140, three times, at 9, 104 and 108), 50 (141, once, at 67), 51 (142, once, at 5), 54 (145, twice, at 15 and 57), 59 (150, twice, at 3 and 107). The line numbers are given so that the next reader can check the count rather than take it, which is the whole point of putting them here. The sixteen chapters are 110, 120, 121, 123, 129, 130, 131, 134, 135, 137, 138, 140, 141, 142, 145 and 150.**

**THE FIGURES THAT SHARE THOSE NUMERALS FOR OTHER REASONS, and which a later agent must NOT add to the twenty-four: Chapter 121's second *thirty days*, at line 141, which is how long the leg has gone without clearing and not how long he has been here — its own sentence says *a leg that had not cleared in thirty days*; Chapter 129's *nineteen days*, four times on line 7, which is an interval from the fourth of the fourth tide and is the same interval the run gives; Chapter 135's second *forty-four days*, at line 63, which is Halse Tarrant's and not Ilyan's — the same man's forty-four at line 57 is Ilyan's, and 135's line 9 establishes the two men apart, the man forty feet off on the shingle with his weight on his good side and a hand-lead in his fist, against Halse Tarrant on the wall; Chapter 132's *nine days*, at line 80, which is how long a sheet of writing paper has been in a man's breast pocket; and Chapter 111's *forty days*, at line 89, which is not a coast count at all and is corrected in its own paragraph — *Not forty days. Forty times.***

**He arrived on the coast about three days before Chapter 101, which is why Chapter 110 closes on day fourteen and the fourteenth day is also the day he says he has been here fourteen days: Corrie Tallow saw him on the second of the second tide, day 3, and paid four days' cart hire to bring him down to the sixth of the second tide, day 7.**

**AND TWO EARLIER CLAIMS ABOUT THIS COUNT WERE FALSE, AND BOTH WERE FOUND BY A PASS THAT THOUGHT IT WAS CONFIRMING IT. The first pass of this close said the count of nineteen is stated at Chapter 112. IT IS NOT: Chapter 112 states no absolute day-count, and its only two instances of *nineteen* are *nineteen years*, both of them Gell Roke's and both in the same speech — *I have painted that staff for the guild for nineteen years* at 112:59 and *It is nineteen years of a man who does not want to be told what he has been doing for nothing* at 112:63, introduced at 112:57. **Bettany Rook's nineteen years is at Chapter 102:7 and nowhere in 112**, and the first pass put it in 112, which is the exact error it was correcting. The second error was arithmetic of a different kind: the second pass of this close recounted the list above, confirmed nineteen in eleven chapters, and was itself short by five — it dropped 121:89, 123:109, 131:21, 134:95 and 138:109, and therefore reported a table checked against nineteen statements when it is checked against twenty-four. **Both errors ran the same way: a list assembled from a partial read, presented as a set.** A Volume 04 agent must not cite Chapter 112 for a figure, and must not cite this count without re-running the grep.**

**Intervals that must not be recomputed from a chapter and must be taken from this table:** 137 → 145 is **eight** days. 141 → 144 is **three**. 5 of 8 → 2 of 9 is **four**. 6 of 8 → 5 of 9 is **six**, and 5 of 8 → 5 of 9 is **seven**. 2 of 7 → 1 of 8 is **six**. 4 of 7 → 4 of 9 is **fourteen**. 4 of 4 → 2 of 7 is **nineteen**, and Chapter 129 states that interval in its own mouth. **4 of 3 → 6 of 3 is TWO days, and the first version of this line said three, which is the fifth error the second pass found in this file and the only one that is pure arithmetic rather than a miscounted chapter: the tide is seven days, the fourth of the third is day 12 and the sixth is day 14.** The line is left here in full so that the next reader can check all nine of them in one place, which is what should have happened to it the first time. **All nine now run forward, earlier tide-day to later. The eighth of them, 2 of 7 → 1 of 8, was printed in this line as `1 of 8 → 2 of 7`, which runs backward and is the same number; a reversed interval is how the wrong value above survives a re-read, and it is corrected here so that every arrow on this line points the way the reader is expected to read it.**

### THE DISTANCES, IN ONE PLACE. Attach no other distance to any of these.

- **Kestrel Reach crossing to Wether Cross:** the flat is **nine hundred yards of shingle**, six crossings on a good day and nine on a bar day.
- **The crossing to the north end, up the lane:** **four hundred yards.**
- **The crossing to the tide office:** **a mile and a flight of stairs up the hill**, with a lock on the door and two panes in oak frames on the north wall. **Not a mile and a quarter, not a mile and a half, not two miles. A distance defect on this walk has been found and repaired five times in this volume** — 144, 149, 147, and twice in earlier passes — **and any new one should be assumed to be wrong until the chapter is read.**
- **Kestrel Reach to Thornglass:** **two miles along the coast road.** Not a mile and a quarter, not a mile and a half, not two hundred yards. A walk of two miles takes him about an hour and a quarter.
- **The guild's two panes** are on the north wall of the tide office, a mile and a flight of stairs up the hill, and **have not been out of that building in nineteen years.** The glassmaker's pane is three years old, on the wall of his own shed at Thornglass, in a frame he made himself. **Neither pane has ever been in one room with the other, and no chapter may put them there.**
- **The guild's head office:** **two hundred miles up this coast**, and it has a printer in it.
- **The school with Belva Cray's copy of the four-hundred-year list:** **a hundred and forty miles up this coast**, four days and eleven shillings.
- **The city behind Ilyan, where the rendering is:** **four hundred miles.**
- **The bar road and Alma Gannock's hut:** **four miles above the north end**, and the road is not gone up at any point in the volume.
- **The bar at the north end:** about **two hundred yards** of gravel from the hard north to where the gravel goes down, and the gravel bank is **two feet**.
- **The wall:** **two hundred and sixty yards**, laid in courses, **nine courses at the inner end and about two at the outer**, about **six foot** at the inner end, so a course is about the thickness of a hand; two courses is **a foot and four**, four is **two feet eight**. Nineteen years and eight months old. Laid in a season of about seven weeks with eight men.
- **The deep off the north end** is **nine foot** of green water on a good day, and on a fourth, a sixth and a seventh the sand comes out of it and the wall stands up out of it and a flat boat can go over the top of the whole of it — **and a man on the seaward face when the ebb starts is on the wrong side of the wall from the only water there is to get off on** (141). The ebb on those three days runs about an hour and a quarter.

### THE CUSTOMS OF THIS COAST THAT ARE CANON AND THAT NOBODY ON IT HAS WRITTEN DOWN

**These are the volume's spine and they are all in somebody's mouth and none of them is on paper. A later volume may not put any of them in a book, a pane, a form or a charter without a person deciding to.**

1. **No person on four hundred miles of this coast answers for another person about a crossing.** About four hundred years old, in nobody's book, and it is the shape of a person not being used by somebody else's pen and the reason a crossing here counts a person and not a cart. **It is consent's shape** (117, 118, 143).
2. **The seventh is the one day there is nothing at all** — no stone, no question, no song that settles anything (134).
3. **The tide stone at the head of the flat is there on the first and the fourth, and a woman stands on it and answers one question about the water, in the open, out loud, and nobody may ask a second.** About a hundred years old, written down nowhere, the charter says nothing about it, and the guild has never interfered. **Roughly four hundred thousand days a week it is bare** (131, 134, 137, 145, 148).
4. **The crossing's song is sung at the stone at the turn on the seventh by Seth Garrick, thirty.** It is a tariff in four lines and it is not written down and it is about a hundred years old and it is not a court and it is not an instrument and it settles nothing (108, 134, 148).
5. **A day-book entry on this coast has no name at the end of any line and never will have, because a person who has said nothing out loud is not standing behind anything** (110, 143).
6. **The man on the chain decides who gets the tide when there is more flat than there is time, and he does it with a slate in his pocket, and there is no rule anywhere on this coast that says he does it, and he has never written it down and is not going to** (143, 147).
7. **A person who is refused something in public loses the standing they had at their stall, their boat or their flat, and feels it every day until they get it back, which is about a year** (148).
8. **A record of a moving thing is a working instrument and instruments do not travel, and the man who has one is the only man who can use it** (142, 144, 145). **A pane is a photograph of one morning taken by a man standing beside an arch, and the authority of it is the man who stood there and the four hundred people who stand at that fire after him, and the guild has been borrowing that standing and does not know it** (144).
9. **A charter on this coast is a pane, and a pane is a year, and the guild may write on anything it likes until a letting day a year off and not one word of it will be the charter** (110). **Which is why the coast's first instrument of its own is paper, and why paper can be pulled and glass cannot** (149).
10. **Nobody answers for a person. A blank is not a no and a blank is not a yes, and a party is not a silence** (110, 143).

### THE MONEY, IN ONE PLACE, AND IT CLOSES

**Taken in Chapter 112 and Chapter 116 and Chapter 113. None of it is spoken anywhere in Chapters 141–150, and the seventy-eight pounds fourteen figure is withdrawn and must not come back.**

- **The crossing takes ninety pounds eighteen a year** (21,816 pence).
- **Eighty-one pounds sixteen is tallage** on carts and goods, taken at a counter in the guild's own building by people who do not live on that road. So **the road comes to nine pounds two.**
- **Eleven households** send somebody over that flat. The chain, the oil and the tarpaulin come out of the nine pounds two and not out of the tallage: **three pounds two.**
- **Six pounds a year to the road.** **Margin of four.** The harbourmaster has kept that crossing for six years and has never once put that four pounds in front of the council.
- **The council of nine, which had not sat for six years, voted five to four** for a person to be paid **two pounds a year** to count the crossings in persons and not carts. **Two pounds out of four leaves two, and the season was three pounds two short, and four men at that table could add.** Four voted no and were right about the money; five voted yes and were right about the boy.
- **Two pounds is forty shillings, and it is nine days and a bit of Corrie Tallow's stall at four shillings and twopence a day** (113). It is not thirty-eight shillings and it is not four and a half days.
- **The tariff is a penny a tide and it is the same penny on every day of the week, including the three days the water is standing up in the middle of it, and the guild has refused twice to write a different price for a day of water and is right to.** A boy's thirty-nine tides a week at a penny is **three shillings and threepence**; the commonest household on the road pays **threepence**; that is **thirteen times** the boy's rate.
- **A bad seventh every week of every year would cost thirteen shillings** — one of the boy's two low days becoming a nine-day instead of a six-day, forty-two tides instead of thirty-nine, a hundred and fifty-six pence, three shillings and sixpence a week, **nine pounds two instead of eight pounds nine**. **There has never been a bad seventh every week in the four hundred years anybody has kept anything, and the thirteen shillings must always be said in the same breath as the fact that it has not happened, or somebody will use it in a room in about four months** (116).
- **What a man to stand at the far end of that flat would cost is two pounds, and thirteen shillings is not worth a fight, and two pounds is not worth a bad year, and the guild is not making money out of the boy and never was and could not if it tries** (116).
- **A blank wage line does its own work.** A clerk in the guild's office two hundred miles up this coast reads a quarter with a wage on it and nobody's name on it, chooses the one of his two options that writes nothing, and will choose it next quarter. **In about a year a clerk will go looking for the minute that voted it and will find that the minute says a person is to be paid, and will not be able to find the person, and will put the minute back** (110, 129, 144).
- **A day on this coast is not money.** A day is a man's own day and a cart and a pair of beasts and an afternoon at a landing that somebody else was going to lose (143, 148). **Eleven pounds of freight and two men's day is what the first refusal cost, and four years of a woman who wanted a thing written down, and a boy's thirty-nine tides** (105, 110).
- **A pane is a person, a fire and a year, and the year is eleven months of the back of the woman at the second fire, paid in advance, and it is paid whether or not there is a pane at the end of it, and nobody has asked her** (125, 128, 144, 146). **The guild's fifth pane's order is in a drawer in the tide office with the date on the outside of it and no figure against it, and the season's money is not in the arch, and the freeze is a year ending at a letting day a year off. The third of the fourth tide is long gone and is not to be used.**
- **The only money-shaped things in Chapters 141–150 are Corrie Tallow's stall at four shillings and twopence a day and a day.** A reader cannot add that batch's figures up because there are none to add, and the charter buys no stone, no cart and no day.

### THE PEOPLE, AND WHAT EACH ONE IS CARRYING AT THE END OF THE VOLUME

**Halse Tarrant, fifty-eight**, keeps the wall, laid it thirty-one years ago and walks the top course every day, has a lead line in a shed the guild put there when they laid the stone and a boat of his own that has not been out in about a year, **wants a day and has never asked, cannot get to the wall even on a bar day, and said the guild was right and asked for that to be said by him and in the open and not once in the next year by anybody else.**

**Sorrel Gannock, thirty-six**, a flat boat at the hard, four generations on the water, the only person on that coast who can get under the wall, eleven years of a thing nobody has ever asked her the name of. **She said yes to being asked and said what a name at the bottom of a thing costs, and said yes to a page of her own and not to a page with anybody else on it, and the third thing she said herself.**

**Hollis Thrale, sixty-one**, a glassmaker at Thornglass, forty-one years at the fire, deaf on the right, his grandfather's ear. His pane is three years old and on his own wall and disagrees with the guild's in two columns out of eleven and he cannot get at the guild's. **He said no first, heard it come out of his mouth, took four seconds, and said yes, and has wanted to be asked for two years.** He has never walked down that road to give his figure to the man who keeps the wall and says that is on him.

**Bettany Rook, fifty-one**, harbourmaster of Kestrel Reach, nineteen years in that office and six of them in the chair, clerk for thirteen of the nineteen. Right about the strait in her own mouth six times in fifty chapters. **A woman with two panes in oak frames that have not left her room in nineteen years and a table of tides she can read out in ninety seconds and about a year of sight left. She said no to the stone before the ask and no again before the second ask and yes to a duty he did not ask about, and refused a version of the record with a building on the end of it, and in four months will have a perfectly good reason to be at that counter for the rest of her working sight and will be the woman who handed them the reason. She was never persuaded of anything and says so.**

**Sabra Trew, twenty-four**, the third-year apprentice at the second fire, paid nothing, stands at it six days in seven, has not answered a letter of her own in four years, has cut every week on that wall for eleven years and has never once been asked to stand behind anything. **Her grandmother cut the set at the bottom of the wall, is dead eleven years, copied the list of weeks out twice in letters, and said once, to a girl of about fifteen: *that is not mine, I found it, I have been finding it since I was a girl, I have never worked it out and I am not going to guess at it in a wall.*** She refused the three pages in a shed, correctly, and the reason was about her own work and not about him, and it was that keeping them would put her in front of a question from a building two hundred miles up this coast. **She may write down that she said no and the reason. She may not write down that she was asked, because she was not.**

**Corrie Tallow, forty-eight**, four years at a slate at Wether Cross, four shillings and twopence a day, owed nothing, has never once said so. She said no for the flat in a lane in front of about nine people and gave the reason in four parts, and refused a magistrate's letter in one paragraph without softening anything, and said the two sentences about what it costs to be refused in public, and is the one who writes every crossing down and is paid nothing for it.

**Hal Bevan, fifty-four**, nineteen years on the chain, a slate in his pocket with three marks on it and no rule behind any of them, spent the only appeal of his life on a piece of paper in a dealer's hand and got a true answer, kept the paper folded in four in his breast pocket and has never taken it out again, and said *a revocation stops a thing and does not undo it* and that he is the last man on four hundred miles who ought to be telling a man something he has not worked out himself.

**Wend Garrow, thirty-three**, a carter since he was nine, lost a cart in the water three weeks before Chapter 140, got his day back on the fourth of the seventh tide, has had nowhere on this coast to put a thing that happened to a man for a month, **and is the first person on the coast to work out that he could pull the charter and is not going to, and asked for that to go in the bundle.**

**Pell Cawley, fifty-two — SETTLED AT THE VOLUME CLOSE, and the state record had it the wrong way round.** He has made and mended flat-bottomed boats on the hard at the north end for thirty-one years, has a slip of his own a long way inside the gravel, and gave the volume its one unguessed observation: a hull drawing about a thumb deeper at the bow than before he touched her, and *I have not taken anything off her and I have not put anything on, and I do not know what that is.* He also supplied the sentence that will kill the charter in about a month: **the day a man would want to spend looking at that wall is a day he cannot use, because on a fourth, a sixth and a seventh the sea is over the crown.**

**Cleve Garrow — Cleve Gannock, thirty-one**, has a house with a good roof and does not go on the water, gave a cart, a pair of beasts and an afternoon to a stranger's wall in about nine words in a lane, unasked, before anybody had asked him anything, was two hours late at a landing and told the man there it was the beasts, and has not been thanked and is not going to be asked again. **His accounts of his father's crossing are not agreed with his brothers' and may not be.**

**Jory Vance, twenty-two**, four years on the counter, writes down what happened while it is happening, is the only person in that building who does not know what Bettany Rook wants, and has a brush in his cuff. **Seth Garrick, thirty**, sold lamp oil, was the crossing's reading man for eleven months at two pounds a year four years ago, has been counting the notches of the tally post in his head for four years, believes he may be getting it wrong, gave a man of thirty-one a bed over a shed for standing at the post with a hammer and a piece of paper at a turn. **The count is wrong and it will outlast the post, and it is the only count of that post that will ever be made.** **Gell Roke, seventy-one**, has painted the staff at the harbour mouth for nineteen years for nothing, and is the man who worked out what a bad seventh costs and who said *there is no person on this coast to be it, so it will be whoever is on the stones.* **Nim Hask, eleven**, runs a lead over nine hundred yards of wet shingle thirty-nine times a week at a penny. **Bel Hask, forty-four**, mends nets and pays three shillings and threepence a week for a thing that is not in the tariff and has said *do not tell the guild.* **Danno Ray**, sells lime, has said *a rule that cannot be changed for a year is not a rule, it is a season* twice on this coast out loud to about nine people and been answered twice by nobody. **Belva Cray, fifty-one**, keeps a school a hundred and forty miles up the coast and has a copy of a four-hundred-year list of weeks in the back of her register with a sheet laid in front of it in Corrie Tallow's name saying it is a record of nothing. **Mare** is dead and her name is inside the lid of the batch book at Thornglass, which is how you keep a name.

**Alma Gannock, eighty-one — STILL NOT TOLD, AND THE VOLUME'S SPINE IS STILL UNSPENT.** A woman of eighty-one, four miles above the north end, knitting, with a boy of eight in the hut on a board with a slate putting something on it and getting it wrong, and she is not correcting him. **She was asked how many carts and was not asked what the bottom line says, and there is a card at the end of a bar road with her name at the end of the bottom line, and he wrote the question he asked and the one he did not on the same page in the same hand and gave it to Bettany Rook, who read it and called it the most honest piece of paper she has read in six years and the least use anybody has ever made of one.** She is referred to once in the volume, in his own interior in Chapter 142, in four sentences, on a road he does not go up. **He has worked out where he was going and left that road off, and nobody on this coast knows it, and nobody is going to be told. Her boy of eight is in the hut and nothing whatever happens to him.**

**Ansa Rell** wrote once, in Chapter 139, by post, and was refused correctly. **She is not softened, not redeemed, and Volume 03 came near redeeming her nowhere.** **Neris Ro was not asked for anything and her right to stop everything herself stands, including from him. Tamsin Ro was not called and nobody looked for her. Sera Quill is not on the coast and did not come.**

### THE DEVICES, EXACTLY WHERE VOLUME 02 LEFT THEM

**The afterimage, the left hand, the rendering, the vantage, the door and the token are all exactly where Volume 02 left them and none of them is advanced in this volume.** The rendering comes up in 110, 124, 140 and 150, always at the same height and the same distance since the third of the second month in a city four hundred miles behind him, and it never moves and it never changes. **THE LEG IS ATTACKED TWELVE TIMES IN CHAPTERS 101–150, NOT TEN AND NOT FIVE, and both of the earlier counts were wrong in the same direction, which is the whole finding. The first pass of this close recorded five because it listed only the attacks from Chapter 131 on; the second pass corrected that to ten and was still short by two. The twelve, in order, each with the line it is on: 104:15 (in a boat, from the hip down); 107:51 (on a crate with his back against a warm wall at Thornglass, from the hip down, all of it, *the way it goes when a seam comes apart in front of him*); 109:9 (on a stool on a bar road); 120:104 (at the second swing, from the hip down, all of it); 123:111 (on him at the second rung of a ladder in a net loft); 127:81 (on the last of the nine hundred yards of dry shingle, all the way, from the hip down); 129:118 (at the top of a rise four miles up a coast road); 131:63 (*Then the leg went*); 138:9 (in a boat on the bar at the crown); 140:90 (on wet shingle, from the hip down, all of it); 145:13 (on wet shingle before he got to the wall); and 150:43 (with his hand flat on brick, from the hip down, all of it). **Chapter 107 is the one both earlier counts dropped and it is the load-bearing one: the second pass's own table fix puts 107 and 110 on the same day, and 110:49 is the four hours at the Thornglass arch that end with *his leg was his own*, so 107 is the attack immediately preceding the volume's only clearing. A record that lists the clearing and drops the attack that sets it up is not a short list, it is a wrong story.****

**THE GREP, SO THAT THE NEXT READER GETS TWELVE AND NOT FOURTEEN. A scan for *the leg went / goes / gone* and for *leg that had gone / leg was gone* across Chapters 101–150 returns FOURTEEN chapters, and two of the fourteen are not attacks in their own right. The twelve are the list above. The two are **Chapter 124:87** (*a leg that had gone from the hip down at the second bell on a moving deck*), which looks back to Chapter 120, four days earlier, whose leg went at the second swing, and **Chapter 130:119**, which is the defective summary sentence recorded as standing defect 8 and not an event at all. Two further near-misses are named so that nobody adds them later: **Chapter 129's attack is real and is counted at 129, but the prose reaches it retrospectively rather than in the moment** — 129:118 is *on a leg that had gone at the top of the rise* — so a reader looking for a narrated attack in 129 will not find one and should not conclude the chapter is clear; and **Chapter 121 narrates a WORSENING rather than an attack** — *a leg that has been bad for a month and it had been going the whole two miles and by the fourth bell it was in the hip* at 121:89, and *the leg was in the whole of him now* at 121:133 — which reads as the tail of Chapter 120's attack on the following morning. **A later agent who wants a fourteenth should say in the record why 124 and 130 are not one, and not simply leave them off a list.****

**It has cleared exactly once on this coast, for about four hours, on the fourth of the third tide at Thornglass, and TEN CHAPTERS SAY SO, not five: 125:9, 130:119, 134:101, 137:87, 138:109, 139:115, 144:79, 145:15, 149:94 and 150:5. That is the complete set — a scan for *fourth of the third tide* across Chapters 101–150 returns ten chapters and no more, and every one of the ten either states the four hours or states that it has not been his own since. The first pass listed five of them, the second pass listed the same five, and both presented a partial enumeration as a set, which is the mechanism behind the two short attack lists in the paragraph above. And it has not cleared since. NO FILE AND NO CHAPTER IN THIS VOLUME MAY SAY IT HAS NOT CLEARED IN A MINUTE OF THE FIFTY-NINE DAYS; the narrow form is the one the prose uses.** He told nobody, every time, and about nine people on this coast have now watched something happen to him and not one of them has ever mentioned it. **Corva Silt and no clerk is the only person who knows he cannot read. The count of four is unchanged — Corrie Tallow, Bettany Rook, Pether Voke and Belva Cray — and not one of them has ever said a word about it. He says it himself in 143 and in 150 and nobody remarks on it either time.** He was produced four times on this coast and the mechanism of production is four hundred years old and is in nobody's book and he was not going to be produced a fifth.

### THE VOLUME GUARDRAILS, CHECKED ONE BY ONE, AGAINST THE TEXT

**Do not restore the historical right of refusal — HELD.** Not restored, and Chapter 150 says so in a paragraph about a piece of paper about a wall: *whether it ever was is a question about four hundred years and about a quarter of a sentence that somebody entered a long time ago, which is four hundred miles behind him and not on this coast, and nobody on this coast is going to answer it and nothing on this coast was offered to anybody as its answer.* **Do not advance the authorship question or the Shale Mirror — HELD.** Neither appears. The C—2 chain, the plate, the fifth mark and the preservation authority are not chased and he says the reason out loud (142, 144). **Do not answer the Bridgeward — HELD.** Chapter 100 is not restaged and is not used as a precedent; the coast's version of the same shape is a bar road with a woman of eighty-one on it, and the road is not touched. **Do not introduce a new final enemy — HELD.** A full-text scan finds no *First Witness* and no new antagonist of any kind; the antagonist is a category with no villain and the guild is right. **Do not give Ilyan a party, a bench, a body or a remedy he chose alone — HELD.** He has none and the volume says so. **Do not make the System omniscient — HELD.** It translated seven documents in fifty chapters and recognised one structure it was not asked for, and it translated no song, tide table, harbour custom, tariff board, pane, kiln wall, slate, post, wheel, rope, stake, lead line, shop figure, school letter or city letter, and **no panel anywhere in the volume certifies a join.** **Do not name a weekday — HELD,** by full-text scan. **Do not name a month the coast has not earned — HELD,** with the three inherited exceptions named above. **Preserve ordinary scale — HELD.** Nobody on this coast is a wizard, a chosen one, a smuggler with a title, or a person who explains the strait; the strait is explained by a man of seventy-one with a pot of paint, in four sentences, once.

### THE REPETITION BAR, AND HOW IT WAS KEPT

**Not written in this volume: a seventh local arrangement, another bounded interval, another room that declines to say anything, another round of written answers from a set of benches, another bench minute as the hero of a chapter, another public answer that turns out to be a price, another fragment recovered from a facing leaf. Not re-staged: a child asked to testify, a private account filed without consent, a preservation office confronted, a plate and an index compared, a foot-gate that opens the wrong way, a barrow that is a load, a handbell attached to nothing, a burden assigned to nobody, a keeper saying he does not read a clause, the toll bar, the four lines off the ninth bridge's frame, the form with a word at the top of it, the bill and the spring ranking, the page with no successor.** **The one marker in a working record that nobody can account for is a driven stake in a wall, and it is shown beside nothing, settles nothing, and cannot be produced at all because it is in the wall** (138). **A child was not asked to testify. A private account was not filed without consent. Chapter 100 was not restaged.** **The three Gannock brothers are staged as themselves and their accounts do not agree, which is the volume's ordinary work and not the closed device, because no account is merged, no account is asked to agree, and the merge that does happen is a false merge of three accounts the protagonist himself assembled and that cost a carter a day and a day's hire** (121–123). **The one answer in the volume that turns out to be a price is a refusal, and it is priced in a day, and a day is a man, a cart, a pair of beasts and an afternoon at a landing, and it is not a price anybody charges** (143, 148).

### STANDING DEFECTS FOUND AT THE VOLUME CLOSE AND NOT REPAIRED, with chapter and line

1. **Chapter 116, line 3, `the sixth of the fifth tide`; and line 88, `The ninth crossing of the sixth day of the fifth tide`.** **Two faults.** Its position between the seventh of the fourth tide (Chapter 115) and the second of the fifth tide (Chapter 117) requires the **first** of the fifth tide, which is day 23; as printed it is day 28. And its own content requires a bar day — it is about nine crossings, it names the sixth four times, and it calls the day the worst day of that week — so it cannot be moved to the first of the fifth without rewriting the chapter as a good-day chapter, which would change what the chapter is for. **CONSEQUENCES, NAMED: (a) the tide run is broken for one chapter in the middle of Volume 03; (b) the absolute day-count is broken for the same chapter, since the seventh of the fifth tide is day 29 everywhere else; (c) nothing in Chapters 117–150 depends on either, because the run from Chapter 120 forward is anchored at Chapter 120's twenty-nine days and closes forward to 59; (d) a Volume 04 agent who tries to date anything before Chapter 120 from this volume must not use Chapter 116. THE HONEST OPTIONS ARE (i) leave it and never build on it, which is what this close did, or (ii) rewrite Chapter 116's day as the first of the fifth tide and rebuild its four bar-day scenes, which is a Batch-scale job and not a close's.**
2. **Chapter 145, closing line, `the fourth time in ten days`.** The same chapter says *the second day in a week* and *standing there empty since the first of the ninth*, and both are right. The count of four is right measured from Wend Garrow on the fourth of the seventh tide, and that is **fourteen** days. **Three frames for one count in one chapter and the closing line is the odd one out. The fix is a wording decision, not a number swap, because two of the three frames are correct and the chapter's own premise fixes the third.**
3. **Chapter 148, Wend Garrow's mouth, `I have been standing on this flat for a day and a half finding that out`.** `state/batch-summary.md` Part six item 7 states this span was corrected in Chapters 147 and 148. **It was corrected in the narration four lines above and not here.** **The second pass of this close established the exact figure and it is this: the narration puts him at the head of the flat *since the fourth bell* that evening, and he says he had heard on the sixth — the sixth of the ninth tide, Chapter 147's day, the day the finding was said out loud — so the span is from yesterday evening to this evening, and *since yesterday* is exact and *a day and a half* is about half a day out.** Not repaired, for the reason given under the closing line of Chapter 145: the fix is a wording decision and not a number swap, and this close does not make wording decisions in closed prose.
4. **Chapter 149, line 9, `in a room with a door shut`.** The chapter's opening argument is that paper can be crossed out and glass cannot, and it forecasts that one of the three will cross her own out in a room with a door shut; the charter the same chapter sets down requires a revocation to be **said in the open at the head of that flat**. Either the crossing-out is not the revocation, in which case the line needs to say so, or it is, in which case it contradicts the terms.
5. **Straight apostrophes on 116 lines in Chapters 101–120** (Batches 0001 and 0002 of this volume), on 405 in Volume 01 and 740 in Volume 02. The house style was normalised in Batches 0003, 0004 and 0005 and never in the first two. Purely typographic.
6. **Chapter 82, one doubled closing curly quote at the end of a speech.** Volume 02 canon, found by a whole-manuscript scan, one character, outside this volume.
7. **Chapters 109 and 110 are four days out of tide order, and this is the second instance of the Chapter 116 class and it is in a chapter this close repaired.** Chapter 110's own day is the sixth of the third tide, which is day 14 and the same day as Chapter 107; Chapter 109 sits on the first or second of the fourth tide, days 16–17, and comes before Chapter 110 in the book. **THE TIDE RUN IS THEREFORE BROKEN FOR TWO CHAPTERS AND NOT ONE, and the correction made in Chapter 110 made it visible rather than causing it: before the repair Chapter 110 claimed fifteen days and stood at day 15, which is Chapter 108's day, so the same pair of chapters were out of order either way.** Nothing in Chapters 111–150 depends on it, because the run from Chapter 120 forward is anchored at Chapter 120's twenty-nine days and closes forward to 59. **A Volume 04 agent must not date Chapters 101–110 from this volume's run and must not build anything on the order of 107, 108, 109 and 110.** The honest options are the same two as for Chapter 116: leave it, or re-date Chapter 109 — which is a Batch-scale job, because Chapter 109's whole shape is a three-week-old discovery arriving on a bar road.
8. **Chapter 130, closing paragraph, `a leg that had gone to the hip three times since the fourth of the sixth tide`.** Found by the second verification pass of this close; it is not in the batch record and no earlier pass found it. **THE ITEMISATION IS NOT THE UNIQUE MAPPING THE SECOND PASS ASSERTED, and the correction matters more than the conclusion it qualified. The second pass mapped *once in a boat on a bar day* to Chapter 104, *once all the way from the hip down on nine hundred yards of shingle* to Chapter 120, and *at the top of a rise four miles up a coast road* to Chapter 129, and presented all three as findings. Only the third is secure: 129:118 is *on a leg that had gone at the top of the rise*. The second is a guess presented as a match — 120:104 reads *The leg went in about the second swing and went all the way, from the hip down*, with no shingle in the sentence at all, and **Chapter 127:81 matches the itemisation considerably better**: *walked out about nine hundred yards of dry shingle to the end of the flat and stood there and let the sea go off it, and the leg went in on the last of the nine hundred and it went all the way, from the hip down*. The first is shakier than either: Chapter 104 is a boat and is day 10, which is a GOOD day and takes six, so *on a bar day* does not fit it; the only boat attack on a bar day is 138, which is nine chapters after 130 and cannot be in a retrospective. **So the sentence's three clauses map onto at least two different chapters under at least two readings, and nobody should build a figure on which.****

   **WHAT SURVIVES, and it is the reason this is still a defect rather than a footnote: the count is wrong under every frame, and now by more than the second pass said.** The leg is narrated going in **seven** chapters before this one — 104, 107, 109, 120, 123, 127 and 129 — and 127 was one of the two the second pass's count of five had dropped. Whichever three chapters the sentence means, the earliest available attack is Chapter 104 on day 10 or Chapter 107 on day 14, and **no candidate start date in the sentence reaches back to either**: the fourth of the sixth tide is day 33, the fourth of the fifth is day 26, and the fourth of the third is day 12, which is the day the leg cleared and the anchor the same sentence uses four clauses later. So the earliest attack precedes every candidate start date, and at least one of the seven falls inside the six days the sentence names. **NO SINGLE EDIT FIXES BOTH THE COUNT AND THE DATE, and swapping a number for a number is the failure that made the second pass over Chapters 141–150 worse than the pass it was fixing.** Recorded, not repaired. **Nobody may build a figure on the leg's history in Chapters 101–139 from this sentence; the twelve narrated attacks are listed under *THE DEVICES* above and those are the chapters, not this summary.**
9. **Chapter 110, lines 39 and 152, `in fifteen days` and `the first time in fifteen days`, both left standing deliberately.** The two instances this close did repair in that chapter are the arrival window — *He was on the coast fourteen days* and *the second kindest thing anybody did for him in fourteen days* — and they are anchored by Chapter 120's twenty-nine days fifteen days later. These two are not that window. Line 39 is about what the coast has done for a boy of eleven, whose crossing custom Corrie Tallow says has been bought off for eleven years, so the window opens before Ilyan arrived; line 152's window is the coast's own worst question, and the sentence before it in the same paragraph says *for a fortnight*, which is fourteen. **Line 39 must not be changed. Line 152 is arguably one out and a writer may change it; this close did not, because the chapter does not define the window and changing it would be a guess dressed as a repair.**
10. **Chapter 121, line 141, `a leg that had not cleared in thirty days`.** Found by the review repair pass, and it is the one place in the volume where a figure of the right shape is wrong on the device's own meaning. **The leg cleared once, on the fourth of the third tide, which is day 12; Chapter 121 is the first of the sixth tide, which is day 30; so the span in which it has not been his own is EIGHTEEN days, and not thirty.** The chapter's own line 89 says the leg *has been bad for a month*, and a month is about thirty days, so the sentence is defensible on the reading *has not got well in thirty days* and is wrong on the reading the rest of the volume gives *cleared* — the word the device uses at 125:9, 130:119, 134:101, 137:87, 138:109, 144:79, 145:15 and 150:5, and the word the standing instruction in *THE DEVICES* is written to protect. **The two readings cannot both stand, and which one the sentence means is a judgement about the prose and not a subtraction, so it is recorded and not repaired. It is also the reason line 89 and line 141 must never be quoted together as two instances of the same figure: one is his thirty days on the coast and the other is the leg's, and only the first is a coast count.**

### WHAT THE VOLUME CLOSE REPAIRED, and what it re-verified and did not repair

**Repaired, six changes and nothing else: Chapter 110 twice (*fifteen* → *fourteen* days), checked against Chapters 120, 129 and 135. Chapter 140 once (*four days ago* → *three days ago*), checked against Chapter 137 and against Chapter 145's *eight days*. Chapters 141 and 148 (Pell Cawley, *fifty* → *fifty-two*), checked against Chapter 122 and Chapter 140. Forty-one paragraph-opening quotation marks in Chapters 131, 133, 135, 136, 137, 138, 139 and 140, checked against the house style in the other one hundred and forty-two chapters. Chapter 147 once (*a mile and a half* → *a mile and a flight of stairs up a hill*), checked against six other instances of that walk. Chapters 1–100 were not touched at all.**

**Re-verified against the prose and found sound: the tide run from Chapter 120 to Chapter 150 and every day-count in it; every interval in Chapters 141–150; the bar-day and good-day crossing counts; the stone custom across 131, 134, 137, 145 and 148; the eleven-times-in-twenty rate; the panel discipline and the WHAT IS NOT DECIDED lines; the absence of any reference to the book in anybody's mouth; the absence of a weekday, a month-date and the word the volume must not use; the pane never given courses; the distances attached to panes; the wall's course arithmetic; the two-hundred-and-sixty-first-against-two-hundred-and-sixty-four inference running the right way; the five pages and no sixth across 139, 140, 143, 149 and 150; the charter's terms as quoted; the recognition as a single unattached line that is not a panel; the four names on the record and the three on the sheets; and the whole of Parts five and six of the batch verification record, every repair of which is present in the prose as it is said to be.**

**And the thing the next agent must know about its own evidence: the log file that the state record cites as the record of the independent reading of Chapters 141–150 does not exist, and never was committed, because `logs/` and `*.log` are in `.gitignore`. The same is true of the three other review logs the record cites. The findings survive only as prose in `state/batch-summary.md` Parts five and six. The repairs in them hold. The artefact does not.**

### THE SECOND VERIFICATION PASS AT THE CLOSE, and what it found in this file

**The close ran twice. The first pass wrote the record above; the second pass went back to the chapters with the record open, which is the only order in which a record like this can be caught. It made NO change to any chapter — it found nothing it could repair with the chapter and the line and the check named — and it found FOUR ERRORS IN THIS FILE, all of them introduced or copied by the first pass, and two new standing defects in the prose.**

1. **The Day column of the clock table was wrong for Chapters 101–110 and internally contradicted its own rows from Chapter 111 down.** Corrected above. The first pass had those ten chapters five days out, which put Chapter 107 and Chapter 110 on the same day and Chapter 109 after both. **It has since been established that those ten rows are a positional inference and not a fact read out of prose, because Chapters 101–110 state no tide day and no day-count at all, and the correction is now labelled as the inference it is.**
2. **The claim that Chapter 112 states an absolute day-count of nineteen was false.** Its two *nineteen*s are *nineteen years*. **The correction of this correction is above: both of them are Gell Roke's, in the same speech, at 112:59 and 112:63, and Bettany Rook's nineteen years is at 102:7. The second pass put her into 112 while correcting a claim about 112.** The true list is given above and is now **twenty-four instances in sixteen chapters, not the nineteen in eleven that this pass reported after counting them itself.**
3. **The device record said the leg was attacked five times. It is twelve.** The five the first pass listed were all from Chapter 131 on. The second pass corrected that to ten and dropped 107 and 127; **Chapter 107 is the attack immediately before the volume's only clearing**, which the second pass's own table fix had put on the same day as the clearing.
4. **The intervals line under the clock table said the fourth of the third tide to the sixth of the third tide is three days. It is two.** The tide is seven days, the fourth of the third is day 12 and the sixth is day 14. **This is the only one of the five that is not a miscounted chapter: it is arithmetic, done in a line whose whole purpose was to stop the next agent doing arithmetic.** The other eight intervals on that line were recomputed and are right. Corrected above, and the line is left in full so that all nine can be checked in one place. **One of the nine, 2 of 7 → 1 of 8, was also printed running backward.**
5. **Two standing defects the first pass did not find, both in the prose: Chapters 109 and 110 are out of tide order, and Chapter 130's closing paragraph states a count and an interval for the leg that the volume's own narration does not support under any of the three available frames.** Both are now recorded above with the evidence, and **neither was repaired, because in both cases a repair would have meant substituting one number for another.** **The second pass's itemisation of Chapter 130's three clauses was also a guess presented as a finding, and its count of the attacks before that chapter was five where it is seven; both are corrected in the entry above, and the entry now gives the second and third mappings as readings rather than as results.**

**The honest summary of the two passes together: the first pass found and repaired six things in six chapters and settled the four defects the batch had flagged. The second pass repaired nothing, found four errors in the first pass's own record and two new standing defects in closed prose, and made the interval line checkable. A close that repairs prose and then writes its own record is still writing a record, and it still has to be checked against the chapters by somebody — and the next volume starts by inheriting a file that has now been wrong five times in two passes about the same four things: the clock, the intervals, a leg, and a chapter that states a figure.**

### THE REVIEW REPAIR PASS, and the third round of short lists

**A review ran over the second pass's output and this close's record. It changed no chapter. It confirmed the arithmetic, the tide run, the 109/110 defect, the Chapter 148 span and the shape of the day-count list, and it found SIX THINGS, of which two were high, two medium and two low: the twelve attacks where the record said ten, a fresh wrong attribution in the very correction that removed another, an itemisation asserted as unique that the prose does not support, a second tide map 780 lines down that contradicts the corrected table, a partial list of the chapters that state the single clearing, and one interval printed backward. All six are corrected above and in the other five files the pass names.**

**AND THE PASS WAS ITSELF SHORT ON THE ONE FIGURE IT CONFIRMED, which is the finding that matters most here and the reason this section exists. It recounted the day-count instances, got nineteen in eleven chapters, checked every one of the nineteen against the table, and reported the list sound. **The list is twenty-four in sixteen chapters**: the pass dropped 121:89, 123:109, 131:21, 134:95 and 138:109.** All five agree with the corrected table, so the table is better confirmed than the record claimed, and the record's exclusion note for Chapter 121 was wrong on both halves — one of that chapter's two *thirty days* is a coast count and only the other is the leg. This is the third round in this volume in which a figure assembled from a partial read came out short and was then handed on as a set: five attacks, then ten, then a partial list of the chapters that state the clearing, then nineteen day-counts. **The pattern is not carelessness and it does not respond to being told to be careful. It responds to being made checkable, which is why the twenty-four and the twelve now carry line numbers and the clearing list is a complete scan.** A Volume 04 agent should re-run the greps before citing any of these three lists, and should treat a count in this file without a line number beside it as unverified until they have.**

## Canon established at bootstrap

- Veyra is a real, living world with civic promises that physically affect roads, water, weather, boundaries, inheritance, and other shared systems.
- The Bench is a distributed judgment network. Its rules are expressed through covenants, challenges, witnesses, burdens, rulings, and temporary exceptions.
- The System is the translated interface for Ilyan, not an omniscient god. It translates formal Bench records, notices, and hearing-field boundaries entered into his open case; it does not translate ambient speech, trade shorthand, tone, or local custom. It cannot read minds, force consent, resurrect the dead, or guarantee moral truth.
- The current Bench descends from the Loom, built collectively by the First Compact. The Warden’s Codification followed the Great Silence and removed or restricted the public right of refusal.
- The First Witness is the old archive intelligence inside the Codification. It is seeded early as a plausible final adversary, not a late surprise threat.
- The Shale Mirror is a one-way translation fragment of the Loom, not the source of Veyra. It connected Morrowlight’s cancelled game to Veyra. Ilyan’s defect report is the record, his tester signature is the external signature, and the repeated reports together form the first external null witness. A separate one-use null passage used a legacy emergency clause to pull him into a hearing field; the Shale Mirror relay itself cannot move people.
- Ilyan Vester is a male former game tester, age thirty-one, with pattern-recognition and documentation skills but no initial supernatural protection. His arc moves from seeking acquittal and escape to sharing responsibility and relinquishing authorship.
- Sera Quill is the assigned recordwright, Ilyan’s first provisional advocate, and the single slow-burn romantic partner. She becomes formally licensed after the Volume 1 hearing. Nadia Kestrel is an Earth anchor and non-romantic co-witness; Tarin Keel and Bram Orren are recurring allies with independent wants.
- The planned final verdict rejects both the game reading and the prison reading. Veyra becomes a plural civic order in which rules are visible, reviewable, refusable, and revisable.

## Opening continuity established in prose

The first movement begins on Earth and shows Ilyan’s involuntary crossing before the Orison action. The signed *Sovereign’s Wake* refusal report was never merged, but its sequence was recognized as an external challenge. The black shale fragment beneath Morrowlight’s room answered the wording; the living-person crossing came from a separate legacy null passage. Ilyan carried a spent blackglass token into Orison with no return route, no automatic burden, and no informed consent. The passage consumed the ninth bridge’s emergency reserve and worsened a western-joint drift that was already present.

Ilyan lands at the ninth bridge as the First Defendant. Tamsin Ro, age ten, is rescued from beneath a bridge whose charter protects registered lawful travelers but excludes the Ro household. Oren Sedge’s bridgekeeper crew uses a clamp, drive pin, and named load path to keep the child alive. The rescue does not settle Neris Ro’s access to work, housing, or the bridge.

## Volume 02 AUDIT — defects found in canon prose on the first of the seventh month

**The volume PASSED. The audit is at `reviews/volume-02/volume-audit.md`. It wrote no prose. The following twelve defects are INSIDE Chapters 51–100, which this phase is forbidden to edit. They are recorded so that no later batch builds on them. Where a state file repeated one, the state file has been corrected here and the chapter stands as canon.**

- **Two exact duplications in Chapter 100.** Line 134 repeats a whole clause verbatim from the end of line 132 and opens a quotation mark that is never closed; line 140 repeats *"What I want to know is why."* entire, four lines after line 138 ends with the identical sentence. The sentence-level scan of all fifty chapters at four words minimum found these two and no others. **This is the volume's only duplicated sentence and the only duplicated paragraph that is not a correct one.** (The one exact duplicated paragraph in the volume is the barrel lid, quoted identically in Chapters 88 and 100, which is a physical object and is correct.)
- **Chapter 96, line 63: "two months and nine days" is twenty-two days.** Corrected in four state locations.
- **Chapter 98, line 45: the form arithmetic does not close.** 400 ordered + 320 ordered = 720; 463 held + 140 in the shop's drawer = 603; 117 unaccounted. **463 is load-bearing — it is the eighth thing Ilyan entered and it runs through Chapter 100. Do not restate the order figures.**
- **Chapter 99, line 5: "A clerk of thirty-eight"** — Perdy Sill is thirty-eight and of a coal yard and is not a clerk. The volume's convention is *a clerk of twenty-six years* (Hulda Vane) and *a clerk of thirty-one* (Corva Silt).
- **Chapter 99, line 77: "has not been in this case since the twenty-fifth of the fourth month"** — contradicted by Chapter 96, earlier in the same volume.
- **Chapter 80, line 122 vs Chapter 91, line 94: two procedures both called the fourth.** Corrected in the Batch 0003 section above.
- **Chapter 70, lines 79 and 87: the refusal in Chapter 70 is called the sixth.** It is the fourth. Four later chapters on later dates are consistent only with the fourth. The appearance count (sixth) and the refusal count (fourth) were conflated.
- **Chapter 59, line 61: "twelve miles"** contradicts "four miles" in the same speech and the canon distance.
- **Chapter 98, line 99 and Chapter 89, line 81: two month names one month out** — "In March" for an act Chapter 70 dates to the twenty-second of the second month, and "in March" for a vote Chapter 68 holds on the seventeenth of the second month. **January is the first month, February the second, March the third, April the fourth, May the fifth, June the sixth, July the seventh; the calendar reconciles with the numbered months everywhere else.**
- **The water rate's start day is given two ways** — the eighteenth in Chapter 69's narrative at line 3 and on the board's own printed text at line 5, the seventeenth of the second month in Chapters 87, 88 and 100. Say "the second month" and do not pick a day.
- **Chapter 92, line 169: "ninety days"** is ninety-four from the third of the second month and ninety-one from the sixth. Treat it as Ilyan's own count and build nothing on it.
- **Chapter 92, lines 119 and 137 against line 145: a hand that cannot open is "still open on the table"** eight lines later.

**Also corrected in place, from figures the chapters do not support:** the second carrier's-clause ask cost *a day and a hedge* (Ch 85), not four shillings and sixpence; the third production cost *three shillings and ninepence and a day and a half* (Ch 99), not four shillings and sixpence; four shillings and sixpence belongs to the appeal notice in Chapter 96. **Two chapter titles in `state/chapter-summaries.md` did not match the prose headings and now do.**

## Power and failure constraints

No respawns, save points, character replacement, or unearned level-ups. A failed ruling remains consequential. A burden must be named and may not be silently assigned to an unwilling person. Death is permanent. Ilyan grows through practice, failure, relationships, changed decisions, and permanent costs. A Foundation Challenger may open a blank interval only with notice, consent from people remaining in the full spill zone, and safe withdrawal margins; the final use permanently costs Ilyan the lived memory of filing the *Sovereign’s Wake* refusal report.

## Ending constraints

The final adversary is the First Witness already introduced in the Bench. Ilyan must reject the Final Exception even though it will work, give up permanent authority and a personal return route, and help create distributed regional charters. The final image shows an ordinary person refusing a specific rule with visible consequences and remaining part of the community. No new cosmic threat is planned.

## Deliberately closed or held — do not advance

- The historical right of refusal. **The right of anybody to answer no is not restored. The fragment is ten words with a crease in them, entered as one quarter of a sentence on the twelfth of the fifth month, and it is not a recovery of the clause.**
- The authorship question and the Shale Mirror.
- The responsible person for the C—2 chain and the standing preservation authority. **Ilyan has said out loud three times that he is not going to, most recently in a lane on the sixteenth of the fifth month.**
- The first witness behind the fifth mark in the count book. Ilsa's grandmother, the woman paid off the load book, and the levy ledger's cut name remain three separate things and Ilsa has still not been asked to decide.
- The Quiet Chamber's original page and Sera's duplicate remain two events on two dates.
- **The Bridgeward's toll and its dead witness list. It is now been put as a question about a person, out loud, on a mud bank, by a boy of seventeen, and it is not answered. It is not to be investigated in a way that damages a road that works.**
- The materials store and the Ro household's ground floor.
- The plate and the older witness index are one document cut once — a result, not a fresh clue.
- **The fifth mark in the count book, the preservation index, and a plate-and-index comparison are all used and closed. Do not restage any of them.**

## BATCH 0004 REPAIR PASS, AFTER THE REVIEW OF THIS BATCH — **NINE FINDINGS, ALL REPAIRED, NO CHAPTER RESTARTED, NO CARD MOVED, NO PLOT CHANGED. A LATER PASS THAT FINDS AN OLD FIGURE IN ONE OF THE FILES BELOW IS READING A FILE THIS BLOCK SUPERSEDES.**

**WHAT THE REVIEW FOUND BLOCKING, AND WHAT IS NOW TRUE.** **THE BATCH 0005 BOARD TABLE DID NOT ADD UP FROM ROW 295 DOWN.** The off-by-one rule says the morning in row *N* is the morning in row *N-1* plus the rings printed in row *N*, and the running line had `63,799 + 8 = 63,813` at row 295, so six consecutive mornings were inflated by eight and the volume's closing figure was eight out. **The six are corrected to 63,807 / 63,813 / 63,817 / 63,825 / 63,833 / 63,841, the ten rings sum to sixty-seven, and 63,774 + 67 = 63,841.** **SO 63,841 IS THE FIGURE AT THE SECOND LIGHT ON CHAPTER 300'S MORNING AND NOT 63,849, and the difference from 63,510 is 331 and not 339, and of that 331 the two hundred still arrived at the start of Batch 0004 and the other 131 is fifty days of a village doing things. Chapter 300's own day of eight is NOT in the table, because the last row's rings are Chapter 299's own day, so the first morning after the volume is 63,849 and that figure is derived in the fiction and printed in no table and in no card.** **CHAPTER 281 SAID ITS OWN DAY THREE WAYS** — a stranger, then four of the clock and three on it, then a day of four — **and the chain needs seven, because 63,714 plus seven is 63,721.** It now says the day was going to be seven and came to seven and it was not the ordinary one. **The two Batch 0004 tables were not wrong; their column headers were**, and both now read *the rings of the day BEFORE this morning, four plus*, the ten own-days are printed on a line of their own, and `state/current.md`'s running line no longer prints `63,710 + 7 = 63,714` with a tick on it. **The ten own-days, which is the only reading of the chain that does not put a chapter's own day on the row before it: 281 = 7, 282 = 7, 283 = 13, 284 = 4, 285 = 4, 286 = 6, 287 = 8, 288 = 4, 289 = 7, 290 = 4, and the ten sum to sixty-four, and 63,710 + 64 = 63,774.**

**THE SEVEN PROSE FIXES, one line each, and no prose was touched except where the fault was in the prose.** `283:5` dated the man of thirty-four's departure to the fourth day of a week whose fourth day is the next chapter and contradicted his own return at `281:71`; it is the second day out and the third day back. `283:101` gave the next morning a ninth stroke on top of its four and `284:3` gave the same morning four bells and nothing on top of them and called the thirteen of the day before the nine; the morning now stands at four with the nine of the day before still on the wall above it, and the woman of about fifty has had one thirteen in eleven weeks and one about four years back, which is what `283:3` has always said. `287:5` said there were not about nine people in the Coldharrow yard at any hour at all and `287:47` put nine of them there at the ninth hour, and `287:51` put that yard nine miles off; line 5 is now scoped to before the ninth hour, line 47 says her own yard, and line 51 says this yard. `290:57` put twenty-two years at a bar inside the mouth of a recordwright of twenty-eight who has never stood at one; it is six years as a recordwright, and the twenty-two years stay with the woman of about fifty at `290:51`. `290:91` dated 63,510 to the first morning of the fortieth day, which is this morning; it is the first morning of the first day of his being in this country, which is what `280:65` has always said. And `290:91` printed the morning figure a second time in the same shape as `290:3`, so that figure has been taken out and the sentence says the board is two hundred and sixty-four higher this morning, which is the same arithmetic and one line instead of two.

**THE TWO RECORD FIXES. THE WITHIN-BATCH FORTY-CHARACTER SPAN COUNT HAS BEEN PRINTED THREE TIMES AND ALL THREE ARE HERE: this delivery's first printing said 1,210 and was wrong, the review measured 1,266 against the chapters as first written, and the delivered chapters now measure 1,156 because the repair took the repeated figure out of `290:91` and gave Chapters 283, 285 and 290 three fresh frames. 1,156 IS THE FIGURE THAT BELONGS TO THE FILES ON DISK.** **AND THE VOLUME'S TWO NON-PANEL TRACES WERE CITED AT `272:41` AND `289:57` AND NEITHER CITATION RESOLVED: `chapter-0272.md` HAS NO ITALIC LINE AT ALL AND THE BATCH 0003 TRACE IS AT `272:11`, WHICH IS THE SENTENCE ABOUT THE ROOM HE HAD STOPPED GOING TO, AND THE BATCH 0004 TRACE IS AT `289:63`.** Both are now cited correctly here and in `state/batch-summary.md`, `state/character-state.md`, `state/open-threads.md` and `workspace/volume-06/batch-0005/PROMPT.md`. **The budget is unchanged and it matters: there are TWO non-panel traces in the volume and Batch 0005 may not add a third.** **AND THE SAME FAULT WAS IN THE CROSS-BAND HALF OF THE SAME RECORD AND IS REPAIRED WITH IT: Chapters 241 to 270 are TWENTY files on disk and not thirty, and the cross-band forty-character count is 525 summed over the two hundred chapter pairs or 366 as a union of spans, and the first printing said thirty files and four hundred and fifty-three and neither reproduces under either method. A scan that does not print whether it sums over pairs or takes a union is a number nobody can check, and that was the unstated half of the error.**

**THE ONE FINDING THAT WAS HALF RIGHT AND IS NOW SETTLED IN WRITING INSTEAD OF ARGUED. The Batch 0004 card asked for the spelled board figure *in a different shape each time*, and the delivery used one orthography in all ten chapters and ten frames, and the writer and the reviewer read the same words two ways.** The rule is now written in `outline/volume-06.md` and in the Batch 0005 prompt: **the figure is spelled in words in all ten chapters and never in digits, it stands in TEN DISTINCT FRAMES, no frame is used twice, and no figure appears twice in the band. The orthography is NOT varied and is NOT to be varied, because a woman of forty-one who has kept one number in one hand for twenty-five years does not spell the same number three ways, and a jitter in the spelling reads as a fault in the prose and not as a figure.**

**AND WHAT DID NOT CHANGE, so that a later pass does not go looking for a change: the batch's goal, its midpoint, its climax, the four accounts that do not agree, the sentence that is not said, the woman of about sixty who pays for it, the eleven families told in a yard by a woman who cannot name a day, the man of thirty-four's sentence, the register, the bell, the corridor's absence, the volume's question, the ending, and every character's age, sex, name and function.** The board chain still reads 63,714 / 63,721 / 63,728 / 63,741 / 63,745 / 63,751 / 63,759 / 63,763 / 63,770 / 63,774, spelled in words, in that order, and the ten files still run 11,416 to 15,708 bytes.
